Attribution is by **thread index**, not a shared XOR sink — so "the server saw the request" and "the client got the reply" are separate claims about separate threads rather than one accumulated number. **v0 deviations:** two processes, one exchange, no preemption — the scheduler switches on blocking handoffs only, so time-slicing is still untested here; endpoints are boot-installed at handle 0 by constant, with no discovery; no capability transfer over the channel (`handle_count` is 0), so `MapDevice`/`DmaAlloc`, ports and the driver framework remain absent on this port; `DebugWrite` is used as a value sink rather than by its documented pointer/length ABI, as on AArch64; programs are hand-written blobs, not ELFs. | Preemptive scheduling; capability transfer and `MapDevice`/`DmaAlloc` here (the dispatcher already implements them — what is missing is the boot glue that grants a device); ports and `PortWait`; an ELF loader; per-thread `SUM`. |
| D102 | **Capability-gated MMIO and DMA from ring 3 on RISC-V 64 (M63).** A U-mode process is granted a Device capability, maps the virtio-mmio window into its own address space with `MapDevice`, reads the transport's identity registers, and gets a DMA page from `DmaAlloc` — the two privileged favours a driver needs, and the last mechanism between this port and a ring-3 driver. **The evidence that D101 landed the port on the shared substrate rather than beside it is that the syscall hook needed no new code at all:** `MapDevice` and `DmaAlloc` were already arms of `kcore::dispatch`, so this milestone is boot glue and one ring-3 program. Neither syscall tells the program anything it could have guessed — the physical window lives inside the capability and never crosses the ABI, and the DMA page's physical address is *returned* rather than requested; what the program chooses is only where in its own space each lands. **The DMA assertion is the load-bearing one, and it is deliberately not "a syscall returned a number".** The program writes a sentinel through its **virtual** address; the kernel then looks for it at the **physical** address the program was told to hand the device, reading through the direct map — a path the program never touched. Two disjoint names for one piece of memory is the entire content of `DmaAlloc`, so that is what gets checked. The MMIO identity is checked the same way: the program reads magic and device-id through its capability mapping, the kernel reads the same two registers through the direct map, and the two must agree. **Teardown is counted exactly, and here the exactness is the point.** `map_device` maps the register page **untracked**, so `AddressSpace::teardown` — which walks tracked mappings — must never see it; a device register block returned to the anonymous-memory pool would surface much later as memory that reads back as hardware. The expected count is derived from the layout rather than observed (four user addresses inside one gigabyte: root + 1 level-1 + 4 level-0 = 6 tables, plus the code, stack and DMA leaves = 3, total **9**) and was right on the first boot. A lower bound would not notice the device page being freed, exactly as D99's `>= 2` did not notice `free_tables` freeing the kernel's own tables. **Four negative checks, all biting:** the capability installed without `Rights::MAP` → `AccessDenied`; the program reading the window *before* mapping it → a contained load page fault, which is what makes the first check mean something (otherwise it would also pass if the address were mapped some other way); the sentinel written somewhere other than the DMA page → the kernel's direct-map read finds zeros; and a kernel-half `vaddr` → `InvalidMapping`. The first and last both present as "wrong report count", so the check now **prints the code the program reported** before failing — `-131080` and `-65542` are different failures and a reader needs to know which. **Discovery** reuses `DeviceTree::virtio_mmio_regions` and `VirtioMmioRegion` unchanged; unlike AArch64 it needs no before-the-table-switch caveat, since the direct map covers the blob for the kernel's whole life. Eight windows on `virt`, all carrying the virtio magic. A machine with none skips the check **with a stated verdict** rather than passing quietly. Note `VirtioMmioRegion::intid` parses a **GIC** interrupt property and therefore reads `None` here: that is "not parsed on this architecture", not "this device has no interrupt", and PLIC-shaped parsing is the interrupt milestone's work. **Verified:** 38/38 tests, lint/fmt clean, every D97-D101 verdict intact, exit 33, five boots byte-identical; x86-64, AArch64, ARM 32-bit and RISC-V 32 artifacts byte-identical. The substrate helpers lost their `ipc_` prefixes (`user_dispatch_hook`, `substrate_exec`, `end_user_thread`) now that a second check uses them. **v0 deviations:** the device is granted by **boot**, not by a device manager, so there is no binding-by-class and no revocation path exercised here; no interrupts — no `IrqComplete`, and the INTID is not parsed; no capability transfer over a channel, so a driver cannot yet *receive* a device; the ring-3 program reads identity registers rather than driving a transport, and the smoke boot attaches no disk, so every window reports device-id 0; and it is a hand-written blob rather than an ELF. | A PLIC-shaped INTID parse and ring-3 interrupt delivery; capability transfer over a channel, then a device manager binding by class (D91's shape); a real ring-3 virtio driver reusing the unchanged `tessera-virtio` core; an ELF loader. |
| D103 | **A device capability crosses a channel on RISC-V 64 (M64).** A manager process holding a device hands it to a driver process that starts with nothing but an endpoint; the driver is told which handle it arrived as, maps the device through it, and reads its identity registers. This is the last mechanism a driver framework needs: until now a device could only be granted by **boot**, which is a shared constant wearing a capability's clothes. Once more the syscall path needed no new code — `handles_ptr`/`handle_count` and the D94 installed-handle report are already `kcore::dispatch`'s work. **Three separate claims, and the third is what makes it a transfer rather than a copy.** (1) The driver starts with no device and ends up mapping one. (2) It learns the handle **from the kernel** — it could not have agreed on one in advance, because the handle did not exist until the receive installed it. (3) Afterwards the driver holds the capability **and the manager does not**, checked on both sides, because "the driver has it" alone would be equally true of a copy. Conservation is what makes one-driver-per-device arithmetic instead of policy. **The handle the driver is told is deliberately unguessable, and that took one line of setup worth explaining.** Boot installs a placeholder in the driver's slot 1 and takes it away again, bumping the slot's generation — so the value reported is `(1 << 16) \| 1` = **65537**, not `1`. A handle is an index *and* a generation, so this is the ordinary case rather than a contrived one: any table that has ever held a capability in a slot behaves this way, which is exactly why D94 had to add the report. It also makes the claim falsifiable, which "the handle was non-zero" was not: the negative check has the driver **guess** handle 1 and it fails the generation check in `lookup`. **Four negative checks, all biting:** the manager's capability installed without **`Rights::TRANSFER`** → `take` refuses with `AccessDenied` (`-131080`), the trap D91 paid for, here reported rather than hung; the driver guessing handle 1 instead of reading the report → check 52; the driver passing `installed_ptr = 0` and so learning nothing → its buffer stays zero and it names its own endpoint → check 52, which is what proves the *report* carries the number rather than the transfer alone; and the manager keeping a **second** handle to the same device → check 54, the conservation assertion firing. That last one is worth reading precisely: the kernel's rule is subtler than this test's expectation — a process that duplicated its capability and gave one copy away keeps the authority *and its register window* (D93, revocation follows the capability, not the route). Check 54 asserts what this protocol wants, not what the kernel forbids. **Verified:** 38/38 tests, lint/fmt clean, every D97-D102 verdict intact, exit 33, five boots byte-identical; x86-64, AArch64, ARM 32-bit and RISC-V 32 artifacts byte-identical. **v0 deviations:** the driver asks for *a* device rather than a **class** — there is no `driver_bind.isl` request on this port yet, so the manager has exactly one device and no matching to do; no revocation is exercised at runtime here (the manager never maps the window before giving it away, so there is nothing to revoke); no restart or re-bind; still hand-written blobs rather than ELFs; and the endpoint handles remain boot-installed constants — what stopped being a constant is the **device** handle, which is the one that matters. | Binding by class (D91's `driver_bind.isl` shape) with a manager that enumerates; a runtime revocation proof (map, transfer, then fault on the old window); a PLIC-shaped INTID parse and ring-3 interrupt delivery; a real ring-3 virtio driver. |
| D104 | **Ring-3 interrupt delivery on RISC-V 64 (M65).** A ring-3 driver maps its device by capability, arms it, **parks** on a port until the device interrupts, acknowledges the device through its own window, and hands the line back with `IrqComplete`. It does this **twice**, and the second round is the design: one interrupt proves delivery while saying nothing about whether the masked line was ever restored. With this the port has the last mechanism AArch64 had and it did not — from here the difference is programs. **The device-tree parse stopped being one machine's.** `interrupts` means whatever its controller's `#interrupt-cells` says, and the two machines disagree: a RISC-V PLIC uses **one cell** that *is* the line, a GIC uses three (`<type number flags>`, SPI + 32). `gic_spi_intid` became `interrupt_line`, discriminating by property length — which is what the tree offers without resolving `interrupt-parent` phandles, and is documented as the shortcut it is. It cannot fail silently: a device wired to the wrong line simply never interrupts, which is what the boot check asserts. `VirtioMmioRegion` became **`MmioDevice`** (nothing about it was ever virtio-specific) and gained a generic `first_mmio_device(compatible)`, because the RISC-V port needed the same three facts about a clock. Two host tests cover the single-cell line and the generic lookup. **The device choice was a capability decision, not a convenience one.** The kernel owns the UART and drives the timer itself, so either would have put two owners on one device — the thing the model forbids — and the virtio slots on this machine have no backend and never interrupt. The **goldfish RTC** is the one interrupt source nothing else here claims. **Two bugs found in the check itself, both the same shape: a failure that hangs instead of reporting.** The pump that waits for the line while the driver is parked was bounded at 1,000,000 iterations — but `wfi` returns on a *tick*, and `timer_check` had stopped the tick, so with no heartbeat the loop slept forever and the bound was never evaluated. The negative checks **timed out instead of failing**, which is precisely what the bound existed to prevent. Fixed by running the periodic tick across the pump — load-bearing, not scenery, and also what a real system looks like — and by making the limit a count of ticks (200 ≈ 2 s). Separately, one negative check was patched in without asserting the patch applied, so it "passed" against an unmodified binary; every negative check now asserts its own injection. **Negative checks — three bite, one does not, and the one that does not is the interesting entry.** The driver skipping `IrqComplete` → only one wake, `irq: only one wake — the line was masked on delivery and never came back`, exit 65: the decisive proof of the re-arm. `IrqComplete` named with the **port** handle instead of the device → `AccessDenied`, so the INTID comes from the resource graph and not the caller. The port never bound to the line → no wake at all. But **removing the bridge's mask-on-deliver changed nothing**: this device's line does not re-assert after the PLIC claim is completed, so it never storms. The mask stays because a level-triggered source that holds its line *would* storm, and it is recorded as **unverified in-repo** — the one interrupt source this machine offers that nothing else owns cannot exercise it (the D99 ASID precedent for how this is reported). **Verified:** 38/38 tests, lint/fmt clean, every D97-D103 verdict intact, exit 33, five boots byte-identical. x86-64 byte-identical; AArch64, ARM 32-bit and RISC-V 32 binaries differ only through the shared device-tree crate and their **boot output is unchanged** (AArch64 modulo its perf line, which is a measured timing). **v0 deviations:** one device, one line, one driver — no sharing, no priority, and the PLIC threshold is left at zero; the driver polls nothing but also serves nothing, so this is interrupt *delivery* rather than a driver; interrupts are unmasked only across the pump's `wfi`, which is the single-core discipline that keeps the bridge out of the executive and is not an SMP answer; and `IrqComplete` stays port-local because re-arming is an interrupt-controller write. | A real ring-3 virtio driver reusing the unchanged `tessera-virtio` core, which needs a disk on this machine; a device manager binding by class; the PLIC threshold and priorities once more than one line matters; SMP, where the unmask window stops being a sufficient discipline. |
| D105 | **A compiled ring-3 driver reads a disk on RISC-V 64 (M66).** `userspace/blk-driver` is a real `no_std` Rust program — an ET_EXEC ELF the kernel's loader parses and maps, the **first `Machine::RiscV64` load** — that maps a virtio-blk transport by capability, allocates four DMA pages, submits a read of sector 0, **sleeps on its port until the device wakes it**, and reports the eight bytes back. It reuses `tessera-virtio` **unchanged**: the same transport core the AArch64 driver uses and the same one the in-kernel proof used before either existed, now driven from ring 3 on a third architecture without a line of change. **What the program does not contain is the point.** No privileged instruction, no MMIO address, no interrupt number, no physical address it computed. The register window comes from `MapDevice`, the device-visible addresses come from `DmaAlloc`, and the line is never named at all — it waits on a port and the kernel decides what wakes it. Its whole bootstrap contract is two handles: the device it drives and the port its interrupt arrives on. Every previous milestone on this port ran a hand-written blob, which proves a mechanism but not that the mechanisms compose into something a person would write; this is that. **New shared machinery, all small:** `elf::Machine::RiscV64` (0xf3); a `riscv64` entry in `tessera_user_binary` so a user ELF can be built for this architecture at all; and a `load_user_elf` that maps each segment writable, fills it, then narrows it to what the segment declared — so a page is never both writable and executable, not even during loading, and a segment asking for both is refused outright. **The bug it cost, and the reason a staged failure code earns its keep:** the first run reported `0xdead_0000_0062_0002` — stage 0x62 (transport init), cause 2 (`BadVersion`). QEMU's virtio-mmio defaults to the **legacy** transport, which this driver deliberately does not speak; `-global virtio-mmio.force-legacy=false` selects the modern one, exactly as the AArch64 test has always passed. A driver that reported only "it failed" would have cost an afternoon; one that reports which stage failed and why cost one grep. **Verification:** a new `virtio_blk_boot_riscv64_test` boots with the deterministic disk attached and requires both exit 33 and the driver's verdict. The plain smoke boot has no disk and prints `blk: skipped — no virtio block device attached`, so the skip is stated rather than silent. 39/39 tests, lint/fmt clean, five disk-attached boots byte-identical. `kcore::elf` changed, so every kernel's binary does; x86-64, AArch64, ARM 32-bit and RISC-V 32 **boot output is unchanged** (AArch64 modulo its perf line, a measured timing). The unsafe gate did its job unprompted: the new package was refused until it had an inventory entry and a `SAFETY:` comment on the entry point. **Three negative checks, all biting, each naming its own stage:** the driver granted the device but **not the port** → `0x64/7`, it cannot wait on what it was not given; the device capability without **`Rights::MAP`** → `0x60/8`, it cannot map what it may not map; and the loader's narrowing step removed → the text segment stays non-executable and the program faults on entry, so the per-segment protection is load-bearing rather than decorative. **v0 deviations:** one device, one request, one sector — no queue depth, no multiple outstanding requests, no write path; the driver exits after one read rather than serving anyone, so this is a driver *program* and not yet a driver *service* (no channel protocol, no clients, no restart); the disk is attached by the test rather than discovered through a device manager binding by class; and the program is granted its capabilities by boot rather than receiving them over a channel — which D103 proved this port can do and this milestone does not yet use. | ~~The driver as a **service**: a block protocol over a channel, clients, and a device manager binding by class (D91's shape)~~ (done, D111); ~~write~~ (done, D129) and multi-sector requests; receiving the device over a channel instead of from boot, composing D103 with this. |
| D106 | **RISC-V 32 empties its user half (M67).** The prerequisite for ring 3 on the fourth port, and it turned out to be one constant rather than a relocation. Sv32 has a single `satp`, so a process's tables must carry the kernel — which they can only do without colliding if the kernel is somewhere no user program can be. On this machine **RAM begins at exactly 2 GiB**, which is what `USER_ADDRESS_MAX` already was (D89 chose it for that reason), so the kernel image and the direct map were *already* above the boundary and the direct map's offset is zero. Where the 64-bit port needed a `KERNEL_VIRT_BASE`, an assembly boot stub and a pre-translation page-table build (D97), this port needed none of it. **What was actually in the user half was the devices.** The platform's registers live below RAM, and `DEVICE_RANGE` was a flat `(0, 0x8000_0000)` — the entire user half, identity-mapped. Both halves of that had to change: the range is now the machine's real device span `(0, 0x1100_0000)`, and it is mapped at `DEVICE_WINDOW_BASE = 0xc000_0000 + phys`. Narrowing was not tidying but necessary — 2 GiB placed at a kernel-half base runs off the end of a 32-bit address space. **The console is two things at two moments, and the port now says so.** Before the switch there is no translation and the only address that works is the physical one; after it, that address is in the user half and deliberately unmapped. So there are two `Ns16550a` statics and a second `init_global` at the switch — which is cleaner than a mutable base, because the console already owns a `&mut` to the first and a second one would alias it. The count of writes dropped across the switch is reported rather than assumed to be zero. The PLIC and the test finisher need no such care: they name themselves physically and go through `set_device_access_base`, the window D97 added to the crate both RISC-V ports share — used here for the first time by the port it was not written for. **Asserted, not announced:** each probe address in the device range must now translate to *nothing* at its physical address and *something* through the window — both directions, because a device that had merely become unreachable would pass a one-sided check as well as one that moved. **Two negative checks, and the second is better than it was designed to be:** leaving the identity mapping in place as well → `paging: FATAL: the device range is still in the user half`, exit 65. A window base that would run off the end of the address space (`0xff00_0000`) → **it does not compile**: `error[E0080]: attempt to compute 4278190080_usize + 268435456_usize, which would overflow`, because on a 32-bit target the console's window address is a `const` in `usize`. The runtime `checked_add` guard in `build_kernel_space` remains for a window computed from a memory map rather than written down, but the written-down case is caught by the compiler. **Verified:** 39/39 tests, lint/fmt clean, 9/9 conformance, exit 33, five boots byte-identical; x86-64, AArch64, ARM 32-bit and RISC-V 64 artifacts byte-identical (only this port's two crates changed). **Still to come on this port:** this is the split, not ring 3. U-mode itself (`UserContextOps`, the `sscratch` stack swap, `sstatus.SUM`), per-process roots, and the kcore substrate follow the sequence the 64-bit port took in D98-D101 — most of which should transfer, with Sv32's two levels and 4 MiB superpages the main difference. | U-mode on this port; per-process Sv32 roots (the user half is now free for them); then the kcore substrate and IPC, as D98-D101. |
| D107 | **Ring 3 on RISC-V 32 — the fourth architecture with a privilege boundary (M68).** The port enters U-mode, serves a syscall, and contains a user fault instead of dying of it. `UserContextOps::init_user`, the user trampoline, the trap vector's `sscratch` swap and `sstatus.SUM` all arrive together, and D106's empty user half is what makes the last of the three checks mean anything. **Almost all of it transferred from D98, and where it did not is instructive.** The mechanism is identical at half the register width: `sw`/`lw` instead of `sd`/`ld`, a frame of 84 bytes reserved as 96 (the ABI wants 16-byte alignment at both word sizes), and `sstatus.SPP` at bit 8 and `SUM` at bit 18 — `sstatus` is narrower here but neither bit is one that moved. `satp` is the real difference: Sv32 puts `MODE` in a single bit 31 rather than a four-bit field, and its `PPN` is 22 bits against Sv39's 44, so `prepare_resume` shifts a 34-bit physical root into a 22-bit field — safe only because `build_kernel_space` already refused any space whose root sat above the 32-bit window (D89). **The `sscratch` invariant holds here for the same structural reason** — the value is *derived* at every exit to U-mode from the stack pointer the exiting code is standing on, so it is not state the context switch must carry (D98's finding, now confirmed at a second word size rather than assumed to generalise). `SUM` likewise cannot be scoped per copy, for D101's reason: a copying syscall may block and hand off inside the window. **Proven at boot** (`umode:` verdicts, D58-gated, in dependency order): entering U-mode and returning — two syscalls, a value round-tripped through the kernel *and* the user stack with a clobber in between, so neither a stale register nor an unmapped stack can pass; **W^X at the unprivileged level** — a store into the read-execute code page takes a store page fault, the address checked against the page; and **the privilege boundary** — a user load of `USER_ADDRESS_MAX` itself takes a load page fault, which is the exact address D106 emptied down to. The program is position-independent, reading its own address with `auipc`. **Three negative checks, all behaving exactly as they did on the 64-bit port:** `.user()` dropped from the code mapping → the program cannot be fetched → check 6; dropped from the **stack** mapping → same, which only bites because the program was written to use its stack; and the handler's `sepc += 4` removed → the `ecall` re-executes forever and the boot **hangs rather than failing**, recorded as what it is. That the failures match D98's one for one is itself worth something: it is evidence the two ports share a mechanism rather than two implementations that happen to agree. **Verified:** 39/39 tests, lint/fmt clean, 9/9 conformance, exit 33, five boots byte-identical; x86-64, AArch64, ARM 32-bit and RISC-V 64 artifacts byte-identical. **Four of five architectures now have a privilege boundary**; ARM 32-bit is the last without one. **v0 deviations:** U-mode runs in the **kernel's own address space**, with the user pages in the (now empty) user half and every kernel page lacking `U` — a real boundary in one table, but no per-process root yet, so `prepare_resume`'s `satp` write is implemented and unexercised; a hand-written blob rather than an ELF; a two-call ABI in the boot glue rather than kcore's syscall substrate; cooperative, with a fault or exit abandoning the thread mid-trap; and no FP state. | Per-process Sv32 roots over the empty user half; then the kcore substrate and channel IPC, following D99-D101; and ring 3 on ARM 32-bit, the last port without it. |
| D108 | **Per-process Sv32 roots on RISC-V 32 (M69).** Two processes, each with its own translation root, running the same program: the same virtual address reads different memory in each, and neither can reach the kernel that is mapped in both. D106 emptied the user half for exactly this, and it makes `prepare_resume`'s `satp` write — implemented and unexercised in D107 — the thing that actually switches a process. **`new_user` copies the kernel's root entries by value**, the Sv32 twin of D99: one `satp`, so "the kernel is mapped in every address space" is not a policy a kernel could choose against. Sv32's shape changes the arithmetic without changing the argument — two levels rather than three, a 1024-entry root whose every entry covers 4 MiB, and the boundary at slot 512. **ASIDs are 9 bits here against Sv39's 16**, a smaller pool for the same job, so `new_user` refuses one that does not fit rather than truncating it into a collision. **Everything about this milestone matched the 64-bit port's, including both of its lessons arriving unchanged.** Teardown is counted **exactly** (root + one leaf table per distinct 4 MiB region: A maps four, B three, so 5 + 4 = 9, right on the first boot) because a lower bound cannot see a teardown that also freed the shared kernel tables — and the negative check that breaks `free_tables` fails on check 17, exactly as D99's did. The code frame is deliberately **shared** between the two processes, so the isolation on show is a property of the tables and not of the memory happening to differ. And the private-page check is made in **both** directions, because "B faults" alone would also hold for an address neither maps. **Three negative checks, and the third is the same honest non-result as D99's:** `new_user` not copying the kernel half → the kernel vanishes at `activate` and the boot **hangs**, because there is nothing left to report with; `free_tables` walking the whole root → check 17; and removing the leading `sfence.vma` for an ASID-owning space → **still boots clean**, because QEMU's TCG flushes the whole TLB on every `satp` write. ASID-scoped flushing is therefore implemented and **unverified in-repo on this port too** — the fence is there because the architecture requires it, not because anything here would notice its absence. **Verified:** 39/39 tests, lint/fmt clean, 9/9 conformance, every D106-D107 verdict intact, exit 33, five boots byte-identical; x86-64, AArch64, ARM 32-bit and RISC-V 64 artifacts byte-identical. **v0 deviations:** the spaces are built and switched by the **boot glue**, not by kcore — no `Process`/`Thread`/`Scheduler` on this port yet, so a "process" here is a root plus a hand-run user thread; ASID allocation is two constants with no recycling or wrap handling; `prepare_resume` still cannot carry an ASID, since the trait hands it only a `PhysAddr`, so a resume through it flushes more than it needs to; and the kernel space keeps its user-half mappings from D107. | kcore `Process`/`Thread`/`Scheduler` on this port, then the Executive and channel IPC — D100 and D101's sequence; an ASID allocator with recycling; hardware validation of the ASID-scoped fence, which no emulator here distinguishes. |
| D109 | **Ring 3 on ARM 32-bit — the architecture matrix now has a privilege boundary everywhere (M70).** The fifth and last port enters User mode, serves an `svc`, and contains a user abort instead of dying of it. All five families can now run unprivileged code. **This is the port where the porting layer earned its keep, because almost nothing transferred.** ARMv7-A **banks `SP` and `LR` per processor mode**, which does two thirds of the job for free and one third not at all. For free: entering SVC mode gives the kernel its own stack and leaves the user's untouched, so there is no swap-through-a-scratch-register dance — `SP_svc` *is* the current thread's kernel stack, because the context switch left it there. Not at all: the user's banked pair is unreachable by ordinary means, so the `svc` vector saves and restores it with the **`^` form** of `stm`/`ldm`, which forbids writeback (hence a separate address register) and must not be followed by an instruction touching a banked register (hence the `nop` — an architectural hazard, not decoration). **Two more differences the other four ports do not have.** `LR` on an `svc` already points *after* the instruction, so the syscall handler advances nothing — the exact opposite of RISC-V, where forgetting to advance `sepc` livelocks; the negative check here is to *rewind* `pc` onto the `svc`, which produces the same hang from the mirror-image mistake. And an abort arrives in **abort mode on the abort stack**, not on the faulting thread's kernel stack, so the vector switches to SVC mode (`cps #0x13`) before calling the handler: containing a fault means switching context, and doing that from abort mode would leave the CPU in it, so the next abort would overwrite the frame this one was standing on. **The boundary is `AP`, not the address, and that changes what the third check must probe.** RAM begins at 1 GiB here — *below* `USER_ADDRESS_MAX` — so unlike RISC-V 32 (D106) the kernel and the user program share the low half, exactly as AArch64's first EL0 milestone did (D70). Kernel pages are `AP_RW_PL1` and user pages `AP_RW_ALL`. So the privilege check reads a **mapped** kernel address and requires a *permission* fault; the check refuses to run at all if that address is not mapped, because an absent one would fault for the wrong reason and prove nothing. The paging layer needed no change: `leaf_attributes` has encoded user pages correctly since D90, including the `PXN` that keeps privileged code out of them — bit 54 being plain `XN` here rather than AArch64's `UXN` is why that was already right. **Proven at boot** (`umode:` verdicts, D58-gated): entering User mode and returning — two `svc`s, a value round-tripped through the kernel *and* the user stack with a clobber in between, so neither a stale register nor an unmapped stack can pass; **W^X at the unprivileged level** — a store into the read-execute code page takes a data abort inside that page; and **the privilege boundary** — a user load of mapped kernel memory takes a data abort. The program is position-independent, computing its own address with `adr`. **Three negative checks, all biting:** `.user()` dropped from the code mapping → the program cannot be fetched; dropped from the **stack** mapping → the stack round trip faults, which only bites because the program was written to use its stack; and the syscall hook rewinding `pc` → livelock. **Verified:** 39/39 tests, lint/fmt clean, 9/9 conformance, exit 33, five boots byte-identical; x86-64, AArch64, RISC-V 64 and RISC-V 32 artifacts byte-identical. **v0 deviations:** User mode runs in the kernel's own `TTBR0` with the program mapped alongside it — a real boundary in one table, but **no per-process isolation** and no `TTBR0`/`TTBR1` split, so `prepare_resume` is still the trait's no-op here; a hand-written blob rather than an ELF; a two-call ABI in the boot glue rather than kcore's syscall substrate; cooperative, with a fault or exit abandoning the thread mid-trap; and no FP/SIMD state. | The `TTBCR` split and per-process `TTBR0` roots, which this port needs before kcore processes; then the kcore substrate and IPC, as D100-D101; an ELF loader. |
| D110 | **ARM 32-bit gets the `TTBCR` split and per-process `TTBR0` roots (M71).** The kernel moves into the high half and is walked out of `TTBR1`; each process gets its own `TTBR0`. Two processes run the same program at the same addresses and read different memory. This was planned as two milestones and **landed as one, because on this port they are not separable**: the moment the kernel's space becomes `TTBR1`-only it can no longer hold a low address at all, so D109's user checks stop working until there is a process space to put them in. Discovering that is the milestone's first finding. **The split had to be a relocation.** `TTBCR.T0SZ = 0` gave `TTBR0` the whole 32-bit space, and LPAE can only put `TTBR1` at the *top* in a power-of-two region — while the kernel was linked at 0x4001_0000, because RAM begins at 1 GiB. There is no split that leaves it there. So the image now links at `KERNEL_VIRT_BASE + phys` with `KERNEL_VIRT_BASE = 0x8000_0000`, `T0SZ = T1SZ = 1` splits the space 2 GiB / 2 GiB, and the entry stub fills **two** boot roots (identity in `TTBR0` so it can keep running, high in `TTBR1` where the kernel is linked), enables the MMU and jumps high. It stays position-independent by loading each symbol PC-relative and subtracting the known `KERNEL_VIRT_BASE` — no relocation machinery for a constant offset. `USER_ADDRESS_MAX` stops being the policy number D90 apologised for and becomes a hardware boundary. **The bug that mattered, and it was not in the stub.** With a 2 GiB region a level-1 index is **one bit**, so `0x0900_0000` and `0x8900_0000` reach the same root entry — and a `TTBR1` space asked to translate a device's *physical* address cheerfully described the direct-map alias instead. The split's own assertion caught it. A space now carries the base of the region its root is walked for and refuses an address outside it, which makes `translate` exact and `map` refuse to put a low address in the high half. The negative check removes that guard and the assertion fails; another maps the user image into the kernel's space and is refused at map time. **Per-process roots are much smaller here than on the RISC-V ports, structurally.** Two translation-base registers mean a process root holds **only** the user half: no kernel entries copied by value, nothing shared by pointer, and no exact-frame-count teardown guard needed against over-freeing (D99, D108). `free_tables` can free everything the root reaches, because everything it reaches belongs to the process. **Supporting changes.** `leaf_attributes` now emits **`nG`** — it had ignored `PageFlags::global()` entirely, so every descriptor was global, which is harmless with one address space and would have silently defeated ASIDs with several; added and verified alone first, with boot output unchanged. `karch-arm-common` gains the device access window D97 gave its RISC-V counterpart, because the GIC names itself physically and ARM 32-bit no longer has an identity map to reach it through; AArch64 leaves the window at zero. **Negative checks: two bite, one does not, and the one that does not is this port's own doing.** Removing the region guard → the split assertion fails; mapping a user address into the kernel space → refused. But giving both processes the **same ASID** changes nothing, because `activate` invalidates the *entire* TLB on every `TTBR0` change — so ASIDs currently buy nothing here and are recorded as unverified. Unlike the RISC-V ports, where the emulator hid it, this one is a line of this kernel's code and is fixable: `tlbiasid` in place of the unconditional invalidate. **Verified:** 39/39 tests, lint/fmt clean, 9/9 conformance, all D109 `umode:` verdicts intact, exit 33, five boots byte-identical. x86-64, RISC-V 64 and RISC-V 32 artifacts byte-identical; AArch64's image grows by one page through the shared crate and **every verdict is identical** (only its memmap extents and perf timings move). **v0 deviations:** the spaces are built and switched by the boot glue, not kcore — no `Process`/`Thread`/`Scheduler` on this port yet; ASIDs are constants and buy nothing until the TLB maintenance is narrowed; `prepare_resume` is still the trait's no-op, so nothing but the boot glue installs a root; and the stub's boot roots are never reclaimed (two pages, inside `.bss`). | Narrow `activate`'s TLB maintenance to `tlbiasid` so ASIDs mean something; the kcore substrate and channel IPC, as D100-D101; an ELF loader. |
| D111 | **The driver framework runs on RISC-V 64 (M72).** A device manager enumerates the machine by probing, a driver binds a `Block` device **by class**, drives it, exits, is torn down, and a second driver binds the same physical transport through the same protocol. The framework's claim was that it is common; this is the first evidence for it that is not prose. **Not one line of mechanism changed.** `kcore::dispatch`, `kcore::exec` and `kcore::process` — the resource graph, the transfer, the window revocation, `reclaim_devices` — were already architecture-neutral and were not touched. What moved was two *programs* and their wiring. **`userspace/uabi`, and what it says about portability.** Five of six user programs had grown identical copies of the syscall stub, the volatile kernel-filled-buffer read and the staged-failure encoding; a sixth and seventh copy was the alternative to extracting them. Exactly **two** things in a user program are genuinely per-architecture, and the crate holds both: the syscall instruction with its register convention, and the *addresses* a program may map things at — AArch64's probe window at 0x1000_0080_0000 is not merely unfashionable under Sv39, it is above `USER_ADDRESS_MAX` and unmappable. Everything else — the bind protocol, the class matching, the enumeration-by-probing — was already portable Rust and moved unchanged. `device-manager` and `blk-probe` are now **one source each, built for two targets**. **The bug, and it is one only a single-root architecture can have.** After `scheduler().run()` returns, `satp` still holds the *last thread's* root — and on RISC-V that root is also what maps the kernel, because `new_user` copied the kernel's entries into it. Tearing that process down frees the tables the running kernel is walking: the next `.rodata` load faults, the trap vector faults pushing its own frame, and the machine spirals **silently**, because the fault handler cannot reach the console. `qemu -d int` showed it in one line — a store fault at `trap_vector` with `tval` marching down 176 bytes a frame. The fix is one statement: return to the kernel's own root **before** touching a process's tables. AArch64 cannot have this bug at all, because its kernel is in `TTBR1` and tearing down a `TTBR0` cannot reach it. **Negative checks, all three biting, each re-proving a trap on a second port:** the manager's capability without **`Rights::TRANSFER`** → the reply's `take` is refused and the driver reports `AccessDenied` (D91's first trap); the manager using plain `ChannelReply` instead of `ChannelReplyContinue` → the *second* bind never happens, and — worth noting — it fails an **assertion** rather than hanging, because D101 made this port count exits (this trap has now bitten four times: D85, D91, D101, and here as a check); and making the manager misclassify block devices → the driver's `Block` bind is refused and it stops rather than falling back to whatever handle it was handed, which is what proves binding is load-bearing. **Verified:** 39/39 tests, lint/fmt clean, every D97–D105 verdict intact, exit 33 with the disk attached, five boots byte-identical, and `driver-rebind: skipped` stated rather than silent on the diskless smoke boot. The `uabi` extraction was done and verified **first and alone** — all five programs converted, then every AArch64 verdict diffed identical — before anything was ported. x86-64, ARM 32-bit and RISC-V 32 artifacts byte-identical. **v0 deviations:** one device and one class, so the manager has nothing to choose between; no resident host, no clients and no select loop (that is `device-host`, which needs a net device too); no restart *policy* — the boot glue is the supervisor, and it restarts once because the check says so; and the framework is still absent from x86-64, RISC-V 32 and ARM 32-bit, the last two because they have no kcore substrate yet. | The resident `device-host` here, with clients and interrupt-driven serving; a restart policy and a ring-3 supervisor holding that authority; the framework on the remaining ports as each gains the substrate. |
| D112 | **The driver framework gets its structured events (M73).** `docs/drivers/01` says lifecycle transitions "are observable through structured events" and `docs/lifecycle/04` makes it a code rule; the framework (D91–D96, D111) emitted **none**. `kernel_event.isl` had reserved `Component::DRIVER` since D57 and nothing had ever used it. Nine `EventKind`s are appended (9–17) and emitted from the four places the kernel actually mediates a device capability — `map_device` (the grant, and **every** refusal arm), `dma_alloc`, `revoke_device_windows_unless_held`, `reclaim_devices` — plus the x86-64 crash-recovery ladder. **What the kernel may name, and what it may not.** There is deliberately no `DRIVER_BOUND`: binding is a user↔user protocol (`driver_bind.isl`) the kernel transports opaquely, and D96 went out of its way to make the return discriminator *structural* (a capability arriving **is** the return) precisely so the kernel would never need to parse it. A kind named for a bind would put back the protocol knowledge the framework exists to keep out. The kernel's vocabulary is therefore mechanics — window granted, window revoked and by which of the two routes, mapping refused, DMA granted, device reclaimed, device lost — and the ladder above it (Discovered → Matched → Probing → Active) stays with the ring-3 manager, deferred below. For the same reason no emit goes on the generic capability-transfer path: it runs for every message with handles, the kernel cannot tell there whether the object is a device, and B31 is unmet (every emission is a live call). Setup-time paths only. **A silent-fallback violation, fixed.** `Executive::reclaim_devices` dropped a device capability with no record when a message had no handle room or the destination queue was full — `docs/lifecycle/04` requires the structured event that says so. Both arms now emit `DEVICE_RECLAIM_LOST`, and the "impossible" unmap failure in `revoke_device_windows_unless_held`, previously a swallowed `let _ =`, rides in the revocation record's `arg3`. **Two defects the new checks found immediately, both the same shape.** The ladder records carried **no causal id**: `run()` returns through `yield_to_boot`, which leaves the ambient context on boot's, so nothing joined a restart to the crash that provoked it — the fault handler now hands the dying host's cause to the supervisor, and the boot's stamped-record count moves 86 → 99. `reclaim_devices` had the same hole for a harder reason — the thread whose cause it would inherit is *dead* — so it mints an origin, which is what D59 calls the case where something outside the running trace becomes work. **What the checks assert, and why counting is not enough.** `kcore::event::summarize_device_events` is pure and host-tested, shared by both framework ports so "what counts as working" has one definition. It pairs every revocation against an outstanding `(device, VA)` grant, because **counting revocations proves nothing**: a kernel reporting one on every capability departure — including the ones D93's `holds` guard correctly declines to act on — produces a perfectly plausible count. It also asks whether **the named device** was granted twice, not whether *some* device was: the first version asked the weaker question and passed a boot where an unrelated device answered it. **Negative checks: three run, two bit immediately, one exposed the check instead of the kernel.** Deleting the grant emit → `mapped=0`, exit 65. Removing the clock → all 16 records unstamped, exit 65. But emitting the revocation *ahead* of the `holds` guard — a false report of a revocation that never happened — **passed**, because the reading only counted. The grant-pairing clause above is the fix, it is pinned by a host test naming this finding, and the injection then failed with `unmatched_revokes=3`. **Verified:** 39/39 tests (230 kcore unit tests, 11 new), lint/fmt clean, `//tools/checks:all` green, no new `unsafe`. RISC-V 64 five boots byte-identical; AArch64 five boots identical except its perf timing line (pre-existing). x86-64 moves in exactly two verdicts — the new `driver-ladder:` line and `correlation:` 86 → 99 — with everything else identical bar its KASLR base and scheduler timings, which vary run-to-run on an unchanged binary. ARM 32-bit and RISC-V 32 untouched. **v0 deviations:** the ring-3 lifecycle ladder is **not** built — the device manager has no way to emit at all, which needs a ring-3 emit path (a syscall, and a decision about which `EventKind`s a user program may name without being able to forge a `PAGER_*` record) and the Stage-1 log service to harvest into; nothing harvests, so the ports drain into a boot check and the records die with the boot; B31 still unmet, so these emissions are not free; and a second no-silent-fallback hole is **recorded but not fixed** — `dispatch::install_transferred_handles` drops *any* transferred capability when the receiver's handle table is full, on a path where the kernel cannot tell the object is a device. | ~~A ring-3 emit path so the manager can report Discovered/Matched/Probing/Active~~ (done, D128: the `DriverLifecycle` syscall, and the kernel refuses a transition that contradicts the history it holds); the log service that harvests these rings; the `install_transferred_handles` drop; compile-time tracepoint gating for B31. |
| D113 | **Rights narrow on transfer, and a device grant can be non-delegable (M74).** `docs/kernel/01` says rights "can be reduced when handles are duplicated **or transferred**"; duplication reduced and transfer did not — `HandleTable::take` returned the entry's rights unchanged. **The defect was a composition, not an omission.** Moving a handle *requires* `Rights::TRANSFER`, and transferred rights passed through untouched, so every capability that could be transferred at all necessarily arrived **able to be transferred onward**. Duplicate-then-transfer could not work around it: the duplicate had to keep `TRANSFER` to be sendable. There was no way to hand a process a capability it could not hand on — the containment gap D91 recorded as a symptom ("the driver inherits TRANSFER too") without naming the cause. **The wire change.** `channel_msg.isl` gains a `HandleTransfer` descriptor (handle, reserved, rights) and `handles_ptr` addresses a vector of those instead of bare `uint32` handle values — which also retires a hand-rolled serialization the guidelines forbid. It is deliberately **not** `@abi`: that convention's size/version/flags envelope belongs to a message, and this is an element of a vector the containing `ChannelMsgArgs` already versions. The rights word is **64 bits** because the rights catalog is — `DERIVE` and `REVOKE` sit at bits 32 and 33, and a 32-bit field would have dropped them silently. `reserved` must be zero and is refused otherwise, so the padding stays available for a later field instead of becoming whatever senders left in it. **The version bump is load-bearing, and that is the subtle part.** `ChannelMsgArgs` keeps its **88 bytes**; only what `handles_ptr` points at changes. A producer left at version 2 would hand the kernel bare handle values to be read as 16-byte descriptors — a handle number landing where a rights mask is expected. Since the kernel hand-decodes that vector rather than passing it through the ISL decoder, `version` is the *only* thing that can tell v2 from v3, so the gate refuses rather than reinterprets. **`take_narrowed` enforces the subset rule** with the same error `duplicate` and `replace_rights` use, still requires `TRANSFER` on the source (narrowing is not a way to acquire the authority to send), and leaves the handle in place on refusal. **The tension this created, and how it resolved.** Making the device manager grant `READ\|MAP` without `TRANSFER` broke reclaim-and-rebind, and the reason is structural: reclaim returned the *dead driver's* capability, which now carried the driver's narrowed rights, so the manager could not grant the device to a second driver — verified as `AccessDenied` on the manager's reply. The fix is that the **device graph node**, not the corpse's handle, is the authority a hand-out carries: `DeviceNode` records the rights the device is administered with, and `reclaim_devices` returns the capability with those. The node is the root a grant is narrowed *from*, and it outlives every grant, which is exactly why it is the right source; recovering from the corpse would recover only what the corpse held. **Producers: 14, not the 9 the plan predicted.** Nine x86-64 asm descriptors, three RISC-V 64 blob descriptors, two AArch64 machine-code words (`movk x10, #0x3, lsl #32`, encoding verified against a neighbouring instruction whose bytes were already known good), and four user programs. The five beyond the estimate were found by **booting**, one refusal at a time — each failed closed with `Protocol` at first use, which is the failure direction the version gate was chosen for. **Negative checks, all three biting:** a stale v2 producer → `Protocol` (10), refused rather than misread; a grant asking for `WRITE` the manager does not hold → `AccessDenied` (8), no widening; and — the one that states the defect as a test — a driver patched to hand its bound device to a third party → `AccessDenied` (8) rather than the `0xffff` the injection reports on success, which is what would have happened before this change. **Verified:** 39/39 tests (238 kcore unit tests, 8 new), lint/fmt clean, no new `unsafe`. RISC-V 64 five boots **byte-identical to the pre-change boot** — narrowing changed no verdict; AArch64 five boots identical bar its perf timing line; x86-64's whole boot identical apart from KASLR and scheduler timings, which vary run-to-run on an unchanged binary. ARM 32-bit and RISC-V 32 untouched. **v0 deviations:** narrowing is per-transfer and there is still no way to *record* a narrowing policy — a manager decides in code what to grant, and nothing checks it against a manifest; the device node's authority is set at registration by boot glue, so "who may administer this device" is still a boot constant rather than policy; rights do not narrow on the `installed_ptr` report path (there is nothing to narrow — it reports numbers); and a zero-rights transfer is permitted, documented as a valid if useless narrowing rather than special-cased. | Narrowing policy as data rather than a constant in the manager — the manifest now exists (D130) and does not yet carry granted rights, so what remains is a field and a manager that reads it rather than a whole mechanism; rights narrowing on `duplicate`-into-a-message when a second sender appears; the `install_transferred_handles` silent drop recorded in D112. |
| D114 | **PCIe enumeration over ECAM — the kernel finds a device nothing told it about (M75).** Every device the system had ever bound was a virtio-mmio transport the device tree named directly; `grep -rn pci --include=*.rs kernel/` returned two comments saying the ECAM window was deliberately not mapped. Now the kernel reads the host bridge out of the device tree, walks config space, and places the BARs of what it finds. On `qemu-system-riscv64 -M virt` with `-device virtio-blk-pci` it reports **2 functions — the host bridge and `1af4:1001` at `00:01.0`, class `0x010000`, given a `0x1000` BAR at `0x4000_0000`.** Nothing in the device tree mentions that endpoint. **Why the kernel walks, stated as a deviation rather than a preference.** D91's rule is that enumeration needs access, which is why the device manager is a program and not a table. PCI is where that cannot hold: config space is **not per-device**, so a capability to it would be authority over every function behind the bridge at once, and `MapDevice` grants a single page against a window of 256 MiB. Handing a program that authority to preserve the rule would trade a small impurity for a large one. What the kernel produces is what `docs/architecture/02` already says the manager consumes — "it receives facts from ... PCIe enumeration". **`kernel/pci` is a new crate on the `kernel/virtio` model:** `#![forbid(unsafe_code)]`, all device access behind a `ConfigSpace` trait, so the ECAM arithmetic, the multi-function rules and the read-modify-restore BAR probe are ordinary logic a mock bus exercises on the host (15 tests). The boot glue implements the trait and is where the `unsafe` lives. **Three things the walk gets right because they are easy to get wrong.** A single-function device answers at *every* function number, so probing 1..8 unconditionally reports seven phantoms — the header-type multi-function bit gates it. A bridge's `bus-range` need not start at bus 0, and the first bus in the range sits at **offset 0** of the ECAM window, so treating the bus number as absolute reads megabytes past the end. And **QEMU leaves BARs unassigned**: placement is this OS's job, sized by writing all-ones and reading the mask back, with the original restored before anything else touches the function and memory decoding enabled only once every BAR is placed — a device left decoding an unplaced BAR answers at whatever address zero happens to be. **Negative checks, both biting on the real machine:** understating the ECAM window by 4× → `OutsideEcam` and exit 65, the walk refusing rather than reading past it; and a forwarding window smaller than the endpoint's BAR → `WindowExhausted`, refused rather than placed overlapping. **Verified:** 39+1 tests, lint/fmt clean, exit 33 with an endpoint attached, and both existing RISC-V 64 boots unchanged — the diskless one reports the host bridge alone ("1 function, none with a memory BAR to place"), which is the honest reading of a bus with nothing on it. **Scope landed vs. planned.** This is the **enumeration half** of the milestone, which the plan named in advance as the seam to split on. What is **not** built: the resource-graph node does not yet carry a PCI identity, there is no `DeviceInfo` syscall, and the manager therefore does not bind a PCI device by class — a PCI function is discovered and placed but not yet grantable. **v0 deviations:** bus 0 only (a bridge is recorded but not recursed into); one 32-bit non-prefetchable window, with I/O and prefetchable ranges skipped rather than misreported; **an unplaceable BAR fails the whole walk** rather than degrading one device, because reporting *which* device degraded needs the event facility this crate deliberately does not have; no MSI/MSI-X and no INTx routing (`interrupt-map` is unparsed), so a PCI device has no interrupt; no SR-IOV, ATS/PRI/PASID, hotplug or option-ROM policy; and no IOMMU — which this milestone is what unblocks, since `virt`'s SMMUv3 and q35's `intel-iommu` sit in front of the PCIe root complex and not in front of virtio-mmio. RISC-V 64 only; AArch64's `pcie@10000000` is the same `pci-host-ecam-generic` and needs only boot glue. | The graph identity + `DeviceInfo` syscall + bind-by-class that complete this milestone; then INTx or MSI so a PCI device can interrupt; then the IOMMU. |
| D115 | **A PCI device is bound by class — the half D114 left (M76).** D114 discovered a `virtio-blk-pci` endpoint and placed its BAR, but a PCI function was not *grantable*: the resource graph held no identity for it and nothing could ask what it was. Now the graph records what the kernel learned enumerating, a **`DeviceInfo`** syscall (28) hands it to a holder, and the ring-3 manager classifies and binds a device **it cannot read**. The verdict says it: *"the manager classified a device it cannot read (class 0x01 from the graph, not from a register) and bound it to two drivers in turn; each reported back 0x504300001af41001 — the vendor/device the kernel enumerated."* **Why a syscall was needed at all, and why it grants nothing.** A virtio-mmio transport says what it is at offset 8, so a manager holding a capability maps it and reads — enumeration by access, the property that makes the manager a program rather than a table. A PCI function says what it is in **config space**, which is not per-device, so that route is closed (D114). `DeviceInfo` is the narrow replacement: it answers a question about a capability the caller **already holds**, requires no `Rights::MAP`, reads nothing from the device and maps nothing. Possessing the capability is the whole check — the kind of query that makes a capability *usable* rather than stronger. **`UNKNOWN` is an answer, not a failure.** A device the graph has no identity for reports `DeviceInfoKind::UNKNOWN`, and the manager's documented response is to map it and read its registers — which is what happens for every virtio-mmio device, unchanged. The manager now asks the kernel *first* and falls back to probing, and the same rule governs the **return** path: a device coming back from a dead driver is re-verified the way it was classified, because a PCI BAR has no magic value to check. **What the drivers report.** `blk-probe` asks `DeviceInfo` about what it was handed and, for a PCI device, reports the identity instead of driving: a **virtio-pci transport is not implemented**, and reading a BAR whose layout this driver does not speak and calling the result a magic value would be the dishonest alternative. That a driver checks what it was given is not ceremony — the bind reply echoes a *class the manager assigned*, and the identity is what the *kernel enumerated*; this is the one place the two are compared. **The bug this surfaced, which was latent.** Running a driver check *early* in the boot made it fail with a contained ring-3 fault. The shared syscall hook flags a `DebugWrite` from a thread that is neither the IPC check's server nor its client — right for the IPC check, wrong for a driver check that collects reports from every driver it spawns. The existing driver checks passed **only because they ran after the IPC check and its stale thread ids happened to match**. A `REPORTS_FROM_ANY_THREAD` flag says so explicitly, and a driver check can now run anywhere in the boot. **Negative check (the one D114 could not run):** record class `0xff` instead of the enumerated `0x01` → the manager finds no `Block` device, the bind is refused, and both drivers report `0xdead…0050_0101` rather than binding whatever was to hand. Binding uses the class the kernel recorded, and nothing else. **Verified:** 41/41 tests (241 kcore unit tests, 3 new), lint/fmt clean, no new `unsafe`, exit 33, and the virtio-mmio `driver-rebind` path unchanged — the manager's new question is asked of it too and answered `UNKNOWN`. **v0 deviations:** no virtio-pci transport, so a bound PCI device is identified and not driven; the PCI class→`DeviceClass` map is two entries in the manager's code rather than data (a manifest is still absent); `DeviceInfo` reports what enumeration found and nothing about capabilities, MSI or link state; and RISC-V 64 only. | ~~A virtio-pci transport so a PCI device can be driven~~ (done, D125); ~~INTx or MSI so it can interrupt~~ (done, D117); ~~the class map as manifest data~~ (done, D130); ~~AArch64's bridge~~ (done, D114). **Everything this entry deferred is built.** What it did not defer, and still stands: `DeviceInfo` reports a device's identity, revision, bus and virtio-pci structure offsets (D130) and nothing about its MSI capability or link state — no consumer has needed either, and adding fields nobody reads is how a record grows past what anyone maintains. |
| D116 | **PCIe enumeration on AArch64, and the machine survey MSI-X needs (M77).** Groundwork for message-signalled interrupts, which is why this port rather than the one that already had PCIe: **the RISC-V 64 `virt` machine cannot receive an MSI at all.** Its device tree has `plic@c000000` and nothing else — no `msi-parent`, no IMSIC — and the PLIC is a wired-interrupt controller. MSI there needs `-M virt,aia=aplic-imsic` and two new interrupt controllers, which is also a standing **RVA23S conformance gap** worth recording: the profile mandates Ssaia and this port runs a PLIC. The AArch64 tree has `arm,gic-v2m-frame` at **`v2m@8020000`**, `msi-controller` and `msi-map` — and on GICv2m an MSI arrives as an **ordinary SPI**, so D84's existing ring-3 interrupt path carries it unchanged. **What porting cost, and what it proves.** `kernel/pci`, `pci_host()`, `DeviceInfo` and the device manager are untouched — the diff is boot glue, which is the claim D114 made and this tested. The walk finds `1af4:1000` at `00:01.0`, class `0x02_0000`, and places its BAR at `0x1000_0000` — an endpoint QEMU attaches by default, that nothing in the device tree names. **Two defects the port found, both about *where* memory is reachable on this port and neither present on RISC-V.** (1) **ECAM is at `0x40_1000_0000`** — 256 GiB up — while `DEVICE_RANGE` is `(0, 0x4000_0000)`. Every device here is reached at its physical address through the **low-half identity map**, and widening that blanket range would identity-map 256 GiB of nothing; worse, the low half is `TTBR0`, which a per-process address space *replaces* (D76), so a mapping made there stops existing the moment a driver runs. The windows are therefore mapped explicitly into the **high half** at `DIRECT_MAP_BASE + phys`, which survives every process switch — the same place RISC-V reaches devices from. (2) **The device tree is not readable at `dtb` after the MMU switch.** Every other reader on this port uses that physical address *before* the switch, while the boot stub's identity still covers RAM; afterwards the blob sits above `DEVICE_RANGE` and reading it there is a data abort. Both surfaced as `esr=0x96000005, far=0x48000000` and were diagnosed from the trap line. Also: the bridge forwards `0x2eff_0000`, not a whole number of 2 MiB blocks, so the mapping rounds **up** — rounding down would leave the last BAR placed in the window unmapped. **Verified:** 41/41 tests per target (the summary reports a build failure as *skipped*, which hid a broken image in D115), lint/fmt clean, exit 33, and every existing AArch64 verdict intact — the PCIe walk sits ahead of the EL0 checks and does not disturb the virtio-mmio path. RISC-V 64, x86-64, ARM 32 and RISC-V 32 unchanged. **What this does NOT do: MSI-X is not built.** The machine is surveyed and the windows are reachable, but no capability list is walked, no MSI or MSI-X is programmed, and a PCI device still cannot interrupt. That is the next milestone, and the v2m frame's location and SPI range (from its `TYPER`) are what it starts from. | MSI-X and MSI: the capability walk, the v2m doorbell, and a device-initiated interrupt proven with `-device edu`, which raises one from a single register write. |
| D117 | **A PCI device raises a message-signalled interrupt (M78).** `kernel/pci` gains a capability-list walk and both message-signalled paths; the AArch64 glue probes the GICv2m frame, allocates an SPI from the range the frame reports in its own `TYPER`, programs a device, and takes the interrupt. On the running machine: *"a PCI device raised a message-signalled interrupt: `1234:11e8` wrote the v2m doorbell at `0x8020040`, the GIC took SPI 80, and it arrived as an ordinary wired interrupt."* That last clause is the design: **GICv2m turns a message back into a wire**, so `enable_irq`, the IRQ→port bridge and the EL0 driver waiting on its port (D84) are reused with no change at all — nothing downstream knows it handled a message. **The bug, and how it was cornered.** The first working version delivered nothing, while every layer read back correct: MSI enabled and 64-bit, address the doorbell, data the allocated SPI, bus master on, the frame reporting SPIs 80..143, the GIC enable the same path the virtio lines use. The split that found it was reading **the device's own interrupt status before acknowledging** — the ack clears it, and an earlier diagnostic that read afterwards had reported a misleading zero. It showed `irq_status = 1`: the device *had* raised, so the loss was between the device and the CPU. The cause is that **`enable()` never touched `GICD_ICFGR`**, leaving the SPI level-triggered — and a v2m doorbell write is `qemu_irq_pulse`, an edge. A pulse into a level-configured input has nothing to latch and is simply lost. Wired device lines are level-triggered and the reset default was right for every interrupt this OS had taken until now; a message is the first that is not a line. `set_edge_triggered` is the fix, and it is what Linux's own GICv2m driver does. **Also built:** the capability walk, bounded three ways — the list-present status bit, every offset inside the 256-byte header and 4-byte aligned, and termination within 48 links — so a device pointing a capability at itself is a typed error rather than a hang. MSI-X programming masks the entry, writes address and data, and unmasks **last**, so a device cannot send on a half-written address; the size field is read as *entries*, not the `entries - 1` the register holds. MSI respects the 64-bit layout bit that moves the data register from `+0x08` to `+0x0c`. 26 host tests, and the mock bus grew from a 64-byte config header to the full 256 because that is where a capability list lives. **A trap avoided:** the delivery wait spins rather than using `wfi`. This check runs before the periodic tick, so `wfi` would have only the awaited interrupt to wake it and a missing one would hang the boot instead of failing the check — the first version did exactly that (D85, D104, now three times). **Negative checks, all three biting, the first being the bug itself:** leave the SPI level-triggered → nothing arrives, exit 65; program an address that is not the doorbell → nothing arrives; allocate an SPI outside the frame's `TYPER` range → nothing arrives. Each distinguishes *this* interrupt from something else on the machine happening to fire. **MSI-X is proven only as far as configuration** and the verdict says so: on `1af4:1000` the capability is found, its table located in a BAR this kernel placed, vector 0 programmed to the doorbell and unmasked, and **read back from the device** — but making a virtio endpoint *send* one needs feature negotiation, queue setup and vector assignment, which is the transport and the next milestone. **Verified:** 42/42 tests per target, lint/fmt clean, exit 33 with `-device edu` attached and every existing AArch64 verdict intact; boots without it report `msi: skipped`. RISC-V 64, x86-64, ARM 32 and RISC-V 32 unchanged. **v0 deviations:** one vector per device; no per-vector masking beyond the initial unmask; the SPI allocator hands out from the low end with no reuse; MSI-X unproven as a *source*; and the interrupt is taken by a kernel hook rather than delivered to a ring-3 driver — the ring-3 leg is D84's and unchanged, so what is new here is the source, not the path. | A virtio-pci transport, which turns the configured MSI-X into a delivered one and makes a PCI device drivable; then NVMe. |
| D118 | **The SMMUv3 encoding layer, and the machine survey behind it (M79).** First step toward the DMA scoping `docs/drivers/01` requires and D78 deferred — on the grounds that "QEMU `virt` has no IOMMU", which **stopped being true when PCIe landed**: `-M virt,iommu=smmuv3` puts an SMMUv3 in front of the PCIe root complex, which is what D114–D117 built. **Machine surveyed and confirmed, not assumed:** the `arm,smmu-v3` node is at `0x0905_0000` size `0x2_0000` — inside `DEVICE_RANGE`, so it is reachable through the low-half identity map like the GIC, unlike ECAM which needed its own high-half mapping (D116). The PCIe node's `iommu-map` is `rid_base 0, iommu_base 0, length 0x10000` — **identity**, so a function's stream id is its RID (`bus << 8 \| device << 3 \| function`). And booting the current kernel with `iommu=smmuv3` changes nothing: exit 33, MSI still delivered, because until `CR0.SMMUEN` is set every stream bypasses. **`kernel/smmu` is a new crate on the `kernel/pci` model:** `#![forbid(unsafe_code)]`, hardware access behind a `Registers` trait, and everything in it is *encoding* rather than poking — stream-table entries, command-queue entries, queue index arithmetic, event decoding, and stage-2 descriptors, with 12 host tests. That split exists because encoding is what a mock can check: an entry with a field in the wrong place yields a device that looks configured and translates wrongly, and the hardware's only comment is a fault. **Two things the crate gets right that are easy to get wrong.** **Stage 2 is not stage 1 with different flags** — `S2AP` sits at bits 7:6 where stage 1 has `AP`, and the memory attributes are a direct `MemAttr` at 5:2 rather than an index into `MAIR`; reusing the kernel's stage-1 helper would produce plausible descriptors that translate wrongly, so the stage-2 encoder is its own function. And a **queue index carries a wrap bit above its index**, which is the only thing distinguishing a full queue from an empty one — both have equal index fields — so `would_overrun` compares wrap as well, and a test walks an index right around its ring twice. Also encoded: `T0SZ` and the walk's start level are returned **together**, because an entry whose `SL0` disagrees with its `T0SZ` faults on every address with nothing in the table to explain it. **What is NOT built: nothing is wired.** No registers are programmed, no stream table exists at runtime, no device is behind an aperture, and no DMA has been translated or refused. The crate changes no boot behaviour, which is why the tree stays green — and it means **these encodings are unvalidated against hardware**: the host tests pin what the specification says the layout is, and only a running SMMU can confirm it. **Verified:** 43/43 tests per target, lint/fmt clean, no new `unsafe`, every port byte-identical. One flake observed and recorded rather than dismissed: `smoke_boot_test` failed once in a parallel full-matrix run and then passed four times in isolation and twice more in full runs; the x86-64 boot has timing-sensitive scheduler and perf demos and the matrix runs QEMU instances concurrently. | Wire it: map the registers, build the stream table and queues, install an STE for `edu`'s stream, enable `SMMUEN`, and prove both halves — a DMA inside the aperture lands, one outside is refused with the event queue naming the stream and address. |
| D119 | **A device now reaches only what it was given (M80).** One PCI function sits behind a stream table with a **one-page stage-2 aperture**, and the same DMA lands or is refused on the address alone: *"stream 0x10 has a one-page aperture: the device's DMA to 0x1000 landed (0x53444d4d55563300 arrived in the page behind it), and the same DMA to 0x200000 was refused by hardware — the SMMU logged a translation fault for that stream at 0x200000."* This is the first time in the project that a device's reach is bounded by hardware rather than by a driver choosing not to overstep, and it closes the reason D78 gave for deferring the IOMMU — a reason that expired when PCIe landed and `virt`'s SMMUv3 turned out to sit in front of the root complex. **The bug that cost the most, and it was not in the SMMU.** An earlier version brought the SMMU up correctly — `CR0ACK` confirming SMMUEN, `GBPA` reading back with ABORT — and observed no transfer at all, in or out of the aperture. The measurement that settled it was one that should have come first: **disable the SMMU entirely and aim the DMA at a raw physical address.** It behaved identically, so the device was never performing DMA and every SMMU-side conclusion drawn until then was unsupported. The cause was a **completion wait bounded in loop iterations rather than in time**: this device runs its DMA off a timer with a delay measured in milliseconds, and a spin counted in iterations expires in whatever wall time those happen to take. Waiting against the generic counter instead makes it work first try — and the STE and stage-2 encodings, suspected for three rounds of debugging, were right all along. **Three encoder bugs were caught before hardware ever saw them,** which is the case for putting encoding in a host-tested crate rather than in boot glue: the start level for a 30-bit address is **level 2, not level 1**, because each level resolves a fixed slice and the start level is the lowest whose slice still holds the address's top bit; **`SL0` is an encoding, not the level number** (`0b00` = level 2, `0b11` = level 3), so writing the level would have started the walk two levels off; and **`S2R` must be set** or a stage-2 fault is terminated silently, leaving an aperture whose refusals cannot be observed. **A device-register lesson:** `edu`'s DMA registers are `dma_addr_t` and its decode has no case for an upper half's offset, so two 32-bit writes set the low word and drop the high one without complaint — the BAR window gained a 64-bit accessor. **Negative checks: all three run, two bite, and the third is recorded as a non-result.** Widen the aperture to cover the second address → the transfer that must fail succeeds, so the boundary is the page table's and not an artefact of the address being unreachable anyway. Install no entry for the stream → even the in-aperture transfer fails, distinguishing \"refused because unmapped\" from \"refused because nothing was configured\". But **removing the configuration invalidation changes nothing** — the boot still passes. The `CFGI` command is what the architecture requires after writing a stream table entry, and it stays; what this says is that **this emulator does not cache configuration in a way this check can observe**, so its necessity is unverified here rather than demonstrated. Recorded as such: the same shape as the ASID-fence checks that do not bite under QEMU (D110, D107). **Verified:** 44/44 tests per target (one new), lint/fmt clean, exit 33, every existing AArch64 verdict intact including MSI delivery; boots without an SMMU or without `edu` report `smmu: skipped`. Other ports byte-identical. **v0 deviations:** one device, one page, one stream; the stream id is taken as the function's RID on the strength of this machine's identity `iommu-map` rather than parsed from it; there is no lease, no revocation on driver crash, and no reuse of the aperture; and **`dma_alloc` is untouched** — ring-3 DMA still returns a physical address rather than an IOVA, so the scoping proven here is not yet what a driver gets. | Wire `dma_alloc` to hand out IOVAs inside a per-device aperture, so a driver's DMA is scoped by construction; then revocation on crash, which is the other half of the design's DMA-safety sentence. |
| D120 | **The resource graph learns what a device may reach (M81).** D119 proved an aperture with boot glue; this is the first step to making it a property of the system rather than a demonstration. `DeviceNode` gains an optional `DeviceAperture` — base, length, and how much has been handed out — with `set_aperture`, `aperture_of_object` and `allocate_in_aperture`. Additive: no behaviour changes, and 5 host tests. **Two decisions worth stating, both about what the type refuses to do.** It **never reuses an address**: an aperture that wrapped would hand a driver an address a device may still hold in a descriptor ring the kernel cannot see, turning a stale descriptor into a write to whatever now occupies it. Recycling needs a way to know the device has forgotten, which is what a lease would give and this does not have. And `None` means **"this device's DMA is not scoped"**, which is the honest state on a machine with no IOMMU and must not be conflated with an aperture covering everything — a test pins that "no aperture" and "aperture exhausted" stay distinguishable, because a caller that confuses them reports an unscoped device as out of memory. **Not wired:** nothing sets an aperture yet and `dma_alloc` still returns a physical address. Wiring it needs a decision this milestone deliberately does not make — what `dma_alloc` should do for a device with no aperture, on a port with no IOMMU at all, where refusing outright would break every existing ring-3 driver and returning a physical address is the fallback the guidelines forbid leaving silent. | Decide the no-aperture case, then have `dma_alloc` map into the device's stage-2 table and return the IOVA, so a driver's DMA is scoped by construction. |
| D121 | **An unscoped DMA grant says that it is unscoped (M82).** D120 gave the graph somewhere to record a device's aperture; this answers the question that blocked using it — what `dma_alloc` does for a device that has none. **It returns the physical address and reports the fact.** Both outcomes are legitimate: a machine may have no IOMMU at all, and RISC-V 64 has none, so refusing would leave a whole port unable to run a driver. But an unscoped grant must not read like a scoped one, and returning the address without saying so is exactly the silent degradation `docs/lifecycle/04` forbids. So the grant proceeds and a `DEVICE_DMA_UNSCOPED` record (EventKind 18, `Severity::Warning`) names the device, the user VA and the physical address the device can use to reach anything. **Why an event rather than a syscall-ABI change.** The distinction is a property of the *system's* configuration, not of the caller's request — the driver asked for a DMA buffer and got one either way. Widening `DmaAllocArgs` would version an ABI that nine hand-built descriptors encode (D113's lesson) to carry a fact the caller cannot act on today, since no device has an aperture yet. When apertures are wired the caller-visible change is the address itself becoming an IOVA, and that is the point at which the ABI question becomes real. **Tested both directions:** a device with no aperture produces the report; a device with one produces the grant record and no report — otherwise the report would be noise rather than a distinction. **Verified:** 44/44 tests per target (248 kcore unit tests, 2 new), lint/fmt clean, every port byte-identical — nothing sets an aperture yet, so every grant on every port is currently unscoped and now says so. **v0 deviations:** the address is still physical everywhere, because no aperture is installed; the report is a kernel event with no ring-3 harvest, so a driver cannot yet read its own scoping state; and nothing maps a DMA frame into a device's stage-2 table, which is what would make the address an IOVA. | Install an aperture per device at bind time and have `dma_alloc` map into it, so the returned address is an IOVA and the report stops firing — the point at which a driver's DMA is scoped by construction. |
| D122 | **A driver's DMA is scoped by construction — `dma_alloc` hands back an IOVA (M83).** The sentence D119 and D121 were each half of: *"ring-3 asked for a DMA buffer and was given an IOVA, not a physical address: `dma_alloc` returned `0x10000` for a page at phys `0x40020000`, and the device reached the driver's own buffer through it (`0xd4a0cafed4a0cafe` came back).
