The same device's DMA to `0x200000` was refused."* The EL0 program is the **unchanged D78 probe** — it asks for a buffer, writes a magic through its own user VA, and reports the number it got — and what changed is the answer, not the question. That is the point: a driver does not know, and must not need to know, whether the address it programs into its device is translated. **The seam, and why it is a trait.** `kcore::devmgr::DmaMapper` is one method — make `[iova, len)` name `[phys, len)` for this device object. The kernel core knows which addresses belong to a device and nothing about SMMUv3, which is right, because **four of the five ports have no IOMMU at all**; a port that has one hands a `&mut dyn DmaMapper` to the dispatcher alongside the frame allocator, and a port that has none passes `None` and says so on every grant. The mapper is keyed by `ObjectId` rather than stream id for the same reason — stream ids are the SMMU's vocabulary, not the graph's. **`map` cannot allocate**, deliberately: a mapper that asks for a frame fails halfway through installing a translation, and unwinding an IOMMU table is harder than sizing it up front, so a stream's page tables are built whole when its aperture is installed and `map` only writes into them. That is what bounds an aperture to `LEAF_SPAN` (2 MiB, one level-3 table). **Three refusals, no fallbacks.** A device the graph says translates is refused — not given a physical address — when the port has no mapper (`InvalidMapping`), when the mapper does not recognize it (`InvalidMapping`), or when its aperture is spent (`OutOfMemory`, which is why D120 made "no aperture" and "spent aperture" distinguishable). Handing back a physical address in any of them would answer a request for a scoped buffer with an unscoped one under the same name, and the caller has no way to tell. A refusal also **reclaims the page** mapped before the outcome was known, so a driver that retries does not lose a frame per attempt. **A positive record, not the absence of a warning:** `DEVICE_DMA_SCOPED` (EventKind 19) carries the user VA, the IOVA and the physical page — the last two differ, which is what a consumer checks to know translation is in play. Every grant now emits exactly one of scoped/unscoped. **AArch64:** the SMMU became an object that is brought up once and stays enabled — disabling it between checks would let a device reach memory in the gap — owning the stream table, both queues, the command producer index and the event **consumer** index, the last so two checks each assert on their own refusal rather than both re-reading the first. `smmu_check` now maps its target page *through the same seam*, so the aperture proof and the ring-3 grant exercise one mechanism rather than two. **Verified:** 44/44 Bazel tests, 253 kcore unit tests (5 new), lint/fmt clean over the whole graph, exit 33; the boot test now requires **both** markers, since the hardware property held without the driver-visible one for two milestones. Boots without `iommu=smmuv3` report `smmu: skipped` and every grant stays unscoped-and-says-so; other ports' verdicts unchanged. **v0 deviations:** one device, one stream, a 2-page aperture, and the aperture is installed by boot glue rather than at bind time by the device manager; **no revocation** — teardown reclaims the buffer's frame but the stage-2 entry and the IOVA stay, so a device still holding the address in a descriptor ring would write into a freed frame, which is the other half of the DMA-safety sentence and is not built here; the stream id is still taken as the function's RID on the strength of this machine's identity `iommu-map`; **no barriers** — table writes are not `dsb`-fenced before the `CFGI`/`TLBI` that publish them, which QEMU does not need and real hardware does (inherited from D119, now stated); the ring-3 virtio drivers are virtio-**mmio** and this SMMU fronts only the PCIe root complex, so no *compiled* driver is scoped yet — that needs a ring-3 PCI driver. | Install the aperture at bind time from the device manager, and then revocation on driver crash — reclaiming the IOVA and its translation together — which is what makes a lease, and what would let an aperture reuse an address. |
| D123 | **A DMA lease — the device stops reaching the buffer when the driver stops holding the device (M84).** D122 gave a driver an IOVA; nothing ever took it away. Now: *"the driver's capability was reclaimed, and the same DMA to `0x10000` — the address that had just worked — was refused too: the SMMU logged a translation fault for that stream at `0x10004`."* The word is the specs' own — `docs/drivers/01`: *"IOMMU mappings are scoped to a device and lease … revoked on driver crash, device removal, or lease expiration"*; `docs/hardware/04`: *"pinned for the life of the DMA lease"*. **The asymmetry that makes this a milestone rather than a tidy-up.** D93 deliberately gave process teardown no revocation code, and was right to: a register window lives in the dying address space and `AddressSpace::teardown` destroys it. **An IOMMU translation does not** — it lives in the IOMMU and outlives the process completely. So a lease has a third departure route a window never had, and skipping it means a device still reaching frames the allocator has re-handed to someone else. `DmaMapper` grows `translates`/`begin_lease`/`end_lease` around `map`; `Executive::end_device_leases` is the sweep, called first inside `reclaim_devices` — *before* a single handle moves, so a device whose capability cannot be **delivered** (message full, manager's queue full) still stops translating for a corpse. **Reuse, which is what D120 was waiting for.** An aperture never reissues an address *within* a lease; across leases it must, or a driver rebound a few times exhausts its window. Ending a lease is the "way to know the device has forgotten" D120 named as missing, and `begin_lease` re-zeroes the table anyway, so a new lease cannot inherit a translation even if a teardown were skipped. `smmu_check` now takes and releases its own lease, so the ring-3 driver demonstrably gets the *same* base back. **The lease begins lazily, at first `dma_alloc`, and the reason is not convenience.** A lease should only begin where it can be made to end: the arrival chokepoint (`install_transferred_handles`) holds no executive and no mapper, and boot glue installs device handles directly, bypassing dispatch — including into processes torn down with no departure route at all. Beginning there would strand leases naming dead processes and, since **a lease is exclusive** (a second holder's `dma_alloc` is `AccessDenied`, or its buffers would vanish when someone else gave the device up), lock those devices out permanently. **Two bugs this found in its own scaffolding.** Setting the stream-table entry back to *abort* on revocation looked obvious and is wrong: an invalid entry raises `C_BAD_STREAMID`, which reports no address and reads exactly like a device that was never configured. Emptying the translation table and leaving the entry **valid** is what "reaches nothing" should mean, and it produces an ordinary stage-2 fault naming the address — enforcement and observability, which `docs/drivers/01` asks for separately. And **one refused transfer does not log one event**: an 8-byte DMA to an unmapped address logged three, so a consumer stepping one record per check read the *previous* check's refusal and would have passed on it; the queue is now drained before and after each transfer it asserts on. **Verified:** 44/44 Bazel tests, 262 kcore unit tests (5 new), lint/fmt clean, exit 33; the boot test requires the revocation marker as well as the two from D122. Other ports pass `None` and are unchanged. **v0 deviations:** the lease begins at first DMA rather than at bind, because binding is still not a kernel-visible event (D122's own "next"); one lease per device, one device behind an IOMMU; the close route is plumbed but unreachable on the ports that have an IOMMU (`HandleClose` has no arm outside x86-64, which has none); `end_lease` cannot refuse, on the grounds that a teardown has no caller that could act on one; frames are **not pinned** for the lease's life as `docs/hardware/04` requires — safety here comes from teardown ordering, and the boot check's order (end the lease, prove the fault, *then* free) is what keeps that honest; invalidation is `TLBI_NSNH_ALL` rather than VMID-scoped; and still no `dsb` fencing before `CFGI`/`TLBI` (inherited, D119/D122). | The device manager taking the lease at bind time, which needs binding to become a kernel-visible event; then pinning, so the lease's guarantee does not rest on teardown order. |
| D124 | **A manager-bound PCI device, behind the SMMU, and its replacement leased the addresses the first driver's death released (M85).** Two capabilities existed in this tree and had never met: DMA scoping and leasing, proven only for `edu` — a device boot glue hands to an EL0 process directly — and bind-by-class, which lived only on RISC-V 64, a machine with no IOMMU at all. `device_register_identified` had exactly one production caller. AArch64 enumerated PCI, placed BARs and ran an SMMU, but put **no** PCI function in the resource graph, so `DeviceInfo` always answered `Unknown` there. Now: *"the manager classified a device it cannot read (class `0x01` from the graph, not from a register) and bound it to two drivers in turn; each reported back `0x504300001af41001`, the vendor/device the kernel enumerated. Both were behind the SMMU on stream `0x18`, and both were leased the same device-visible addresses from `0x10000` — the second driver got back what the first one's death released."* **The bug this would have shipped, caught by reading the mechanism rather than the output.** AArch64 folds ring-3 driver reports into one sink with **XOR**. The virtio-mmio rebind check survives that only because each incarnation rotates the magic by its incarnation number, so the two reports differ. A PCI device's identity is *the same both times* — two successful runs would have cancelled to **zero**, and a check reading the sink could not tell "ran twice, agreed" from "never ran". RISC-V 64 had already hit this and answered it with an indexed report array; AArch64 gains `EL0_REPORTS`, keyed by order, and `driver_rebind_check` returns both reports instead of a fold. That landed and was verified against the unchanged virtio-mmio verdict *before* anything depended on it, because a check that passes on nothing passes quietly. **The SMMU stopped being gated on `edu`.** It was brought up inside the `(Some(smmu), Some(edu))` arm, so a machine with an IOMMU but without that one debug device had no IOMMU as far as the kernel was concerned. It now comes up whenever the device tree has one, into an `Option<Smmu>` — stored into its slot *before* anything takes a pointer, since `EL0_DISPATCH_IOMMU` is a raw pointer and a slot later moved out of would dangle. The new boot test attaches **no `edu`**, which is what proves it. **`blk-probe` asks for DMA on the PCI path**, because taking a lease is what a bound driver does; it does not *use* the buffer, since no virtio-pci transport exists to use it with, and saying so beats pretending. The report format is unchanged, which keeps RISC-V's expected value byte-identical — verified by running that port's PCI boot, where the same call returns a physical address and an honest `DEVICE_DMA_UNSCOPED`. **Why `edu` cannot be the bound device:** its class is `0x00ff00`, which the manager maps to `DeviceClass::Unknown` and never matches. Teaching it a class would have been a protocol change in service of a test. **A defect D123 shipped, found by running the two halves in one boot.** With the SMMU checks and the driver-framework checks in the same image, `device-events` failed: the lease sweep emitted `DEVICE_DMA_LEASE_ENDED` records carrying **no causal id**. `reclaim_devices` mints one because a process dying is work that begins outside the running trace and the thread whose cause it would inherit is gone; `end_leases_of` is the same class of work and did not. It does now. No standard test caught it because no test ran both check families together — which is itself the finding. The single-lease path (a transfer or a close) still inherits rather than minting, because there the departure *is* caused by the caller's work and minting would break the chain. **Verified:** 45/45 Bazel tests (one new), 262 kcore unit tests, lint/fmt clean, exit 33; every existing AArch64 verdict intact, RISC-V 64's `pci-bind` unchanged, and a boot with all four devices attached now green end to end. **v0 deviations:** the bound driver identifies its device but does not drive it — a virtio-pci transport is still absent, so the lease is proven by the addresses issued rather than by a transfer through them (the transfer proof remains `edu`'s, on its own stream in the same boot); one lease per device; the window is still installed by boot glue rather than negotiated at bind; and the SMMU bring-up is still nested inside the GICv2m arm, so a machine with an IOMMU and no v2m frame would skip it. **The IOMMU pointer's lifetime, recorded rather than left to be rediscovered.** `EL0_DISPATCH_IOMMU` is a raw `*mut Smmu` set before a check's scheduler run and cleared after it, and now has two set-sites rather than one. Two properties hold only by argument, not by construction. It is cleared on the **success path only** — an early `?` between the set and the clear leaves it set, which is contained today because every caller reports FATAL and the boot exits, and would stop being contained the moment one recovered instead. And the `&mut Smmu` the check holds stays live across `scheduler().run()` while the syscall hook derives a second `&mut` from the pointer; that is the same discipline `EL0_DISPATCH_FRAMES` has relied on since D79 — parked borrows are never dereferenced while parked — but the lease work made it load-bearing for a second object, and inheriting a rule silently is how it stops being true. | A virtio-pci transport, so a bound PCI device can be driven through the lease rather than only issued one; then the manager negotiating the window at bind time; and a scope guard for the dispatch-hook pointers, so clearing them is structural rather than a path every early return has to remember. |
| D125 | **A virtio-pci transport — the device is driven, not just identified (M86).** `blk-probe` said the gap plainly in a comment: *"a virtio-pci transport is not implemented, so this driver reports what it was given and stops there rather than driving a BAR whose layout it does not speak."* Three ledger entries closed with the same "next". Now: *"sector 0 read over the PCI transport from `1af4:1001`, magic verified; its controls were found through 5 vendor capabilities."* **The seam, and why it is a seam rather than a rewrite.** `kernel/virtio` was virtio-mmio-shaped: `Blk`/`Net` generic over a two-method, 32-bit-only `Mmio` trait. They are now generic over a `Transport` trait stated in *operations* — reset, agree features, configure a queue, notify — and **a blanket `impl<M: Mmio> Transport for M` leaves all four existing drivers untouched**, in-kernel and ring-3 alike. That step landed first and was verified against unchanged verdicts before anything depended on it. Everything below the seam — the split-virtqueue layout, descriptor chains, used-ring polling, the block codec, the accessors that refuse to panic on device input — is written once and is what both transports run; the host test proves it by driving the *same* device-side queue logic through the PCI path. **Natural-width access is the substance, not a detail.** virtio-pci's common configuration is a packed record, not an array of registers: `device_status` is one byte at +0x14, and `queue_select`/`queue_size`/`queue_enable` are 16-bit fields packed three to a pair of words. A 32-bit write to `device_status` also rewrites `config_generation` and `queue_select` — the field that decides which queue every following access means. So `Regs` has 8/16/32-bit accessors and **the mock asserts the width of every access**, which is the only reason a wrong-width bug could not ship. **Two things the hardware taught that the specification's arithmetic hides.** A **transitional** device id is not the modern one minus a constant: QEMU presents `1af4:1001`, and `0x1001 - 0x1040` is a device type of `0xffc1` — a driver knowing only the modern `0x1040 + type` rule refuses a working disk. Both encodings are decoded and both are pinned by a test. And **the capability walk had to become resumable**: virtio publishes one vendor capability per structure, and `find_capability` stopped at the first, so a driver would configure a device it had not finished reading. **A hardcoded literal, caught by refusing to keep it.** The verdict first reported "3 vendor capabilities" because three are what the transport needs; the device publishes **5** (it also carries DEVICE and PCI_CFG). Reporting the measured count rather than the expected one is what surfaced it — a verdict that states a constant proves nothing. **Verified:** 46/46 Bazel tests (one new), 14 `kernel/virtio` host tests (6 new), 28 `kernel/pci` (2 new), lint/fmt clean, exit 33; every existing virtio verdict byte-identical on both ports. **v0 deviations:** the in-kernel driver **skips when an SMMU is running** — with `GBPA` aborting, a device whose stream has no live translation cannot DMA, and driving one through an aperture the kernel holds is the next milestone rather than a silent part of this one; the ISR structure is read-to-clear and MSI-X vectors are not assigned, so completions are polled; `num_queues` and per-queue MSI-X vectors have no representation; and the ring-3 driver still cannot use this, because `MapDevice` grants exactly one page while these structures span four. | ~~`MapDevice` over a device's whole window~~ (done, D126), ~~so the ring-3 driver can reach a virtio-pci device's config structures~~ (done, D130 — the kernel reports the offsets, since a driver cannot read config space); then the in-kernel driver taking a lease so it can be driven behind the SMMU. |
| D126 | **A device window is the right region, at its real size, mapped whole (M87).** `MapDevice` mapped exactly one page, and the length the resource graph held was used for nothing but an event field — right when every device was a virtio-mmio slot 0x200 bytes wide, wrong once PCI landed. Now a ring-3 driver reads a word from **`0x2000` into its own window** and the kernel reads the same physical address through its direct map and gets the same bytes: *"each was granted its device's whole `0x4000` window and read `0x800` from `0x2000` into it — past the first page, and the same bytes the kernel reads at that physical address."* `0x800` is 2048, the test disk's capacity in sectors, which is what makes the agreement evidence rather than two zeroes matching. **Three problems, and only one was visible.** The page count is the obvious one. Underneath it: **no registration site anywhere passed a real length** — all fourteen passed `FRAME_SIZE` while the truth sat discarded at the call site (`let (bar, _) = f.first_bar()`, and `block_device_base` returning only a base). And **`first_bar()` names the wrong BAR**: it is the lowest-indexed one, which on a virtio-pci function is the MSI-X table, not the BAR the configuration structures live in. Mapping the whole window without that fix would have mapped all of the wrong region — so the device's own vendor capabilities now choose, on both ports, and only a function publishing none falls back to the first BAR. **The constraint that decided the design.** `summarize_device_events` pairs each `DEVICE_WINDOW_REVOKED` against an outstanding `(device, VA)` from `DEVICE_WINDOW_MAPPED`, and `describes_a_rebind` calls a device rebound at **two grants**. One event per *page* would have made a single four-page mapping read as four grants — the driver-rebind check would have passed after one mapping, and the evidence it exists to produce would have quietly evaporated. So a window is one record and one event carrying its length, and `Process::device_windows` gained the extent rather than the count of slots. A test pins it. **A latent bug this surfaced.** The failure path called `take_device_windows(object)`, draining *every* window the process held on that device rather than the one being undone. Invisible while a device meant one page; with several it would forget live windows and leave them mapped with nothing able to revoke them. It now takes back only what it just recorded. **Bounded, and refused rather than truncated.** 64 KiB, because D114's argument for enumerating PCI in the kernel was that "`MapDevice` grants a single page against a window of 256 MiB" and an unbounded grant would quietly re-open exactly that; it is also `uabi::layout::PROBE_WINDOW_STRIDE`, so a window can never run into the next device's probe slot. A larger window is a refusal — a driver handed part of its device would find out by faulting somewhere unrelated. **Rollback is total.** Device pages are deliberately untracked, so the space's overlap check is blind to them and a collision is only discovered part-way in; `map_device_range` unmaps what it installed and frees nothing, because every frame here names MMIO and handing one to the allocator would put device registers in the pool the kernel serves anonymous memory from. **Verified:** 46/46 Bazel tests, 267 kcore unit tests (5 new), lint/fmt clean, exit 33 on both ports; `driver-rebind` and `device-events` unchanged, which is the reading the one-event-per-window decision protects. **v0 deviations:** 64 KiB ceiling; the graph still holds **one** region per device, so a device with structures in two BARs cannot be fully granted (`docs/hardware/02` models a device as having several regions); the ring-3 driver reads at an offset the *check* knows and it does not, because the structure offsets live in PCI config space and nothing yet tells a driver where they are — which is what still stands between this and a ring-3 virtio-pci driver. | ~~Tell a driver where its device's structures are — the last thing between a granted window and a ring-3 virtio-pci driver~~ (done, D130); then the in-kernel driver taking a lease so it can be driven behind the SMMU. |
| D127 | **A DMA fault is harvested, recorded, and acted on; a driver's interrupts die with it; and the crash-recovery ladder runs on the ports that have the framework (M88).** Three things `docs/drivers/01` describes and this tree performed only halfway. **(1) Faults were refused, never reported.** The SMMU's event queue was drained by boot checks and by nothing else, so a fault on a running system was invisible: `drain_events` was called from `smmu_check` and `scoped_dma_check` and that was the whole of the harvest. Now the unit's `IRQ_CTRL.EVENTQ` is enabled at bring-up, its event-queue INTID comes from the device tree, and a refusal raises an interrupt that drains the queue into `kcore::event` as `DEVICE_DMA_FAULT` — with the SMMU's own event codes normalized to a `DmaFaultKind` (`kernel/smmu`'s host-tested `Event::class`), because a consumer that had to know which IOMMU produced a record could not read a fleet. **(2) The fault now does something.** `IsolationPolicy` (`Report`, `EndLease`, `EndLeaseAndStop`) is the *"can trigger driver isolation"* half of the spec sentence, and the split between `devmgr::record_dma_fault` (a free function, unconditional) and `Executive::isolate_dma_fault` (needs the graph) is the *"are logged **and can**"* split made structural: a port harvesting a fault from early boot, where no executive exists, still records it. The boot proof: *"the device's refused DMA reached the kernel through the SMMU's own event-queue interrupt (2 records, not by polling), was recorded as a structured DEVICE_DMA_FAULT on stream 0x10 at 0x10004, and policy ended the lease held by process 0x51: the same device's DMA to 0x10000 — the address it was entitled to a moment earlier — is now refused too."* **The bug that hid for two builds** was not in any of that: the spin loop watching the harvest read a plain `u32` the interrupt handler wrote, and LLVM hoisted it out of the loop, so the check timed out on a fault that had already arrived. The counters are atomics now, stored *after* the record, which is what makes the record itself safe to keep as a plain field. **The bug underneath that** was the interrupt never arriving at all: QEMU pulses the SMMU's line and a GIC input left configured level-sensitive latches nothing from a pulse — no error anywhere, the interrupt simply does not exist. The device tree says `EDGE_LO_HI`; nothing read it. `MmioDevice` now carries an `IrqTrigger` parsed from the GIC descriptor's flags cell, and `None` means *the binding does not say* (a one-cell PLIC value) rather than a default someone invented. **(3) Interrupt revocation, which kcore did not have at all.** A register window follows a departing capability (D93) and a DMA lease follows it (D123); the IRQ→port bridge did neither, because it was a bare `port_bind` only the boot glue knew about — so a dead driver's line kept firing into a port nobody would ever drain, and the next driver granted that device would have found its own route refused. `IrqRoute` joins the aperture and the window in the resource graph, `InterruptRouter` is the `DmaMapper`-shaped seam a port implements (GIC, PLIC, and the x86 PIC all do), and revocation runs on the same three departures a lease has — transfer, close, death — masking the line, unbinding the port, and emitting `DEVICE_IRQ_REVOKED`. The proof is what the supervisor *does not* say: *"its device interrupt route was revoked with the driver: the supervisor named no INTID and no port, the resource graph did."* **(4) The ladder, on the ports that have the framework.** `driver_rebind_check` reaped and respawned by hand and emitted nothing, so AArch64 and RISC-V had a rebind and no crash-recovery ladder; the x86-64 port had the ladder and had grown its own copy of the policy. `kcore::supervise::RestartSupervisor` is now the one implementation of the budget and the three records, driven by all three ports, and `kcore::event::summarize_driver_ladder` is the one *reading* of them — host-tested against runs a boot cannot produce on purpose (a restart with no crash behind it, a give-up filed at the wrong severity). The rebind check's first incarnation now **crashes holding its device** — `blk-probe` takes a `CRASH_AFTER_BIND` bit and faults on a null read, a real contained CPU fault rather than a tidy exit, because a corpse that asked to leave has already given back what it held. And the negative half a healthy machine never shows: `driver-giveup` runs a host that crashes every time against a budget of 3 and stops. Read back from the records on both ports: *"4 contained crashes, each answered by exactly one reclaim-and-rebind that recovered 88 frames, and 1 supervisor that spent its budget and gave up rather than respawning for ever."* **Verified:** 46/46 Bazel tests, 297 kcore unit tests (30 new), lint/fmt clean, exit 33 on all five ports. **v0 deviations:** the fault harvest is AArch64-only, because it is the only port with an IOMMU; `IsolationPolicy` is a boot-set static rather than per-device policy from a binding manifest; `EndLeaseAndStop` names the holder for a supervisor to stop rather than stopping it, since `isolate_dma_fault` may run in interrupt context and has no allocator; interrupt routes are still installed by boot glue rather than by a ring-3 manager, so the *acquisition* of a route is not yet capability-mediated the way `MapDevice` is. | Per-device isolation policy read from a binding manifest rather than a boot-set static — the manifest landed in D130, so this is now a `ManifestEntry` field and the harvest reading it, not a missing mechanism; a ring-3 syscall that routes a device's interrupt to a port the caller holds, so a route is acquired under the capability that authorizes it. |
| D128 | **The driver lifecycle exists, and the crash-recovery ladder runs all seven of its steps (M89).** `docs/drivers/01` lists thirteen states and one sentence that decides where they live — *"transitions are observable through structured events"* — and the tree had neither: D112 deferred the ladder to a ring-3 device manager that had no way to emit a record, so the states were a design document and the ladder was three of its seven steps. **The states are ABI now** (`driver_lifecycle.isl`), and `DriverLifecycle` (syscall 29) is the emit path that closes D112. **Who owns what is the whole design.** The manager owns the lifecycle — deciding when a device is Matched, when probing failed, when a degraded device is worth resetting needs a driver table and a binding policy, none of which belong in a kernel. What kcore owns is the two things a manager must not be the only holder of: the **table of legal edges** (`kcore::lifecycle::legal`, host-tested across all 169 ordered pairs) and the **state each device is actually in**. That split is not bureaucracy: a record stream is evidence only if it is a *sequence*, and a manager that emitted `Active -> Degraded` twice or skipped `Probing` would produce a perfectly plausible history that never happened. The kernel refuses a transition whose `from` disagrees with what it recorded, and a lifecycle that tries to open part-way through — a first claim of `Active`, skipping everything that would have had to happen first — is refused too. **Two of the thirteen belong to the driver**, not the manager: a manager hands a capability away and the next thing it hears is whether a bind reply was consumed, so whether the *device answered* is known in `blk-probe` and nowhere else. It declares `Starting -> Probing -> Active`; a probe that fails leaves the device in `Probing`, which is exactly the state that says "a driver was given this and could not confirm it". **The four missing rungs.** Step 2 (mark degraded) is the manager's, on the principle that *a device the manager did not ask for is a device whose driver stopped without saying so* — nothing in the bind protocol returns a capability, so an arrival is always a driver that ended. It does not know how far that driver got, so it walks the bound states and keeps the one the kernel accepts; every wrong attempt is refused and records nothing, and the one that lands is by construction the device's real state. Step 3 is `capture_crash_dump`, which needed a non-destructive `EventRing::tail` — the ring has exactly one consumer and a dump that *drained* records to describe them would delete the evidence it was summarising — and filters the tail by the dead host's **correlation id**, because a boot interleaves several things and the last eight records before a crash are mostly somebody else's. Step 4 is a **dependency edge in the graph**: `DeviceNode.dependents` holds endpoints, the kernel fills in a `ServiceNotice` and sends it, and both the delivered and the undeliverable counts go into the record — a dependent that never learns its device is gone waits on it for ever. Step 5 is `DeviceResetter`, the third seam of the `DmaMapper` shape, where "per class" actually lands: a virtio transport is reset by writing zero to `Status` and **reading it back**, and a PCI function is a refusal, because an `Ok` from a resetter that touched nothing would have the next rung taken on a false premise. Step 7 is `FailurePolicy` — restore, disable, fallback, quarantine — and quarantine is *enforced* rather than advertised: `reclaim_devices` does not hand a quarantined capability back, so nothing can bind the device again, not because a manager chooses not to but because it has nothing to bind. **Read back from the records, on both ports:** *"4 contained crashes, each contained and dumped (18 trace records captured with them), 4 devices marked degraded by their manager, 4 dependent services told, 4 resets attempted, 4 reclaim-and-rebinds that recovered 92 frames, and 1 supervisor that spent its budget, gave up, and quarantined the device. 16 lifecycle transitions were recorded and every one of them followed the last, so the states join up end to end rather than merely each being plausible."* That last clause is the `unmatched_revokes` idea applied to lifecycles: counting transitions proves nothing, and only checking that each `from` is where the previous one left *that device* catches an impossible order. **Verified:** 47/47 Bazel tests (1 new), 333 kcore unit tests (33 new), lint/fmt clean, exit 33 on all five ports. **ABI:** five new `EventKind`s (25-29) and `KError::NotSupported` (14), both append-only; `SyscallNumber::DriverLifecycle` = 29. **v0 deviations:** the manager's bound-state walk stands in for a *read my device's recorded state* call, which is the honest fix; `FailureAction::Fallback` is host-tested and not exercised on hardware, because this tree has one driver image per class — a binding-manifest concern; `ResetPolicy` and `IsolationPolicy` are chosen at the call site rather than read per device from a manifest; the x86-64 port answers `DriverLifecycle` with `ENOSYS`, having no ring-3 manager to declare anything. | A syscall for a manager to read a device's recorded lifecycle state, retiring the bound-state walk; per-device reset and isolation policy read from a binding manifest — which landed in D130, so what remains is the fields and the call sites reading them — and a second driver image per class, without which a fallback cannot be exercised however completely the policy is expressed. |
| D129 | **A class contract with all ten of its elements, a second class to prove the shape, a write path, and a conformance suite that is not the ABI test (M90).** `block_driver.isl` was two structs — a read request and its reply — which is a *message pair*, not a contract. `docs/drivers/01` ("Driver Class Contracts") lists ten things a contract defines, and the difference is what a second implementation can be held to: two structs say what the bytes look like; a contract says which methods must exist, what may be absent and how a driver says so, who owns a buffer while a call is in flight, what a reset leaves behind, and which errors a client is allowed to see. All ten are now there — a `protocol` with required and optional methods and server-initiated events, `BufferOwnership`, `BlockDmaRules`, `BlockPowerState`, a **closed** `BlockError` set, reset behaviour stated where the method is, `BlockTracePoint`, and `VendorNamespace`. **The kernel change that made methods possible.** A receiver could not learn which method it had been called with: the header carried `method_id` and delivery dropped it, invisible while every service here had exactly one method — a block driver could only ever be a block *reader*, because reading was the only thing a request could mean. `ChannelRecv` and `ChannelReplyRecv` now write the arrived ordinal back into the descriptor in place, the same symmetry `inline_ptr` already had. **A write path, checked from outside the machine.** `tessera-virtio` gains `write_write_request`, whose one difference from a read is that the data descriptor is **not** marked device-writable — a write whose buffer is writable hands the device permission to scribble on the driver's data and still reports success, which is why the direction lives in the encoder rather than in a flags argument at the call site, and why a host test compares the two chains bit for bit. The client writes `TESSERAW` to sector 2, reads it back off the medium, and the **boot test then reads the disk image after QEMU exits** — everything else in the run is the system agreeing with itself, and only the file on disk can contradict a driver and a client that agreed about a write that never landed. **A second class.** `network_driver.isl` is the same ten elements in the same order, and the places it *differs* are the finding: events are the data path here rather than the exception path, buffer ownership is per method rather than per contract because a received frame is `TRANSFERRED` and a block write is not, and the error set is not the block set with words changed — `NO_MEDIUM` becomes `LINK_DOWN`, and a link that is down still accepts configuration. The seven elements that generalised unchanged are the evidence the first contract was a class contract rather than one device's interface written out. **The conformance suite is a different artefact from the ABI tests, and neither implies the other**: a driver can encode every struct perfectly and violate every rule, or obey every rule while disagreeing about a field offset. `//api/class-conformance` reads a **transcript** rather than calling a driver, which is what lets the same rules run in the two places that cannot share a caller — host tests drive it with transcripts no real driver would produce on demand (an advertised method that refuses, a vendor ordinal answered with nothing negotiated, a reset that comes back suspended), and a real ring-3 client feeds it what it actually observed at boot. It reports `unchecked` separately from `passed`, so a transcript that skipped a method reports the clause as unreached rather than satisfied — the failure mode a conformance suite exists to prevent rather than to have. One checker serves both classes by taking a `ClassSpec`, which makes "the shape generalises" something the code demonstrates rather than something the comments claim. **Vendor namespaces are enforced, not documented**: ordinals at or above `0x8000_0000` belong to a declared `(vendor, namespace, version)`, the driver declares none and answers `PROTOCOL` to every one of them, and the suite checks it — without that refusal a vendor method is reachable by anyone who guesses an ordinal, which makes it a public interface nobody wrote down. **Verified:** 49/49 Bazel tests (2 new), 334 kcore unit tests, lint/fmt clean, exit 33 on all five ports; the live driver passed every conformance rule and the written sector is in the disk image. **ABI:** `block_driver.isl` gains seven types and a protocol, all additions — `BlockReadRequest`/`BlockReadReply` are byte-identical, because a class contract that broke the wire two programs already speak to look tidier would be changing an ABI for no reason. `ChannelMsgArgs` is unchanged in layout; only the receive direction's behaviour is new. **v0 deviations:** ISL's dispatch codegen is deferred (D69), so method ordinals are named constants in the driver and the client rather than generated — a wrong number there is a driver serving a different method than the one it was asked for, which is why they are constants and not literals at the match arms; the network contract is declared and its ABI is conformance-gated, but no network driver implements it yet, so its class-conformance rules are exercised only by host transcripts; `Discard`, `Suspend`/`Resume` and the out-of-line buffer modes are declared and unimplemented; and a vendor extension is proven only by its refusal, since no driver in this tree declares a namespace. | An ISL-generated dispatch stub, retiring the hand-written ordinal constants; a network driver implementing its own contract, so the second class is exercised by a driver rather than by transcripts. |
| D130 | **Binding is a decision against a manifest, and a driver is told where its device's structures are (M91).** Two things closed here, and the second is D126's open item. **Binding had one input and one output.** The manager matched on device class and the answer was a capability, which is a lookup rather than a binding decision — the difference shows the moment a machine has two devices of one class that must not run the same driver, a driver whose signature nobody trusts, or a device whose firmware is too old for the driver that would otherwise claim it. `docs/drivers/01` lists ten inputs; a class map can express one. `//api/binding` is the manifest and its matcher, and the design turns on a division the spec does not spell out: **facts about the device** (class, vendor, product, revision, bus, firmware) come from enumeration and a manager only observes them, while **policy about the driver** (security domain, power domain, signature, contract version, product policy) comes from the manifest — a system that read the second kind from the device would be letting the device decide what it is allowed to do. `SystemPolicy` is the third thing neither is: what this installation permits, against which a manifest entry is itself checked, so an entry that satisfies its own claims can still be refused. **Specificity beats order** — entries are scored by how many optional fields they constrain, so a catch-all class entry cannot shadow a device-specific one and a manifest's meaning does not depend on line order. **A refused best match does not fall through** to a weaker one, which would silently bind a device to a less specific driver because the right one was unsigned. And a refusal is an enum, not a `None`: nothing matched, the signature is untrusted, and an operator disabled it are three administrative situations with three different fixes, and reporting them identically would leave all three looking like missing hardware. `BindReply` v2 carries the outputs a manifest decides — required services, update channel, security and power domain, contract version — and the driver **checks them**, so a manifest that stopped filling them in breaks something. Three of `docs/drivers/01`'s six outputs need no field at all: the host identity, the granted capabilities and the resource leases are produced by the transfer itself. **The revision was being shifted off the end.** `tessera_pci::Function` computed `class_code = class >> 8` and discarded the register's low byte, which is the hardware revision — a binding input in its own right, since a driver may support a device from revision 3 onward and a manifest that could not name it would have to claim every revision or none. **Telling a driver where its structures are (D126's open item).** A virtio-pci function does not say where its controls live in any register it exposes: it says so in **config space**, one vendor capability per structure, and config space is not per-device — no capability to it can be handed out, which is why D114 put PCI enumeration in the kernel. So a driver granted exactly the right window still had no way to find anything in it, and D126 closed by admitting the check read at an offset *it* knew and the driver did not. The graph now holds a `DeviceLayout` per device and `DeviceInfoRecord` v2 reports it — as **offsets within the granted window**, never absolute addresses, because the first is usable by a process that mapped a capability and the second is a fact about the machine no driver should be given. `layout_valid` is reported rather than inferred from a zero offset: offset zero is where the common configuration structure actually sits on these machines, and a driver that read zero as "absent" would refuse to drive exactly the devices that work. The proof: *"the manager matched this device against a binding manifest (class 0x01 from the graph, vendor 0x1af4, revision 0, on PCI — five inputs, not one) ... and then found its device's common configuration structure at offset 0x0, which the kernel read out of config space the driver cannot reach and reported to it: the driver wrote a feature selector there and read it back."* Writing and reading back is the smallest thing that distinguishes the common configuration structure from somewhere else in the right BAR. **A latent bug this surfaced.** `BindReply` grew from 24 bytes to 48 and every program's bind buffer was 32, so the reply was truncated on the wire and the read-back overran the buffer. It failed loudly and immediately; a reply that had grown by four bytes instead of twenty-four would have fitted, been silently truncated, and handed every driver a plausible set of zeroed outputs. **Verified:** 50/50 Bazel tests (1 new), 335 kcore unit tests (2 new), 12 binding tests, lint/fmt clean, exit 33 on all five ports. **ABI:** `DeviceInfoRecord` v1→v2 (40→72 bytes) and `BindReply` v1→v2 (24→48); `tessera_pci::Function` gains `revision`. Both records are kernel- or manager-produced and consumed only in this tree, so the versions move together. **v0 deviations:** the manifest is a compiled-in array, because the thing that should deliver it is a configuration service reading a signed package and this system has no filesystem — the manager consults a manifest rather than deciding for itself, and where it comes from is one substitution away; driver signatures are constants compared against a trusted list rather than verified cryptography, which is a field and not yet a control; `device_config` is recorded but always zero, since no device on these machines publishes that capability; and the layout is resolved on AArch64 only, the only port that walks virtio-pci capabilities. | A configuration service delivering the manifest as signed data, retiring the compiled-in array; real signature verification behind `SystemPolicy::trusted_signatures`; and the RISC-V port resolving the same layout, which needs its virtio-pci capability walk. |
| D131 | **A message can carry memory it does not contain — out-of-line grants, transfer mode (M92).** D19 said it plainly: a channel message is *"an inline byte payload (≤ 256 B) and a small transferred-handle set"*, and `block_driver.isl` said what that costs — *"full-sector transfer needs a shared-memory grant, deferred"* — while truncating a 512-byte sector to 64. Every class contract past `block` was blocked on the same missing thing: network frames, NVMe scatter-gather, framebuffers, sound rings. Now a client creates a **memory object**, fills a whole sector, transfers the buffer to the driver, gets it back, and the boot check reads all 512 bytes out of the disk image after QEMU exits. **The wire needed no new representation, and that is the design.** `docs/kernel/04` defines an out-of-line reference as *"a memory-object handle with an ownership mode declared per field"* — handles already travel in the message's handle vector, so what was missing was the object, not the transport. `HandleTransfer.reserved` became `mode` (`ChannelMsgArgs` v3 → v4, a flag day across five ports and four ring-3 programs): version 3 required that word to be zero, so every descriptor a v3 producer ever emitted **is** a v4 one asking for `TRANSFER` — the field changed meaning without changing a byte, which is what reserving it was for. v3 is still refused, because "identical today" is a property of this kernel's mode set rather than of the format. **Transfer, and not share, for a reason the ports impose.** Share needs `HandleTable::duplicate`, which retains in an `ObjectTable` — and three of the five ports have none, fabricating object ids directly; handing them an empty table would alias the fabricated ids rather than track them. Transfer touches no object table at all, so it works on every port today. `SHARE` is defined in the ABI and refused with `NotSupported`, checked **before** the handle leaves the sender's table: `take_narrowed` cannot be undone, so a mode discovered unsupported afterwards would leave the capability belonging to nobody. **Ownership, not sharing, is what makes the payload checkable.** A transferred object's frames are revoked from the sender before the message is delivered, so the receiver validates memory the sender cannot rewrite — a time-of-check race closed by construction rather than by discipline. The same revocation is what lets the driver map every request's buffer at one fixed address: the object leaves with the reply, so the address is free for the next one, and a second mapping over an occupied address would be refused. Also fixed here, from the same spec paragraph: an over-limit message is now **rejected** rather than truncated (`docs/kernel/04`: *"Messages exceeding a limit are rejected at send with a protocol error rather than truncated"*) — a 300-byte request silently becoming 256 gets a reply to a request nobody made. And a memory object outlives its creator's handle table, which forgets its handles on drop by design, so process teardown sweeps the objects an owner still held. **What is deliberately still open**: share and snapshot modes (above); ~~zero-copy — the driver copies between its `dma_alloc` page and the grant~~ (done, D133: the driver attaches the object to the device and never maps it); partial mappings (offset + length), because a range that can be unmapped piecewise is one whose revocation is no longer all-or-nothing; ~~**releasing a grant before exit**~~ (done, D134); x86-64, whose dispatcher is handed `NoFrames` and answers `MemoryCreate`/`MemoryMap` with `ENOSYS` rather than failing obscurely on the first frame it asks for. | ~~Closed when a driver can be handed a memory object as a DMA target without copying through a `dma_alloc` page~~ (done, D133); still open until share mode lands on a port that refcounts object ids. |
| D132 | **What the schema declares about a handle now reaches the code built from it, and two programs stopped copying the contract by hand (M93).** `docs/api/03` says a `handle<type, rights>` field is *"a handle whose object type and minimum rights mask are **part of the type**"*, that out-of-line fields *"declare an ownership mode"*, and that *"Handles are indexed references into the message's handle vector … Payload bytes never contain handle values"*. None of it survived compilation: lowering produced a bare `Handle` variant, the object type and rights and mode were dropped, and `WireError::HandleIndexOutOfRange` had **no producer anywhere in the tree**. The cost was visible in the two programs D131 had just written — 19 hand-written method ordinals that `BlockDevice::*` already generated, and `0x1 \| 0x2 \| 0x4 \| 0x80` computed in each of them for a mask the contract knows. **The compiler's rights catalog gained its bit values**, which is what lets a handle field emit its own mask, and a test now compiles `handle_abi.isl` and checks its `bits Rights` against that catalog name for name and value for value — the first thing able to notice two of D16's three copies disagreeing. **The generated dispatch found a bug the hand-written one was free to have.** The contract pairs `Discard` with a `BlockWriteRequest`; the host decoded a `BlockControlRequest` for it and the client sent one, so both were wrong in the same way and the conformance suite passed. Ordinal and payload type now come from one place, so there is nowhere left to write a different answer. **A positional installed-handle report, first, because the index model is unsound without it.** `install_transferred_handles` used to append only successes, so a sender that attached a buffer and an endpoint whose endpoint was dropped produced a report of length one — leaving the receiver's slot 1 holding whatever the *previous* message left there, and a field naming index 1 resolving to a stale handle number rather than to an error. Entry *i* is now the sender's *i*th descriptor or `HANDLE_NOT_INSTALLED`, which is `u32::MAX` rather than 0 because 0 is the first handle a fresh table hands out. **The bound survives an envelope**: a table is the default shape for a service protocol, so a sub-reader that lost it would skip the check on exactly the fields that need it. The boot verdict is unchanged and the sector-3 comparison still holds byte for byte, which is the claim — the wire grew a field and the behaviour did not move. **What is deliberately not built**: the receiver-side *rights check* the spec describes, which needs `HandleQueryRights` per arriving handle — the generated constant is what a receiver checks against, and making the check automatic is a separate step; index-versus-raw-handle as a property of the **decode call** rather than of the type, when the compiler could derive it from protocol reachability (a syscall argument carries a raw handle because the kernel resolves it against the caller's own table; a message payload carries an index because the kernel translated at transfer — both true, and nothing in the bytes says which); an ownership mode on a `vector`/`string` field, which is validated and still dropped at lowering because no contract has one; and `device-manager`/`blk-probe`, which speak `driver_bind.isl` and are a separate adoption. | Closed when a receiver rejects a handle carrying fewer rights than its field declares, and when the compiler — not the decode call — decides whether a handle field is an index. |
| D133 | **A device reaches a client's buffer directly, and stops reaching it on command (M94).** D131 named its own exit criterion — *"a driver can be handed a memory object as a DMA target **without copying through a `dma_alloc` page**"* — and the driver was copying, because a grant's frames are ordinary anonymous memory no `DmaAlloc` had made addressable to the device. That copy is what kept the out-of-line path a demonstration: every class past `block` (network frames, scatter-gather, framebuffers, sound rings) exists to move bytes a CPU never touches. **`DmaAttach` makes an object a device can reach, and the strongest form of the proof is what the driver no longer does.** `device-host` does not map the client's buffer at all — no `MemoryMap`, no fixed grant VA, no `copy_from_slice` — it attaches the object and puts the returned device address straight into the virtqueue's data descriptor. A driver holding no mapping of a buffer cannot have copied it, and the boot check still reads all 512 bytes of sector 3 out of the disk image and compares them byte for byte. **The dangerous half is detaching, and it happens four ways.** A translation lives in the IOMMU and outlives the process; the frames do not. So the object is detached when it is transferred away (the driver never calls detach and cannot forget to), when it is destroyed, when its owner exits, and — the one that starts at the device rather than the object — when the device's lease ends, where the record is cleared **without** unmapping because the translations are already gone and the address range belongs to whoever leases next. `MemoryTable::destroy` refuses outright while an object is attached and says so in an event: it has no mapper and cannot detach, and leaking frames is the lesser harm next to handing a device's live target to the next allocation. **`DmaMapper` gained `unmap`, which its own docs argued against.** `end_lease` is all-or-nothing because *"which of these addresses is the driver still entitled to"* is not a question a dead driver can answer — but that is revocation *imposed* on a corpse, and detaching is a live driver naming a range it still knows about. It returns a `Result` where `end_lease` cannot, because there is a caller here that must not mistake a failure for "the device can no longer reach that memory". **The SMMU boot check proves the hardware half**: a memory object is attached to a real device, the device DMAs into it, it is detached, and the same address the device had just used raises a translation fault for that stream. Bookkeeping alone would have agreed either way. **What is deliberately not built**: scatter-gather — one contiguous IOVA run per object, so a device gets one address, and a descriptor per frame needs a vector return; a **multi-page object on a machine with no IOMMU**, refused rather than partly attached, because physical frames are not contiguous and returning the first page's address would name memory that runs off the end of it; ~~**address reuse after a detach**~~ for the *same* object (done, D134); still never for a different one, so a driver's distinct buffers remain bounded by its aperture; and `Rights::MAP` doing double duty as "may be placed in an address space" and "may be reached by a device", which wants a distinct right and therefore a rights-catalog change. | ~~Closed when a detached address can be safely reissued within a lease~~ (done, D134, for the object that already held it); still open until a device can be given a scatter-gather list rather than one contiguous run. |
| D134 | **A resident driver stops running out of things (M95).** The previous three milestones built a mechanism a driver could not use for long. Two bounds, both recorded as open and both **reachable in tests that already existed**: a program could free a memory object only by dying or by handing it on, because `HandleClose` was not a dispatched syscall — it was x86-local, on a handler no other port uses — so a service that made a buffer per request got eight of them; and every attach spent a device address that was never reissued, on a machine whose whole lease is **two pages**, one of which `dma_alloc` had already taken. The second attach failed and the driver was finished. **Closing a handle without an object table.** `HandleTable::drop_handle` clears the slot, bumps the generation and returns the object, touching no `ObjectTable` — the shape `reclaim` already uses, and its argument carries: the table counts *references*, and three of the five ports have none because they fabricate object ids rather than allocate them. What a close actually needs is "does this process still hold the object", which is `holds`, per-process and table-free. The dispatched arm then follows the capability out exactly as the transfer path does — a device's register window, DMA lease and interrupt route all end with it — plus a fourth case the transfer path cannot have: a memory object the caller **owns** is detached and its frames released. Ownership decides it and must: a *receiver* closing a handle to a buffer it was lent must not free the sender's memory, and ownership is the single-valued fact that tells the two apart. x86-64 keeps its own close path; it has the object table the shared one deliberately avoids, and giving it second semantics to match a table it does not need would be the second dispatcher D79 forbids. **An address is reissued for the memory it already named.** An object remembers the `(device, address)` it was last attached at, and re-attaching to the same device lands there again. This does not weaken the never-reuse rule, because that rule is about naming *different* memory: a device may hold an address in a descriptor ring the kernel cannot see, and reissuing it for something else would turn a stale descriptor into a write to whatever now occupies it. An object's frames are fixed when it is created, so a stale descriptor here resolves to the same buffer with the same owner — not a capability escape, which is the line that matters. The trade is stated rather than hidden: a stale descriptor that would have faulted now writes into the buffer it named, a loud failure exchanged for a quiet one, and worth it against a driver that stops after two buffers. The remembered address is dropped when the device's lease ends, because the whole range then belongs to whoever leases next. **Both halves are proven by the machine.** The SMMU check runs six attach/detach rounds at one address through a two-page lease — an aperture that would have been spent on the second round — and the device still reaches the object on the last one. The ring-3 client closes its grant when it is done, and the exit sweep now reports **zero** frames still owned where it reported one: the difference between memory returned when a program says so and memory returned when a program dies. **What is deliberately still open**: an address is never reissued to a *different* object, so a driver's distinct buffers stay bounded by its aperture — a property of its design rather than of its uptime, which is the right shape; and the dispatched close reports nothing about what it destroyed, because the refcount that answered that on x86-64 is the object table three ports do not have. | Closed when a port other than x86-64 carries an object table, at which point the two close paths converge and a close can say what it destroyed. |
| D135 | **The first departure nobody chose — device removal (M96, partial).** Every departure the tree knew was voluntary or posthumous: a capability left because its holder handed it on, closed it, or died. There was no path where the *kernel* takes a live capability from a living, unwilling holder — and that is what removing a device is. `Removed` had existed as a terminal lifecycle state with a full transition table since D128 and **nothing could ever reach it**, because no mechanism performed a removal. **`Executive::remove_device` is `reclaim_devices` with the target inverted**: that one takes every device from one process, this takes one device from every process. Same order and for the same reasons — the DMA lease and the interrupt route end *before* a single handle moves, because a device that has been pulled must stop translating and stop interrupting whatever else succeeds; then every holder's handles go, taken with `reclaim`, which requires no `TRANSFER` precisely because this is the kernel taking a capability back rather than a process handing one on; then each holder's register window is unmapped; and only then does the node leave the graph. **Dropping the node last is what makes the capability invalid rather than merely unheld**: every syscall that reaches a device resolves it through the graph — `MapDevice`, `DmaAlloc`, `DmaAttach`, `DeviceInfo`, `IrqComplete`, `DriverLifecycle` — so all of them refuse afterwards and not one had to learn a new rule. Three new departure reasons, because *removed* is neither of the two answers that existed, and a record that could not say which one it was would be answering the wrong question. An attached memory object is detached by the removal too: the device is gone, and an attachment record that outlived it would make a later detach unmap into a lease that no longer exists. **Lease expiry is a third caller of the teardown, not a second teardown.** A lease gains a deadline in scheduler ticks — the only monotonic source the kernel has, and a *liveness* bound rather than a wall clock: it answers "is the holder still asking for this". `expire_leases` ends everything past its deadline through the same `end_one_lease` a departure uses, with `LeaseEndReason::Expired`, so a lease that expires leaves the machine in exactly the state a lease that was given up leaves it in. A lease with no deadline never expires, because every driver that predates this has one and giving them all a lifetime they never agreed to would be the mechanism breaking its own users on the way in. **The class contracts gained the event and the error**: `OnDeviceGone` on block (22) and network (23), and `REMOVED` appended to both error sets — not `DEGRADED`, and the difference is what a client does next, since degraded invites a retry and removed forbids one. **Three things the first pass missed, now closed.** `Removed` is finally *recorded*: `remove_device` emits its event but never declared the transition, so the terminal state the framework has described since D128 still had nothing that reached it — it now opens from whatever was last recorded, which is a legal edge from every live state, and `Discovered` when nothing was. **Dependents are told**, through the same `ServiceNotice` the crash ladder uses and before the node is dropped, because the graph is where the dependency edges live and removing the node takes them with it; a dependent that had to tell "the driver failed" from "the device left" by which mechanism reached it would be learning the kernel's internals rather than its own situation. And **`DmaRenew` exists**, so a lease deadline is reachable from ring 3 — expiry detects the absence of a syscall, and until there was one to be absent it detected nothing. **And the driver is woken.** A driver parked waiting for an interrupt from a device that has left waits for something that can no longer happen — the one failure a removal creates that correct bookkeeping does not fix, because the driver is not running to observe any of it. Removal signals its port *after* the route teardown, not before: ending a route unbinds the port and an unbound binding takes any event pending on it, so a wake delivered first is thrown away by the cleanup that follows. It arrives on its own signal, and **the number matters** — a port's signals are one namespace, one being an interrupt and two a channel message, so a removal numbered two reaches a resident server as a client request and is answered as one. That is not hypothetical: it is what the first attempt did, and the ring-3 host boot caught it. `device-host` now answers whoever is waiting with `REMOVED` and stops. **The end-to-end proof is built, and the two blockers are worth writing down.** The kernel-side check exists (`pci_removal_check`: hold the function, poll its config space, remove on all-ones, verify the graph forgot it and the holder was stripped without being consulted) and it arms only on a machine that has a PCI-to-PCI bridge — discovered rather than declared, because that bridge is exactly what makes a device removable on this bus. The harness exists too (`tools/qemu/hotplug_boot_aarch64.sh`), and is the first script here that talks to QEMU *while it runs*: removal is not a state a machine can be booted into, it is an event, and an event needs somebody outside to cause it. Two blockers were found and one remains. QEMU refuses `device_del` on the root bus — *"Bus 'pcie.0' does not support hotplugging"* — so the victim must sit behind a `pcie-root-port`, and nothing behind a bridge was reachable: `-kernel` boots with no firmware, so a root port's bus numbers and forwarding window were never programmed. **That is fixed** — the enumerator now configures bridges and a virtio-blk device behind a root port is driven through a window it assigned. The check also failed silently, which turned out to be its own setup rebuilding the `KCORE_PROCESSES` static **on the stack**: a table is sixteen processes each carrying a 1024-entry handle table, and the boot stack does not have a couple of hundred kilobytes to spare, so it overflowed into a fault with no stack left to report from. It now arms correctly on the function behind the port. The third finding was that **PCIe hotplug is a request, not an event**: the root port raises an eject and waits for the guest to acknowledge through the slot control register, and a guest that never answers is a guest the device never leaves — a second `device_del` answers *"already in the process of unplug"* rather than forcing it. So the guest half is built: find the port's PCI Express capability, watch slot status for the attention-button and presence-changed bits, then clear them (write-one-to-clear, so writing them back is what clears them) and turn the power controller and indicator off, which is how a guest says it has finished and the slot may be de-energized. **`//tools/qemu:hotplug_boot_aarch64_test` now passes**: a `virtio-blk-pci` device behind a `pcie-root-port` is bound and read, removed over QMP while the machine runs, the eject answered, and the function stops responding — at which point the kernel takes the capability from a holder that never asked, and the graph forgets the device so every syscall that reaches one refuses. It reframes the claim honestly: what this proves is an **eject the guest acknowledged**, because on this bus there is no such thing as a surprise removal. | ~~Closed when a device pulled out of a running QEMU is observed by the kernel, its dependents told, and the driver woken to answer its caller~~ (done). Still open until a **ring-3 driver is woken by a real removal on a machine that can perform one**: the AArch64 host serves virtio-mmio devices, which cannot be unplugged, and the PCIe check holds its device in the kernel. |
| D136 | **The resource graph became a tree (M97).** `DeviceTable` was eight independent nodes: nothing in it could say that one device sits behind another, which was sufficient while every device was a leaf and stopped being sufficient the moment a controller had children. `docs/drivers/01` ("Bus Topology And Data Paths") opens by saying bus controllers *are* drivers whose children are drivers, and none of what follows was expressible because there was no edge to hang it on. **Four things landed, and the first was a latent defect the second level exposed.** (1) *Enumeration that survives a second level.* `configure_bridge` wrote `subordinate = secondary`, and its own comment said what that cost: "a bridge behind a bridge would need it widened after the far side had been walked." It has to be widened **before** the descent, not after — a bridge claiming only its secondary forwards no configuration cycle to its grandchildren, so the walk would record an empty bus rather than fail, which is indistinguishable from a switch with nothing plugged in. So the bound goes in wide (the host's last bus), the far side is walked, and it is narrowed to what was found; narrowing matters as much as widening, because a bridge left claiming its *sibling's* buses is a fabric that decodes ambiguously. Both halves are guarded by tests that fail for the omission of exactly one of them. The walk had **no bridge coverage at all** until now — `configure_bridge` was debugged on hardware — so the mock had to start enforcing forwarding: a config cycle reaches a bus only if every bridge on the path from the root claims it, without which the numbering could be arbitrarily wrong and every test would still pass. (2) *One edge, pointing up.* `DeviceNode.parent`, with children derived by scanning the eight-slot pool — no child array, so there is no second copy of the relationship to disagree and no `MAX_CHILDREN` to be wrong about. `set_parent` refuses an absent parent, a self-parent and any edge closing a cycle, because "everything below this" has to terminate on a path where not finishing is a hang with the hardware already gone. (3) *Removal takes the subtree*, leaves first. Not a second teardown: `remove_device` became a bounded iterative walk over the one that existed, so each node's lease, route, window, dependents and handles die exactly as they did. Leaves first is load-bearing rather than tidy — no removal runs against a parent whose descendants are live, and no child is left pointing at an emptied slot. (4) *`Rights::DERIVE` acquired its first user.* It had been in the catalog since the catalog was written and nothing had needed it. `DeviceChild` (syscall 35) answers **from the graph's own edges**: a controller names an index, never an id, so there is no set of permitted devices anybody maintains and therefore none to get wrong. The installed handle is the parent's rights **less `DERIVE`** — a controller may hand out its children, a child may hand out nothing — so authority attenuates one level per level of tree instead of the whole subtree being reachable from anywhere in it. Holding a bus is deliberately **not** authority to broker it, which is why this is a right rather than an implication of `MAP`. `KError::InvalidArgument` was appended rather than folding an impossible graph into `Protocol` (documented as a *message*-protocol violation) or `BadHandle` (an absent parent is a race worth retrying; a cycle is a caller that will ask forever). **The proof is settled by hardware, and one line of it is incidental and the strongest.** The machine became `pcie-root-port → x3130-upstream → xio3130-downstream → virtio-blk-pci`, and `virtio-pci: OK — sector 0 read` now names a function on **bus 3** that the old numbering could not have reached at all. Then `device_del` on the *switch* — which unlike a root port can be pulled, because it sits in a slot that hotplugs — and QEMU's delete recurses into child buses, so three functions stop answering at once. One `remove_device` call takes three nodes; the root port above them survives, and its holder still holds it, which no count of removed nodes would have revealed. | Closed when the graph holds parent/child edges, a capability scoped to a subtree cannot name outside it, and a bus pulled from a running machine takes its subtree with it ~~(done)~~. ~~Still open until a ring-3 bus controller derives its children~~ (done, D137). |
| D137 | **A ring-3 manager holds a bus and derives what is on it (M97, closing).** D136 left `DeviceChild` exercised only from host tests, because no machine had both halves — the one with a real bridge held its devices in the kernel, and the ones running ring-3 drivers had flat topologies. Putting the `pci-bind` machine's `virtio-blk-pci` behind a `pcie-root-port` gave it both, and the manager stopped being handed its inventory: the kernel installs the **bridge** in its handle table and nothing else, so a driver bound to the device at all holds a capability that can only have come out of the graph's parent/child edges. There is no other way for one to exist, which is why this needs no report from the manager to be believed. **The rights rule changed, and the ring-3 half is what showed the first one was wrong.** D136 narrowed a derived handle to *the parent's rights less `DERIVE`*, which reads as conservative and is not: a bus and the devices on it are different objects wanting different authority, so a root port would have had to be granted `MAP` — authority over its own register window, which nothing should want — purely so the endpoints below it could be mapped by their drivers. What a child handle carries is now the **graph's own record for the child** (`rights_of_object`, already documented as *what a kernel-originated hand-out of this device carries*), and the parent's `DERIVE` is the gate on whether the kernel hands one out rather than a ceiling on what it is worth. `DERIVE` is also **carried down** now: stopping at one level would make the deepest thing a bus controller could reach its immediate children, which on the very topology D136 added is a switch's upstream port and nothing beyond it. Containment is the **edge** — `DeviceChild` walks down from what the caller holds and can name nothing off that subtree — and a driver handed a device over a channel brokers nothing because it was never given `DERIVE`. The manager needed no flag telling it which kind of machine it is on: it asks each granted handle what is behind it, a bus expands into the devices on it, and a leaf stands for itself. **Two defects fell out of moving one function onto bus 1.** A PCIe stream id *is* the requester id, so the device's stream went from 0x08 to 0x100 and the SMMU's 32-entry stream table refused it — a table sized by the devices on bus 0 rather than by the bus numbers, surfacing as a driver that cannot be given DMA and saying nothing about buses. Growing it to 1024 entries then broke the *other* SMMU check, because a linear stream table must be aligned to its own size and `alloc_contiguous` promises an unbroken run and nothing about where it starts: a misaligned base has the unit read the table from a lower address, find no valid entry for any stream, and abort every transaction — DMA that silently does not land, with a perfectly well-formed table sitting in memory. | Closed: a ring-3 manager is granted a bus, derives the device behind it, and binds it — with its own boot marker, because a manager that fell back to being handed its inventory would bind exactly as well and prove one less thing. |
| D138 | **A controller with per-child queue separation (M98, partial).** `docs/drivers/01` ("Bus Topology And Data Paths") says that where the controller hardware separates per-child queues, a child's queue is mapped straight to the child and a transfer crosses no extra process. The tree had no multiqueue device of any kind: `Blk::init` configured queue 0 and `Net::init` queues 0 and 1, both hardwired. **The device was chosen twice, and the first choice was wrong for a reason worth recording.** virtio-net multiqueue is the example the doc reaches for, and it cannot be driven here: `mq=on` derives its queue-pair count from the netdev, and a `user` netdev has exactly one queue — so the device offers one pair, the driver asks for two, and the bring-up refuses. Multiqueue **virtio-blk** needs no netdev, has no control queue, and gets its count from a plain `num-queues=` on a file-backed drive; it is also the closer analogue of the doc's own "multi-queue NVMe namespaces". `num_queues` is a 16-bit field at byte 34, so it is the *high* half of the aligned word at 32 — reading the low half gets `writeback` and an unused byte, which on a device with write-back caching is 1, a perfectly plausible queue count and the reason that extraction is a tested function rather than a comment. **`page-per-vq=on` is what the claim actually rests on, and the check fails rather than passes without it.** Rings are per queue whatever the device does, but a doorbell is a register write, page granularity is the unit of granting, and two queues sharing a doorbell page cannot be handed to different processes however separate their rings are. The multiplier is 4 by default, which puts every queue's doorbell in one page; asking for a page per queue makes it 4096. So the boot check reports both doorbell offsets and **exits Failure when they share a page** — a device whose queues all work and none of which could be delegated is the outcome this milestone exists to distinguish. Proven on hardware: two of two request queues in service, sector 0 read on **queue 1** with queue 0's used ring still empty (the transfer went where it was posted and nowhere else), doorbells at 0x0 and 0x1000. One incidental finding: a `pcie-root-port`'s 1 MiB window (D136) is too small for a `page-per-vq` function's BAR, so the multiqueue device sits on the root complex — the bridge window is carved before the far side is scanned, so its size is chosen without knowing what is behind it, and sizing it to its children needs a probing pass over a bus that does not answer yet. | Closed for the controller's half: a device with per-child queue separation is brought up, a transfer is served on a queue other than queue 0 without touching queue 0, and the queues' doorbells are on separate pages. ~~Still open until a ring-3 child process holds one of those queues and drives it~~ (done, D139). Also deferred, as scoped: per-queue MSI-X completion, which needs MSI-X delivery proven for its own sake first (`msix_configure_check` still says the device sending one is unproven). |
| D139 | **A ring-3 child holds one queue and drives it (M98, closing).** D138 proved the *hardware* separates queues; this proves the *system* hands one over. And the child process was left in the table on the way out: the process table is shared across every check in the boot and a leftover process still owns a thread index and a handle table, so the *crash-recovery ladder three checks later* counted the wrong incarnations and reported a driver that would not die. Neither is visible from the check that causes it. One evidence weakness was caught before it could pass: every sector of the test image begins with the same four-byte tag, so the controller's read of sector 0 and the child's read of sector 1 were indistinguishable at four bytes — the check now compares eight and requires the two to differ, which is what tells "the child's request was served" from "something was served". | Closed: a ring-3 process holding only a controller capability derives a queue, maps its doorbell page, publishes and notifies, and the device serves it — with the child's register-window holding checked to be exactly the one page.
