Bit 62 now separates the two address spaces, and no port's user half reaches it — AArch64's is 2^48, x86-64's 2^47, Sv39's 2^38, the 32-bit ports' 2^32 — so the mask is the identity on every address a driver can hold. **What is NOT proven, and is unchanged:** no shipped driver's DMA discipline is watched here, because the two that use the SDK are `no_main` binaries for other architectures; the harness watches the discipline and not them. | 124 tests pass; each fix reverted on its own fails its own test — the low-16 fold fails `pages_a_megabyte_apart_are_told_apart`, an unchecked `grant` fails `one_address_is_granted_once`, and an unguarded `holds` panics at `range end index 4097 out of range for slice of length 4096` |
| D181 | **The answer for a card that left, tested where a card can leave (Phase 6.12).** The SD boot check's header named a companion script that runs the same driver against an empty slot, and called the pair what makes `NO_MEDIUM` attributable to the card. That script was never committed, and running the machine it describes shows why it could not be: QEMU's `sdhci-pci` reports card-present in an empty slot, so the kernel's present-state probe reads true with no `sd-card` attached and the with-card verdict comes back unchanged. **The driver's medium-gone path was therefore executed by nothing** — the transport core's `NoCard` was covered by a mock, but `sd-host`'s translation of it into the block class's `NO_MEDIUM` sat in a `no_main` AArch64 binary no host test could call, and the tree was green either way. Closed at the tier that can run rather than papered over: the translation moved into `sd_host_medium`, generic over the transport's `Registers` trait so the driver supplies real MMIO and a test supplies a model; and the mock moved out of `kernel/sdhci`'s test module into `//kernel/sdhci-mock`, so the tier that turns an empty slot into `NoCard` and the tier that turns `NoCard` into `NO_MEDIUM` are held to **one** model — a second mock could disagree with the first about when a card is gone and both suites would pass while describing different hardware. `present` and `irq` became public for it, which is register-bit constants and not behaviour. **What is NOT proven:** the join — that the driver reached from ring 3, over a channel, through the kernel's mapping of the controller, answers the same way. That needs a card that can leave, and no emulator here has one; the boot check now says so instead of implying a script that would have. | 132 tests pass; 5 inversions, each failing the test that names its claim — dropping `NoCard` from the read path fails `a_card_that_goes_mid_request_is_no_medium_and_not_an_io_error`, a `check_presence` that acts on nothing fails `a_card_taken_out_between_requests_answers_no_medium`, making every failure `NO_MEDIUM` fails `a_controller_error_is_an_io_error_and_the_card_is_still_there`, restoring `ready` on re-insertion fails `a_card_put_back_is_not_served_until_it_is_identified_again`, and issuing a command with no card fails `a_request_with_no_card_issues_no_command`; one inversion of the shared mock — a card that leaves without clearing the presence bit — fails tests in **both** tiers, which is the one-model claim rather than an assertion of it; `sd_boot_aarch64_test` passes uncached |
| D182 | **The package-coverage gate is blind in one direction under Bazel.** `//:all_srcs` is the hand-written list every tier-0 gate reads through, so a package missing from it is never inspected and every gate still reports success. `//tools/checks:packages_test` closes that, except for the half Bazel cannot answer from inside a test: the runfiles tree a test is handed *is* `//:all_srcs`, so an omitted package contributes no files and cannot be seen. | D183 | **The ports and the kernel binaries have never been linted, behind a comment saying they were.** `.bazelrc` claimed the `--config=lint` aspects "propagate through the kernel platform transition, covering crates that only build for bare metal". They do not: `platform_binary`/`platform_library` expose the transitioned target through their own `binary`/`library` attribute, and an aspect propagates along the attributes *it* declares (`deps`, `crate`), so it stops at the wrapper. Measured, the host config emits 231 clippy outputs across 64 packages and **none** for `karch-x86_64`, `karch-aarch64`, `karch-riscv64`, `karch-riscv32`, `karch-arm32`, `karch-arm-common`, `karch-riscv-common`, `width-conformance`, or any of the five kernel binaries — that is, for essentially every line of `unsafe` in the tree. The `--config=lint-<arch>` configs added beside it reach them, and report **505 findings**: `kernel/kernel` 170, `kernel-aarch64` 232, `kernel-riscv64` 90, `kernel-riscv32` 3, `kernel-arm32` 3, `karch-x86_64` 6, `karch-aarch64` 1 (each figure is the target and its dependencies under that platform). Each property is now a claim key (`claim <group>.<name>`, one per line, from `kcore::verdict::claims`), 113 of them, and the scripts match those instead. | D185 | **The verdict lines were prose on the wire, and now they are not.** Seventy-six format strings exceeded 150 characters, the longest 4,573 — nine paragraphs of argument printed to a serial console on one line. Each is now a message plus its values, with the prose moved verbatim into a comment above the emission, which is where `docs/lifecycle/04` says it belongs; five diagnostics too wide for one line became two, three or four separate messages rather than one wrapped one. `kprint!`/`kprintln!` now carry the envelope `docs/observability/01` mandates — `[<ticks> <module>] `, the module from `module_path!()` at the call site so it cannot drift, the tick from the same clock the event ring uses, read without blocking so the panic path cannot deadlock on it. | D186 | **Test code lived inside the code it tests, and two fixtures shipped with it.** 20,888 lines across 71 source files were `#[cfg(test)] mod tests { … }` bodies — 66% of `kcore::dispatch.rs` alone, which read as a 9,000-line file and was a 3,000-line one. | D188 | **The SDK seam is the interface for three of twelve ring-3 drivers, and what the other nine need is now known rather than guessed.** `usb-hid` is converted: its syscall numbers, its hand-rolled `ChannelMsgArgs` encoding, its `patch_args`/`kernel_u32` volatile helpers and its receive/reply loop are gone, and 509 lines of it are USB and HID. | D190 | **Two more copies unified, and one that only looked like a copy.** `load_user_elf` — reserve writable, copy, narrow to what the segment declared, publish executable pages to the instruction cache — was carried by AArch64 and RISC-V 64 at 96%. It is `kcore::elf::load_into` now, generic over the porting layer, beside the parser it uses; the single real difference between the two copies was the `Machine` they parsed for, which is a parameter. `map_user_word` was carried by three ports at what the similarity table called 100%, and **it was not one function**: two took a `u32` and the third a `u64`, so the three wrote four bytes into the page or eight. It is `boot_checks::map_user_bytes` now, taking the bytes and leaving the width to the caller that knows its register size. `user_transform` diverges the same way and is *not* shared — a rotate over a `u32` and a rotate over a `u64` are two functions with one name — and each port's copy now says so. **What is NOT proven, and was asserted three times before it was checked:** that a high similarity score means two functions are the same. The score in D189's table was the best-matching *pair*, and on `kernel_sections`, `map_user_word` and `user_transform` the best pair agreed while another differed by register width. Compilation caught two of the three; the third was caught by reading. A similarity number is a place to look, not a reason to merge. | The 0.70–0.90 band taken one function at a time, each divergence read before the copies are joined rather than after the compiler objects. |
| D191 | **CI exists, and is a script rather than a provider's file.** `README.md` and `CONTRIBUTING.md` both referred to "the same gate CI runs" while no CI configuration was tracked anywhere: every gate this tree has built was opt-in and local. `tools/ci/presubmit.sh` is now what both a developer and a workflow run — tiers 0–2, rustfmt and clippy over the graph, the per-architecture lint held to its baseline, and one architecture's boot, in 14 seconds warm against `docs/lifecycle/02`'s thirty-minute budget. `tools/ci/continuous.sh` runs the full tier-3 matrix post-merge. The logic is in the scripts because `docs/lifecycle/02` ("Developer Experience") asks that the local presubmit be the same execution as CI pre-merge, and that only stays true without anybody checking if both run one file. **It found three failures on its first run** — two rustfmt diffs dating from D186's SDK split, and unused imports in D189's new crate — all of which `bazel build //... --config=lint` had been reporting since, and which a verification habit of `grep -cE '^error'` never saw, because rustfmt prints `ERROR:` and `Diff in` and clippy's summary line is the only one that starts lower-case. **What is NOT proven:** most of what `docs/lifecycle/02` asks of CI. Tier 4 needs hardware (D34) and the fuzzing fleet needs infrastructure (D12), so neither runs; the release-candidate lane — tier 5, the reproducible-rebuild check, the ABI diff, the budget report — does not exist; and the workers are GitHub-hosted rather than hermetic and network-isolated, so build inputs are pinned but the machine is not. | The lanes above as their subjects land; hermetic workers with the remote cache `.bazelrc` already reserves a place for. |
| D192 | **Two more drivers converted, and the sweep stopped there by direction.** `usb-storage` (776 → 703 lines) and `gpu-driver` (849 → 747) name no syscall now: their `SYS_*` constants, their hand-rolled `ChannelMsgArgs` encoding, the `patch_args`/`kernel_u32` volatile helpers that read what the kernel wrote into an args block, and their receive/reply-continue loops are the SDK's, and what is left is USB mass storage and virtio-gpu. `gpu-driver` also stops forming a pointer to its own DMA pages — `with_page` calls `Machine::with_dma`, which is what makes a driver's use of a page something `//userspace/sdk`'s harness (D179, D180) can watch at all. One thing did **not** transfer mechanically: the SDK's `Dma` names the two addresses `va` and `device_address`, where the driver's local type called the second one `phys`, and the SDK deliberately derives no `Default`, because a zeroed page is not a page. The rename is the point of the seam and the array of not-yet-allocated framebuffer pages now says what it means by an empty entry. **The remaining nine drivers are not converted**, and this is a decision rather than a pause: the seam is demonstrated on five of twelve (`crypto`, `usb-hid`, `usb-storage`, `gpu`, plus what `sdk-sim` covers), which is enough to hold the shape, and D188's list of what the other nine would need from `Platform` stands as the record of the work not done. **What is NOT proven:** that the two converted drivers' serial output is unchanged in full. `gpu_boot_aarch64` passes and its claim keys match, which is the contract D184 made testable; a line-by-line diff of the kind run for `usb` was not run here. | Each remaining driver converted when it is touched for its own reasons, rather than in a sweep — and `Platform` grown one method per change, with the driver that needed it as the argument for its shape. |
| D193 | **The 0.70–0.90 band, read one function at a time — one joined, one refused, one split in half.** `irq_complete` looked like a 39-line pair and was not one: AArch64 re-armed **every** line of the device, RISC-V 64 re-armed the first. Everything above the controller write is now `kcore::dispatch::resolve_irq_lines` — the authority check and the lines themselves — and each port keeps only its GIC or PLIC write. That closes a latent defect rather than tidying two copies: no RISC-V 64 device declares an extra line today, so nothing was observably wrong, but `MAX_EXTRA_IRQS` is `kcore`'s and not a port's, so the first multi-queue controller that port grew would have had a queue go quiet with nothing saying so. The line buffer is now `&mut [u32; MAX_IRQ_LINES]`, because `intids_of_object` fills a caller's buffer and *stops when it ends* — for a short buffer that is a silently dropped line, and a fixed-size parameter is a caller that cannot be given one. `process_space_check` is the opposite verdict: **three functions with one name, and they stay three.** The narrative is shared, but the user program is entered by each port's assembly, its trap reported through each port's statics, and the teardown frame count fixed by each port's page-table geometry — a joined version takes every one of those as a closure and is a scaffold, not a check. Reading them did find a real defect: ARM 32's teardown asserted `> before`, which is exactly the "at least" form RISC-V 64's own comment records as having let a deliberately broken `free_tables` sail through. It asserts `== 11` now, derived from the layout (every address in the second gigabyte: one root, one level-2, one level-3 per 2 MiB region touched — 6 for A, 5 for B) and *then* confirmed against the machine, in that order. `driver_giveup_check` split: the spawn, the hook and the address-space dance stay per port; step 7 — the failure policy, the quarantine, the closing lifecycle record and the two assertions — is `boot_checks::apply_giveup_policy` and `driver_giveup_verdict`, because it is the shared supervisor's policy and two ports deciding it separately is two ports that can end the same ladder differently. **Four doc comments were attached to the wrong function**, found by a doc block extractor walking backwards into a second one: `mmio_of_object`'s had drifted 1,500 lines up onto `device_set_mmio_irq`, a stale near-duplicate sat on `device_add_mmio_irq`, `driver_rebind_check`'s was orphaned when a struct was inserted between them, and — **mine** — D189's extraction moved `device_events_check` to `//kernel/boot-checks` and left its doc behind in the AArch64 port, glued onto the next function. **What is NOT proven:** that these four are all of them. The same signature — a doc paragraph opening straight after a completed sentence with no `///` separator — matches 107 places tree-wide, and spot-reading says most are ordinary style rather than drift. Separating them is the deferred comment sweep (F32/F33), not this. | The 107 candidates read and the real ones fixed; and a tier-0 gate for the class, if one can be written that does not cry wolf 100 times. |
| D194 | **The kernel stopped naming userspace, and a probe caught three false verification readings.** Five kernel BUILD files carried 31 dependency edges on `//userspace/*` image crates, and three `main.rs` files carried a `#[cfg(has_x)] fn y_elf()` / `#[cfg(not) fn y_elf()` pair per program — 31 pairs, differing only in a symbol. Which programs a machine boots with is composition, not kernel: the kernel is what userspace is built *against*, and a kernel package naming `//userspace/gpu-driver` points the dependency the wrong way. The list is `//components` now, one `tessera_image_components` call per machine, and each kernel names one label. The accessors are generated from that same list, so the list and the code cannot disagree. **Six cfg flags became one** — `has_ring3_host`, `has_nvme`, `has_ring3_driver`, `has_root_task`, `has_device_manager` and `has_blk_probe` existed *solely* to gate those accessors, which is checkable rather than asserted: 48 + 6 + 8 uses across the three ports, and every one of them was an accessor. The image crates' visibility moved from the kernel packages to `//components` alone, which is a tightening. **It is deliberately not called a manifest**: `docs/architecture/01`'s component manifest is a runtime object carrying capabilities, restart policy and budgets, and putting two different things under one word is how a word stops meaning anything. **One reference was not an accessor**, and it mattered: the certificate encoder hashed `crypto_driver_image::CRYPTO_DRIVER_ELF` directly. Because it named the crate, a build without that crate did not compile — loud, but only by accident; routing it through the composition makes such a build possible, and `sha256(&[])` is a digest of nothing wearing a certificate that says it measured a driver. It is refused now. **What is NOT proven, and what three readings claimed before a probe checked:** the kernels are **not** byte-identical. The three that changed compile against a different crate graph and differ; the two untouched ones are identical. Before that was established, "ALL 7 BYTE-IDENTICAL" was printed three times against **stale artifacts** — `bazel-bin/kernel/kernel-aarch64/kernel-aarch64_bin` is not what the boot check consumes (it consumes `kernel-aarch64_image_bin`, under a transitioned config), so it was never rebuilt and its hash never moved. What settled it was a probe: change a string the boot prints, confirm the hash changes, and only then trust the comparison. What *is* established is the invariant that matters — booting both AArch64 images under the same QEMU gives 74 serial lines differing in one place, the context-switch perf line, which is timing. Also unproven: that the cargo fallback compiles, because no bare-metal Rust target is installed on this machine. | The remaining two ports folded in when they grow ring-3 programs; and a helper for artifact comparison that takes its paths from `bazel cquery` rather than from `bazel-bin`, so the stale reading above is not available to make again. |
| D195 | **The kernel's sizing became a surface, and it is not ISL.** Twenty-seven `pub const MAX_…` declarations sat in the sixteen `kcore` modules whose tables they size. Each was right where it was, and together they were a configuration surface nobody could see: no list of what a machine can be tuned to, no bound saying which values are sane, and no way to build the same kernel two sizes without editing source. They are `config/kernel.config` now — the number and the reasoning that fixed it, moved together — and each module re-exports its own, so `kcore::process::MAX_PROCESSES` still resolves and no call site changed. **The plan said ISL and the plan was wrong.** `docs/api/03`'s schema language defines *wire* boundaries and has no construct for a defaulted, ranged scalar; adding one would change a language whose job is ABI compatibility, for values that never cross a wire. `//tools/kconfig` reads this format and nothing else does. **A refusal, never a clamp:** a profile asking for a value outside a tunable's declared range does not build, because clamping would produce a kernel sized differently from the one that was asked for and nothing downstream could tell. Both build paths run the same program over the same declaration — Bazel's `//config:values` genrule and `kernel/kcore/build.rs` — bridged on `kconfig_bazel` exactly as the ISL bindings are on `isl_bazel` (D24/D54), so a host unit test and a release image cannot be sized differently. The one difference between the two outputs is `#![no_std]`, which is a named `Form` on the emitter rather than a file that gets post-processed. `//tools/checks:config_test` holds three ways this can quietly stop being true: a tunable nothing re-exports, a number that drifts back into a module, and a profile that has stopped resolving — the last mattering because `kconfig` only refuses the profile something is *built with*, and a profile nobody builds is one nobody finds out about until they need it. **Two defects were found by inverting rather than by reading.** The redeclaration scan was passing a *path string* to the matcher instead of the file's contents, so it matched nothing and the gate reported clean on a tree that violated it — caught only because the inversion that should have failed did not. And rustfmt sorts a contiguous run of `pub use` and leaves doc comments where they sit, which silently attached each number's reasoning to a different number; the re-exports are separated by blank lines so they are not one group. **What is NOT proven:** that any profile other than `default` produces a working kernel. `minimal` demonstrates that the surface is real — it emits different constants and its out-of-range values are refused — and its own comment says the boot checks are not expected to pass under it, because the AArch64 machine runs 24 ring-3 programs and it has no room for them. Nothing builds a kernel with it. Also: the kernels are **not** byte-identical (`kcore` links a crate it did not before); the values are unchanged, and booting both AArch64 images gives 74 serial lines differing only in the context-switch perf line, which is timing. | ~~A profile that is built and booted, so "configurable" is proven by a second working machine rather than by a refusal~~ (done, D199); the remaining sizing — the ports' own constants, which are per-machine rather than per-kernel — brought in when a second machine needs them to differ. |
| D196 | **The AArch64 port's composition root stopped being the whole port.** `kernel-aarch64/src/main.rs` was 15,314 lines: the boot stub, the machine's statics, `kernel_main`, and — the bulk of it — every boot check this machine runs, one function per area, in a single file nothing could navigate. It is 2,952 lines now (boot glue, statics, `kernel_main`, the trap and panic path) with 25 new modules beside it, grouped by what they are about: what the machine *is* (`discovery`, `pci`, `smmu`, `virtio_pci`), the substrate ring 3 runs on (`el0`, `ipc`, `device_access`, `host`), properties of the machine itself (`isolation`, `perf`, `power`, `relay`, `firmware`), one per device class (`net`, `pci_bus`, `nvme`, `snd`, `gpu`, `crypto`, `gpio`, `usb`, `sd`), failure (`recovery`, `restart`), and `certify`. **The split is organisational and the code says so.** Every module opens with `use crate::*` and the root re-exports each module, so the namespace is the one flat namespace it was when this was one file; what the modules buy is a name and a header per area, not a boundary. Claiming encapsulation here would be false, and tightening the globs into named imports is its own change. **The one real win beyond navigability is the unsafe inventory.** It is keyed by path, so one entry used to cover the entire port — a single `scope` field of roughly 500 words describing boot entry, the EL0 harness, the SMMU glue and the power glue in one breath, which is a sentence nobody could check against anything. There are 26 entries now, each naming what its own file does; the twelve check modules that share one harness (the kernel-space alias, the check-scoped allocator pointer, the raw access to the static executive, the GIC line) share one wording, because the operations genuinely are the same. **What changed in the output:** three serial lines. `kprintln!` takes its module from `module_path!()`, and the envelope was already written to keep the submodule path and shorten only the crate — so `msix`, `gpio: armed` and the context-switch measurement now read `[kernel_aarch64::pci]`, `[kernel_aarch64::gpio]` and `[kernel_aarch64::perf]`. That is the envelope working, not drifting; only three lines move because the checks report and `kernel_main` prints. **What is NOT proven:** that `kernel_main` is any better off. It is 2,520 of the 2,952 remaining lines — one function that runs every check in order — and splitting a function is a different job from splitting a file. The lint ratchet caught this change adding two findings (`pub(crate)` on `const _`, where it has no effect); they are fixed rather than baselined, and the count is back to 233. | `kernel_main` broken into the phases it already runs in; and the module globs replaced by named imports, which is what would turn this from a file split into a boundary. |
| D197 | **Twelve doc comments were describing something other than what they were attached to.** D193 recorded 107 candidates matching the merged-doc signature and said most were probably ordinary style. That held: sharpening the detector — a paragraph break with no `///` separator, *inside a block that uses separators elsewhere*, so the author's own convention is the evidence — left 116, and reading all of them found **twelve real ones**. The rest are wrapped prose, and are left alone. Each real one was a doc whose owning item had moved or been renamed, leaving the text glued to whatever came next, and in every case **the true owner had no documentation at all** — which is how they survived: nothing looked undocumented, because the words were still on screen a few lines away. `boot_memory_map` on two ports (AArch64 and RISC-V 64, the same drift independently), `pcie_enumerate`, `struct Smmu`, `Executive::declare_lifecycle`, `Executive::remove_one_device`, `Process::owns_thread`, `DeviceTable::register_declared`, `read_mmio_reg`, `write_desc`, `AddressSpace::table_for`, `far_word`, `blk-client`'s and `crypto-driver`'s `run`, and the device manager's `MANIFEST`. The worst was `kcore::exec`, where **three** functions' docs had merged into one block above the middle one. Two more were not orphans but the same accident in miniature — a group comment that had become a `///` doc for the constant under it (now a `//` comment, which is what it is), and a superseded summary folded into the constant that replaced it. **Verified as a move, not a rewrite:** the multiset of doc lines across `kernel/`, `userspace/`, `api/` and `tools/` goes 20,466 → 20,461, and every one of the five is accounted for — four are the group comment changing prefix, and the rest are three docs reworded where merging demanded it. **What is NOT proven:** that the remaining 99 candidates are all benign. They were read once, by one reader, against a rule of thumb; a doc that describes its item badly is invisible to this detector, and so is one whose owner is also undocumented but sits in another file. | The detector kept and pointed at new code, if a cheap version can be written that does not cry wolf a hundred times; until then this is a sweep that was done once rather than a gate. |
| D198 | **Two more drivers converted, and the first measurement of what the seam costs.** `snd-driver` (1,056 → 944 lines) and `nvme-driver` (898 → 789) name no syscall now. They were the only two of the twelve whose every syscall already had a `Platform` method, which is a fact about the trait rather than about them: the remaining programs need between one method (`device-host`, `gpio-driver`, `sd-host`) and five (`blk-probe`, `power-manager`) before they could convert at all. **The cost, measured rather than asserted:** text grows 43,343 → 49,622 for `snd` (+14.5%) and 35,863 → 44,326 for `nvme` (+23.6%). There is no dynamic dispatch anywhere in the SDK — no `dyn`, so every call monomorphises and the syscall inlines — and `gpu_driver::run` actually *shrank* by 876 bytes when its loop moved into `tessera_sdk::serve`. What the growth buys is the argument validation the hand-rolled paths skipped. **This corrects D188**, which recorded a driver-size comparison as "equal"; that reading came from a stale `bazel-bin` path, the same trap D194 records, and the honest number is the one above. Nothing measures *time*: the tree instruments one budget, B7 context-switch, and has no driver-path benchmark, so the claim that a few compares are noise beside a syscall trap is reasoning and not a result. **`nvme` is converted with one thing left raw, and it is stated in the code rather than glossed:** `Platform::with_dma` hands out a *scoped* slice, which is what makes a driver's page use watchable, and this controller's submission and completion rings are long-lived — borrowed for the driver's life and threaded through every request. A scoped view cannot express them, so `page()` still forms the pointer, and nvme's DMA discipline is not watchable the way `gpu`'s and `snd`'s are. **A defect in D192's own work, found by reading the SDK while converting:** `tessera_sdk::serve` already stages a reply into its own scratch before sending, and the `gpu` conversion had added a second staging buffer and a `copy_from_slice` on top of it. Removed; `gpu` loses 72 bytes and one copy per request, and all three drivers now write their reply straight into the buffer that will be sent. **What is NOT proven:** that either driver's behaviour is unchanged in any way the boot checks do not assert. Both checks pass and the AArch64 serial log is 74 lines before and after, differing only in the memory map (the embedded ELFs are bigger, which is the cost above) and the context-switch timing line. | The `channel_send` + `device_declare` pair, which three programs — `pci-bus`, `platform-bus`, `usb-host` — want identically, and which is therefore the next method shape with more than one caller arguing for it. |
| D199 | **The configuration surface grew its other two thirds, and a second machine boots from it.** D195 declared the kernel's *sizing* and left two larger surfaces where they were: the features, which were `--cfg=` string literals hand-written into five kernel `BUILD.bazel` files, and the composition — which ring-3 programs each image carries — which was a Starlark dict per machine, i.e. **the driver-selection surface, as a build-file edit**. Both are `config/kernel.config` now, so a *profile* is one statement about one machine rather than a statement about a third of one. **`requires` is Tessera's `depends on`, and deliberately not Linux's.** The constraints that actually exist here are arithmetic relations between capacities, and they were prose: `MAX_WAITERS` "is sized to the scheduler's thread table", an object larger than the free list "could not be reclaimed". True sentences, in comments, which a profile could break with nothing to notice. They are `MAX_WAITERS >= MAX_THREADS` and `MAX_OBJECT_PAGES <= MAX_FREE_FRAMES` now — the second only expressible because writing it found that `pmem`'s two capacities were **not declared at all**: they sat behind a bare `const` rather than a `pub const`, and D195's redeclaration scan looked only for `pub const`, so the setting that bounds `MAX_OBJECT_PAGES` was one nothing could see. The scan now reads both, and the sizes are 29. Components carry the boolean form (`gpu_client -> gpu_driver`), which is what makes turning off a driver a refusal naming the client rather than a link error. **Where the composition lives, and why it is split.** Bazel must know the image labels statically to form dependency edges, so the labels stay in `//components/BUILD.bazel` and the *selection* comes from the profile through a `//tools/kconfig` genrule; the tool holds the two lists to each other in both directions. A component that is off keeps its accessor and returns an empty slice — not a special case invented for the option, but the state every check already handles, because it is what the cargo inner loop has always seen. Nothing then references the image crate, so the linker never pulls the program's bytes in: **measured, the lean AArch64 image is 860,160 bytes smaller**, which is the difference between removing a program and removing the mention of one. **`--//config:profile=<name>` reconfigures the whole invocation** — constants and composition together, one graph configured twice rather than two graphs to keep in step. The profile list is globbed, so adding a profile is dropping a file into `config/profiles/`; `TESSERA_PROFILE` is the cargo half. **The tool is one program because the answers must agree**: `emit` and `components` are the build, and `show` (the resolved `.config`, with the provenance of every line — an object this tree has never had, since resolution happened inside a genrule and reached a compiler without ever reaching a file), `menu`, `diff`, `migrate` and `check` are for the person deciding. The browser is a pure state machine — 76 host tests press keys and read the screen — behind a terminal shim thin enough to read, because this tree vendors no third-party crates and the alternative to `stty` was `unsafe` in a host tool. Every edit in it is a full resolution, so a value the build would refuse is refused at the keystroke, with the reason. **D195's exit criterion is met.** `//tools/qemu:profile_boot_aarch64_test` builds and boots `aarch64-lean` through a build-setting transition: clean exit 33, the stacks it drops reporting themselves skipped rather than failing, the block path it keeps still working, and the image smaller. **The bug it found was found by inverting**: two configurations of one kernel keep the same filename, so a test handed both receives one of them twice — the size comparison returned equal, which is exactly what a profile name with no file behind it also produces (a `select` takes its default arm). `profile_binary` renames its output, and the size assertion stayed as the check for both. **What is NOT proven: feature *selection*.** A profile that changes a feature resolves, and changes what `show` and `flags` report, and would build a kernel with the feature at its default — because a kernel's `--cfg=` flags are Starlark literals and Starlark cannot read a profile, so no `select` can be built from one. Rather than ship that divergence, `//tools/checks:config_test` **refuses a profile that changes a feature**, and holds every kernel's `--cfg=` set to the features the declaration says that machine has — in both directions, and including a `--cfg` with no matching `--check-cfg`, which is a typo that compiles. Feature *declaration* is real (they are listed, documented, in the `.config`, and gated); feature *selection* is not. Also not done: the ports' own per-machine constants (D195's remaining half), and each port's `#[cfg(not(has_components))]` stub module, which still lists its machine's programs by hand — a third copy the composition gate does not yet read. | Features selected by a profile rather than only declared by one, which needs the store accessor routed through the generated per-machine crate the way the components are (and a components target for `riscv32`/`arm32`, which have none); the ports' stub modules generated from the declaration rather than hand-listed; and the ports' own sizing constants declared, when a second machine needs them to differ. |
| D214 | **A filesystem the tree did not write, so a test can be wrong about the code and not about the format.** `//api/ext2` is the read path — mount, walk a path, read a file — `no_std`, `forbid(unsafe_code)`, allocation-free, generic over a `BlockIo` trait that asks only for 512-byte sectors, which is the unit the block class actually delivers (`dma_max_transfer_sectors` is 1 on every driver here). The model is `kernel/virtio` and `api/image-store`: format logic that knows nothing about a kernel, exercised on the host, with the ring-3 program left to supply sectors. Two 4 KiB scratch buffers are the whole of its working memory; the indirect walk reuses one of them at every level, so double and triple indirection cost no more buffers than single. **Porting a format rather than inventing one is the testable choice.** `mke2fs` lays out the image the tests read, so eighteen host tests assert *ext2* rather than this crate's opinion of it — and the same script builds the image for both build paths, cargo through `build.rs` and Bazel through a genrule, so a unit test and a boot check cannot read different bytes. Getting that image byte-reproducible took three findings: `mke2fs` writes a random UUID and hash seed unless told otherwise, `-d` stamps each copied inode's `ctime` from the real clock and `ctime` is the one timestamp `touch` cannot set (normalised through `debugfs`, so every byte is still e2fsprogs'), and `awk`'s `%c` emits multibyte UTF-8 above 127 in a UTF-8 locale — which silently made a 70,000-byte fixture 105,000 bytes. **Hostile input, and the gate says so:** ext2 joins `fuzz_gate::HAND_WRITTEN_PARSERS` beside the device tree, the image store and the update channel, with a harness over a hand-built superblock that mounts, iterates, looks up and reads at `u64::MAX`. **Two of four inversions did not fail, and both were the test's fault rather than the code's.** The directory-record property is held by two guards, not one — removing either leaves the other holding, and removing both *hangs*, which is the failure the guards exist to prevent and is how the property was confirmed pinned. The hole test was genuinely vacuous: block zero of an `mke2fs` image is itself all zeroes, so "a hole reads as zeroes" and "a zero pointer was followed and block zero read" were indistinguishable until the test began poisoning block zero. **A verification failure worth recording:** the first attempt to fix that test silently did not apply — `cargo fmt` had reflowed the file and the match string no longer matched — and the re-run was then reported against unmodified source. The same stale-verification shape as D194, in a different costume: an edit must be confirmed present before what it changes is measured. **What is NOT proven:** nothing runs this on a machine. There is no block service, no filesystem service and no ring-3 caller yet (M3, M4); the write path is M5; double and triple indirection are implemented but reached only by crafted tests, because `mke2fs` on a 4 MiB image has no file large enough to need them; and `mke2fs`/`debugfs` are host tools outside the pinned dependency set, which is the price of an outside authority on what the bytes mean. | The block service (M3) and the filesystem service (M4), which turn this from a parser into something a program can read a file through. |
| D200 | **The SDK can carry a capability, so a driver written against it can serve the out-of-line path at last.** `tessera_sdk::serve` could not attach handles to a message, which is why `nvme-driver`, `sd-host` and `usb-storage` all answered `ReadInto`/`WriteFrom` with `NOT_SUPPORTED` and only `device-host` — on raw syscalls — implemented them. `Platform` grows `call_with`, `receive_with`, `respond_with`, `dma_attach` and `dma_detach`; `serve_transfers` is the loop that uses them. The new type carries **no transfer mode**: the kernel refuses `share` and `snapshot` outright, so offering a choice the machine does not have would only move the failure from compile time to run time. `nvme-driver` implements both ordinals for real — the client's buffer is attached to the device, named in the NVMe command, and handed back with the reply, without the driver ever mapping it, because a driver with no mapping of a buffer did not copy it. Proven by `blk-client`'s existing round trip, which writes a sector through a memory object, reads it back and compares every byte; the nvme machine has one client, so it now runs id 3 and carries **both** proofs, the conformance suite and the transfer, rather than leaving one never run against this driver. **Two bugs found on the way, both in what the SDK had to model.** The kernel does not report how many capabilities arrived — `handle_count` is a field a *sender* fills — so the count is the number of non-zero entries in the installed report, which is what `device-host` had always done and what a reading of the descriptor alone would get wrong. And the decoder must be told that count: passing zero to `Reader::in_message` makes a request naming a handle index fail to decode, which silently turned every out-of-line call into a protocol error. **One inversion found redundant code rather than a missing test.** Removing the explicit `dma_detach` changed nothing observable, because `kcore::dispatch` detaches on the departure path deliberately — "a driver that cannot forget is better than one that must remember". Worse, the call had a failure mode of its own: a detach error was folded into `IoError`, so a completed read could have been reported as a failed one. It is gone, and the trait method stays for its real use, which the kernel names — a driver wanting a buffer back in CPU-land while continuing to hold it. **A latent bug fixed while here:** `sd-host` and `usb-storage` answered ordinals 10 and 11 with a `BlockControlReply` where the contract pairs them with `BlockBufferReply`. Both are 24 bytes, so it decoded without error and put `transferred` where `state` sits — harmless only for as long as nothing called them, which is exactly what stopped being true. **What is NOT proven:** that a driver may hold a transferred buffer across replies (the model gives every one back), that more than one capability per message works (the contract sends one, and `MAX_TRANSFER` is asserted nowhere but the type), and that `call_with` is right — no program uses the client half yet, since `blk-client` still hand-rolls it. Separately: `snd_boot_aarch64_test` failed once during this work with 5 audio periods where 7 were wanted, and did not reproduce in twelve subsequent runs, six of them at HEAD. It touches no part of this change; recorded because a boot check that flakes once is a gate nobody can read. | The block service (M3), whose upward interface is this same contract, and which is the second caller `call_with` needs before its shape can be argued for. |
| D201 | **A block service, speaking the same contract on both sides.** `docs/drivers/02` puts six layers between an application and a device; `//userspace/block-service` is v0 of the one below a filesystem. It is a `BlockDevice` **client** of the driver and a `BlockDevice` **server** to its own client, so a consumer cannot tell it from a driver — and it is therefore held to the same `api/class-conformance` battery a driver is, with no second contract to keep in step and no way for a consumer to have to know which layer it reached. It holds **no dirty state**, which is `docs/storage/02`'s stated reason a block service's restart is survivable; caching and reordering between barriers are permitted by that document and deferred to when there is something to schedule. **Verified by rewiring an existing check rather than adding one.** In `ring3_host_check` the service now sits between the driver and client A, while client B still calls the driver directly — so one boot covers both the layered path and the direct one, and every assertion that check already made applies unchanged. Client A ran the full conformance battery through the service and it came back `is_complete()`. **The property this layer exists for was pinned only after an inversion showed it was not.** A service that answered `Flush` itself, never asking the device, passed everything: the conformance suite checks that an advertised optional is *answered*, not that it reached the medium, and from every side except the driver's a faked flush and a forwarded one are identical. `device-host` now reports `FLUSH_SEEN_TAG` once per boot and the check's sink expects it, so the same inversion fails. That is the durability chain — "never acknowledges a flush it has not pushed to stable media" — made checkable rather than asserted. **Geometry stopped being invented.** `device-host` hardcoded `sector_count: 2048` ("the test disk is 1 MiB"); it reads the capacity out of virtio-blk config space now, through `tessera_virtio::blk_capacity`, which is host-tested on both halves of the 64-bit field — a version reading one word would report every disk under 2 TiB correctly and truncate the rest. `sd-host` and `nvme-driver` still report zero, which a filesystem cannot tell from an empty medium; they are unchanged here and named in M4's dependencies. **What is NOT proven:** that the service survives a restart (nothing kills it), that it is correct with more than one client (it serves one endpoint, and a second would need the port-select `device-host` uses), that `Reset` forwarded through it leaves the *driver's* state defined rather than the service's, and any of what the layer is eventually for — queues, scheduling, integrity, discard policy. Its `call_with` use is the first, so the client half of D200's transfer path now has exactly one caller rather than none. | The filesystem service (M4), which is this service's first real consumer and the thing that will care whether the geometry is true. |
| D202 | **A file, opened by name off a real ext2 volume, read through the whole stack.** Four ring-3 programs: `device-host` drives the disk, `block-service` is the layer between, `fs-service` reads ext2 through it, and `fs-client` asks what is in `/hello.txt` and compares **every byte** against what `mke2fs` wrote. Each layer was already proved on its own — the format by `//api/ext2`'s host tests, the transfer path by `blk-client`, the block layer by the class-conformance battery — and none of that established that they compose. `fs_service.isl` is the contract (`Open`/`Read`/`Close`, ordinals 4–7 reserved for the write path), with reads carrying a transferred buffer because the kernel's inline limit is 256 bytes and a file read shaped around a message size would be a contract built around the wrong thing. `fs-service` is the first user of `call_with`, which D200 recorded as having no caller. **The bring-up is shared rather than copied.** Both checks want the same manager, driver, devices, interrupt route and client channels, and differ only in who sits on the channels — so that is `bring_up_device_host`, and `ring3_host_check` was rewritten onto it first, proved by a serial diff: 91 lines before and after, differing only in the context-switch timing line. Copying it would have been three hundred lines of the port glue D189 and D193 are about. **Four failures found by running it, none by reading it.** A null boot-allocator pointer (`0xbad2`) because the check never exposed one; a **stack overflow** arriving as a level-3 translation fault, because an ext2 reader holds two 4 KiB scratch buffers and the user stack was four pages — six still failed, eight is the first that works, and the number is measured; a deadlock, because a disk completion lands after every thread has parked and this check had no interrupt pump; and `device-host` refusing the volume outright, because it self-tests by reading `TESSERAV` from sector 0. That last one has a clean answer: ext2 reserves the first 1024 bytes for a boot block, so the markers live there and one volume is both a filesystem and a disk a driver will accept — `e2fsck` still passes. **Two checks, two machines.** The ring-3 host check writes to its disk and those writes land where an ext2 superblock is, so a two-disk machine is the filesystem check's and the other check now says so and skips. Funnelling them through two helpers took the addition from eleven to four; the baseline file now says which class may raise it and that a raise without a ledger entry is the thing it exists to prevent. **What is NOT proven:** every deviation from `docs/storage/02` that v0 carries — there is no VFS layer, no page cache, no `mmap` coherence, no readahead, and a read *copies* through a transferred buffer rather than faulting on a pager-backed object, because no ring-3 program can be handed one on any port. | D203 | **The ext2 write path, judged by `e2fsck` rather than by its own reader.** `//api/ext2` writes now: block and inode allocation out of the bitmaps, single-indirect growth, directory insert, inode write-back, and truncate. Ten new tests, and every one that mutates a volume hands the result to **`e2fsck -fn`** — which recomputes the bitmaps, the free counts, the link counts and `i_blocks` from the structures themselves and says whether what came out is a filesystem ext2 would recognise. That is the whole return on porting a real format: this crate's reader agreeing with this crate's writer would prove only that one hand wrote both. **The accounting is part of the write, not bookkeeping after it.** A bitmap bit set with the counters left alone produces a volume that mounts, reads correctly, and is reported corrupt by the first tool that looks — so the group descriptor's free counts and the superblock's move together in one function. Freeing is where a filesystem loses data twice, and both halves are pinned: a block released but still pointed at is one the next allocation hands to a second file, and a block forgotten but not released is space nothing reclaims. **Five inversions, all caught by the oracle** — allocating without the counters, allocating without the bitmap bit, freeing the bit but keeping the pointer, clearing the pointer without freeing the bit, and forgetting `i_blocks`. A missing `e2fsck` is now a loud failure rather than a skip, because a suite that quietly stopped checking reports the same green as one that checked. **What is NOT proven:** double and triple indirect are *read* but not *grown* — a file past ~268 KiB is refused rather than written, which is a stated limit and not a silent truncation. | D204 | **A write through the filesystem service, and a durability claim checked by killing the machine.** `fs-service` answers `Write`, `Sync`, `Create` and `Unlink` on the ordinals `fs_service.isl` reserved, and `fs-client` creates a file, writes to it, syncs, reads it back through the service, then creates and removes a second name and asks for it again — because an `Unlink` that answered OK and left the entry would pass any check that read only the reply. **`Sync` is answered from the device, not from the service.** It issues `Flush` downward and returns what came back; `device-host` reports the first flush it is ever asked for as a tag the boot check's sink requires, so a service that answered a flush itself fails the check by the sink being short of exactly that value. **The cut is a separate check.** `fs_boot_aarch64.sh` stops the machine cleanly, which cannot distinguish a write that reached the medium from one a tidy shutdown pushed there; `fs_crash_aarch64.sh` watches the image from outside and SIGKILLs QEMU the instant the bytes appear — no exit path, no teardown — and they must still be there. Everywhere else an EL0 fault meant the thread had done something wrong and was ended, so a region *recorded but not populated* could not exist outside x86-64, which is the mapping a page cache is built out of. `kcore::fault::repair` is that step written once: it takes an address space, an address and whether the access was a store, and answers what the trap handler must do. Two answers are handed back rather than acted on — `NeedsPageIn` needs a channel and a scheduler to forward a request and block the faulter, and `Fatal` is the exception path — because this module has neither and pretending otherwise is how a shared helper becomes a scaffold. **x86-64 was moved onto it and the move was proved, not asserted.** Its resolver now calls `repair` and keeps only what that port knows: `CR2`, the error-code write bit, and who to ask for a page. The serial diff is the evidence, and it needed a control: before and after differed on the KASLR base, the tick count and twelve `perf:` lines — but the *same binary* run twice differs on the same twelve, one of them by 7x (page-in p50 30144 then 212265). The timing lines are host noise wider than anything the change caused; no verdict, claim or behavioural line moved, and the KASLR base is deterministic per image and moved because the image did. **AArch64 got the mechanism and a check that earns it.** `dpage` maps two pages demand-only, and a ring-3 program reads one *before* writing it, writes both, and reads them back; the report folds in the pre-write read, so a page that arrived holding somebody else's bytes fails the check that a report alone would pass. Nothing complained: this check ran, left its stack mapped, and the next check to spawn a thread failed inside `spawn_user` on an occupied address, reporting a number about nothing in particular. It now has its own block, three `const` assertions, and its stack back at teardown. The second was on another port entirely: RISC-V 64's `blk: OK` line is 150 bytes with a seven-digit timestamp and 151 with eight, against a 150-byte limit — so it failed the moment this change shifted that boot past ten million ticks. A memory object may now be **service-backed**: `MemoryCreatePaged` (45) makes one whose frames are all absent, `PageSupply` (22, given a number long ago and an implementation here) fills one page from a page of the caller's own memory, and `MapObject` (46) maps it so that absent pages are page-in requests rather than drift. | D209 | **A pager that never answers now leaves its reader a fault, not a thread nobody can account for.** D207 and D208 both recorded this as the open gap and both described it wrongly, which measuring fixed: a page-in that is never answered does **not** hang the machine. Three shapes, and the difference matters — a pager that *exits* unwinds the run, a pager that *stays alive and silent* also unwinds the run, and in both the faulting thread is left `Blocked` for ever with nothing delivered to it: no fault, no error, no record. Only a pager that *spins* hangs, and that is preemption, not paging — any ring-3 infinite loop does it, pagers included. So what was closed is the first two, which are the ones `docs/kernel/03` step 5 is about. **The trigger is a decision, not a timeout.** `Executive::run` now expires an outstanding page-in when the scheduler returns with nothing runnable: the answer would have to come from a thread that is not going to run again, which on a cooperative scheduler is decidable rather than guessed. Each expiry wakes the faulter, marks the object faulted so the *next* access fails immediately instead of waiting on a pager that has already failed, counts a supervisor miss, and emits the record. The faulter learns when it runs again, inside the `call` frame it was parked in — a blocked thread has no frame in which to receive an answer. **`KError::TimedOut` is a new variant** (17, appended) because the alternative was already there and false: finding no reply produces `PeerClosed`, and the peer is alive and merely silent. A caller told its peer had closed stops retrying something that may work next time. **The premise was wrong the first time, and the filesystem check said so.** "Nothing runnable" means no *thread* can answer; it says nothing about a *device*. The fs stack parks entirely on the block driver's interrupt while the disk reads a page — indistinguishable from a pager that never will, and every fs boot broke. The two are told apart by asking whether anything is parked on a port: a thread there waits for hardware and the boot's interrupt pump will run it. That check is held by `fs_boot_aarch64`, so removing it fails a test rather than a review. **A late reply is discarded rather than delivered.** A pager that finally answers after the kernel gave up puts its reply on the endpoint the next page-in calls from; queued, it would be dequeued by that call and taken for its answer. Endpoints count abandoned calls and every reply now goes through one delivery point, which also removed three copies of the enqueue-and-find-the-caller dance. **Seven inversions, all caught**, and two of them earned their keep: removing the wake-the-faulter path was *invisible* until the check asserted **which** error the reader got — because finding no reply fails the access too, so every other assertion held with the mechanism deleted. It is the D207 lesson exactly, one milestone later. And the abandoned-reply discard had no falsifying scenario a boot check could build, so it is host-tested. **`.scheduler().run()` is gone** — 39 call sites across two ports now go through `Executive::run`, because a mechanism a check can bypass is one a check will bypass, and the first version of this shipped bypassed by the very check meant to prove it. **What is NOT proven:** a spinning ring-3 program still hangs the machine, and that is the preemption gap, unchanged and unrelated. The trigger is all-or-nothing — a pager that is merely *slow* is waited on for as long as it likes, because nothing distinguishes slow from stopped while other threads still run; a tick deadline for that needs a timer that can unblock a thread, which is the same missing preemption. `PageInSupervisor`'s escalation is wired and counted but nothing acts on it: three misses report a supervised restart and no pager is restarted. A faulted object stays faulted for ever — there is no clearing it, so a pager that recovers cannot say so. | The write path through the cache: dirty tracking at the write fault, write-back before a page is dropped, and `Sync` flushing pages rather than going around them. |
| D210 | **Writes through the cache: a store is recorded, and a writer at the bound is held until a page is persisted.** D205 flagged the hole and it was the reason every object handed to a client so far was mapped read-only — `WriteGranted` made the page writable and recorded nothing, so a store landed in a page the kernel believed unchanged, and a page believed unchanged is one eviction throws away. **The dirty bit is software and the fault is the mechanism.** A page is supplied read-only *even when the mapping grants write*, precisely so the first store faults; that fault is the kernel's only chance to notice. `kcore::fault` no longer grants — it returns `NeedsDirty` and stops, because the decision belongs to the object's accounting and `fault.rs` has no Executive to reach it. Granting there had made the accounting unreachable by construction: by the time anyone saw the answer the page was already writable. **The accounting is `ObjectCache`, wired at last.** It has been in the tree since the pager-pressure harness, host-tested, with the throttle rule stated — and unused (D206). Each paged object now holds one. It duplicates residency with the frame array deliberately: the two are written in the same functions, so they cannot drift without a missing call the compiler sees, and the alternative was re-deriving a rule that already exists and is tested. **Throttling is a hold, not a refusal.** `docs/kernel/03` holds a writer past the dirty bound until a write-back drains one, and this does: the writing thread blocks inside its own store while the kernel asks the object's service to persist the lowest dirty page, then tries again. Safe to take inline because *the writer is never the service* — a client at the bound is not the thread that answers — and the service that faults on an object it serves is the self-paging case, refused by the same guard page-in uses. The first version of this shipped as a plain refusal for one stage, which was honest and lost nothing, and `write_back` is what made it a hold. **A page is clean only after the service says so, and re-protected when it is.** The re-protection is the half that matters: a clean page left *writable* takes its next store with no fault, nothing records it, and the write is dropped by an eviction that believes the page unchanged. **Six inversions; two of them were not caught the first time and the fix was to make the check harder, not to argue.** Removing the re-protection passed, because the writer never wrote the same page twice — so it now writes the drained page again, and a page still clean there is a lost write. Removing the *service's* answer also passed and still does: the reply is believed, and a check that a **refusal** is honoured is not built. Two defects found by running it: an object of four pages against a bound of eight can never reach it, so the throttle test proved nothing until it was sized from the constant; and the write-back service replied with `ChannelReply`, which blocks the replier — the trap this tree's own notes name — so its report never happened and the sink was short by exactly its half. **What is NOT proven:** `Sync` still flushes the device and goes *around* the cache, so a file written through its mapping is not yet made durable by syncing it — that is the next step and the reason this is half a milestone. A service that answers `persisted: false` is untested. Nothing evicts, so write-back happens only under the dirty bound and never under memory pressure; `WriteBackReservation` is still unused. The dirty bound is half an object and is per-object, not system-wide, so a thousand objects at their bound is a thousand times the memory with nothing to say about it. And the write-back window is one page at a fixed address: two concurrent write-backs would collide, which single-core cooperative scheduling makes impossible today and will not later. | `Sync` through the cache: a service that flushes its object's dirty pages before it answers, so an acknowledged write to a *mapping* is as durable as one through the `Write` ordinal. |
| D211 | **`Sync` flushes the cache instead of going around it: a store into memory becomes a byte on a disk.** `fs-client` maps `/mapped.txt` read-write, stores into it — **sending the service nothing at all** — and asks for a sync; the boot script then finds those bytes in the volume after the machine has stopped. Until now `Sync` flushed the device, which answered for writes that had gone through the `Write` ordinal and knew nothing about writes that had gone through a mapping. That is the difference between a durability claim and a device command. **The query is the shape, because a callback would deadlock.** `fs-service` is the pager for its own file objects, so a kernel-initiated `WriteBack` would arrive at a service already blocked handling the `Sync` that caused it — the self-paging cycle, refused. `docs/kernel/03` names the alternative in the same breath: *"the kernel exposes dirty-range queries to pagers for coordinated flushing (for example, filesystem `fsync`)"*. `MemoryDirtyPages` (47) is that query and `PageWrittenBack` (48) is the acknowledgment; both need `SUPPLY`, because which pages of a file have changed and whether they are on the medium are facts about its contents. The kernel still never marks a page clean on its own. **`MemoryUnmap` (49) exists because nothing could give a mapping back.** A program that mapped an object held that address until it died, which a service mapping a different file per flush runs out of at once — and the client did too, on its second file. The mapping's own frame references go back with it; frames the object or another mapping still holds stay alive. The service must map per flush rather than once at open, because `MapObject` installs the pages resident *at map time* and a page supplied later would fault in the service — into a request only it could answer. **Four inversions, and one needed the check made harder.** Sync not flushing the cache, the service reporting pages it had not written, and the dirty query answering nothing are each caught by the volume search. Cleaning a page without re-protecting it was **not**: the client wrote once and synced once, so the missing fault had nothing to show. It now writes a second marker *after* the first sync — the store that only lands if the kernel put the fault back — and the script names that failure specifically. **Two defects found by running it.** The client mapped two files at one address and never released the first, which is what sent me looking for an unmap. And the service unmapped the *largest* extent a flush could handle rather than the object's own, so `reclaim_range`'s exact-match refused it, the window stayed, and the failure surfaced on the **second** sync as an I/O error about a perfectly good file. **What is NOT proven:** a service answering `persisted: false` is still untested, so the refusal path is written and unexercised — unchanged from D210. Nothing evicts, so a cache still only grows and `WriteBackReservation` stays unused. The flush window and the write-back window are each one fixed address, which cooperative single-core scheduling makes safe and nothing else would. `Sync` flushes one file's object; there is no whole-cache flush, so a machine going down cleanly has no way to drain everything. And the dirty bound is per-object, so the memory a thousand cached files may hold dirty is a thousand times the bound with nothing to say about it. | The eviction half: reclaiming clean pages under memory pressure, which is what makes a cache bounded rather than merely correct — and the first thing that needs `WriteBackReservation`. |
| D212 | **The cache has a ceiling: a reader walks more pages than it can hold, and every one of them is right.** Reclaim is what makes a page cache bounded rather than merely correct, and until now nothing was ever dropped — a cache only grew. **The budget is the mechanism, not a tuning knob.** The allocator only fails when the machine is out of memory, which no check can induce and which is the worst moment to start reclaiming: a dirty page needs a write-back and a write-back needs memory. So the cache draws from a small `WriteBackReservation` — in the tree since the pager-pressure harness, host-tested, and unused until now — whose *reserved* slice is the frames a write-back can always have. That is the reclaim deadlock expressed as a number. Reaching the ceiling evicts a clean page; when nothing clean is left, one dirty page is written back first and then taken, which is the sequence the reservation exists to keep possible. **A dirty page is never evicted**, checked in the last place that can tell. **Four inversions, and two took three attempts to make catchable — which was the work.** Removing the budget is caught at once. Evicting a dirty page needed a host test. But *eviction without unmapping* passed twice: it does not corrupt anything, because the mapping holds a frame reference of its own, so the frame stays alive and the reader goes on reading correct bytes — it **leaks**, returning the budget slot while keeping the memory. The second attempt measured fresh frames drawn from the memory map, and that is zero either way: earlier checks leave a free list deep enough to absorb the whole run. What finally separated them was the **eviction count**, and measuring is what produced it — with the unmap, the reader's second pass faults on every page it lost and evictions exceed the object's size (13 against 12); without it, the second pass never faults at all and the count stops at one per page (6). The reader walks the object twice for exactly that reason. **A number that surprised me was worth chasing.** Six pages resident against a budget of eight looked wrong until the reservation explained it: the ordinary ceiling is capacity *less* the reserve, and the check now asserts against that — asserting against the whole budget would have passed with the reservation deleted, which is the number that keeps reclaim from deadlocking. **What is NOT proven:** eviction is driven only by the cache's own budget, never by the machine being short of memory — nothing consults the frame allocator, so a system under real pressure reclaims nothing. The victim is the lowest-offset clean page, which is not a policy so much as the absence of one: no reference bit, no age, no second chance, so a sequential walk evicts exactly what it is about to want. `evict_candidate` scans an object at a time and `any_evictable` scans objects in table order, so the first object always loses its pages first. The budget is global and fixed at eight frames, which is the right size for a check and no size at all for a machine. And a service answering `persisted: false` remains untested, unchanged since D210. | The cache is now bounded, written through, and durable on demand. What it still lacks is a *policy* — reclaiming under real memory pressure, and choosing a victim by something better than its address. |
| D213 | **The page cache becomes a reserve: when the machine is short of memory, cached pages go back — whatever they belong to.** D212's ceiling stops the cache growing; this is the other direction, and they are different limits. A cache well inside its own budget can still be the only memory left to take, and `docs/kernel/03` reclaims clean pages under *memory* pressure, meaning the allocator rather than a bound the cache set for itself. `FrameSource` gains `frames_available`, whose default `None` is a real answer — the page-table walker's source is init-only and genuinely cannot say, and a caller that read silence as "plenty" would decide it is not under pressure on no evidence. Two triggers, and the split matters: a **watermark**, so pages are given back before the last frame is gone; and an **allocation that failed**, which needs no forecast and is the only path a silent source can take. The fault path reclaims *before* repairing, because a demand fill and a copy-on-write copy are each handed a bare `FrameSource` with no way back to the cache — an empty allocator reaches them as an unrepairable fault and kills the thread. **The pressure in the check is real.** Nothing simulates a low-memory flag: the check carves a run of frames out of the boot allocator, builds a second allocator over exactly that run, and hands it to the syscall path. A reader then walks an object three times the size of the memory behind it, and finishes. **Two of the assertions were wrong in ways the inversions exposed, and both are worth stating.** The first pool was forty frames: the walk finished, the check passed, and it proved *nothing new* — with that much memory D212's budget stopped the cache at six pages and the allocator was never short. Only a pool whose usable part is smaller than that budget makes pressure the binding constraint, and the check now asserts `resident < the cache's own ceiling`, which is precisely the difference between this and D212. The second was worse: it asserted the pool ended at or above `RECLAIM_WATERMARK` — phrased in terms of the very constant the mechanism uses, so setting that constant to zero moved the goalpost and the check passed with the watermark deleted. It now asserts the pool never reached zero, which is absolute and is what the watermark buys. **Five inversions.** Watermark removed, watermark reclaim removed, reclaim-on-failure removed, a dirty page taken for pressure, and eviction of the wrong page. The failure path is unreachable while the watermark works, so it is proved by a host test with a **silent** frame source — one that leaves `frames_available` at its default — which is exactly the case it exists for. **What is NOT proven:** the fault-path reclaim is a backstop nothing independently exercises. With the watermark holding, a demand fill never meets an empty allocator, so removing that call breaks no check; it is kept for a demand-fill-heavy workload with no page-ins, where nothing else would reclaim, and that workload does not exist here. Reclaim still never writes back — a dirty page is passed over rather than persisted, so an all-dirty cache under pressure frees nothing and the allocation fails. The victim is still the lowest-offset clean page of the first object in table order: no reference bit, no age, no second chance, so a sequential walk evicts what it is about to want. And `RECLAIM_WATERMARK` is one number for every machine. | A victim policy — a reference bit and a second chance — so that reclaim under pressure stops evicting the page about to be read. |
| D215 | **A boot check that has failed three times and been recorded in nobody's name.** `snd_boot_aarch64_test` has failed intermittently across three milestones. The first round had a cause and got a fix: D176/`db46fc7` found the fed stream reporting six periods played and one gap against a claim that required zero, which is a real-time property asserted on a host that promises nothing about when a ring-3 program runs — it held in isolation six times for six and failed three times in five with the other twenty-three QEMUs alongside. The claim was withdrawn to `played >= PERIODS_TO_PLAY` (3, of the 7 the client writes) and the counts moved into the report for a reader rather than the verdict. **Then it failed twice more, and neither observation says which claim failed.** D200 recorded one failure that did not reproduce in twelve subsequent runs, six of them at HEAD; D202 recorded a second, non-reproducing in six — both noted as "five audio periods where seven were wanted", both written into the middle of an entry about something else, and neither carrying an entry of its own. The reading is a diagnostic number rather than a verdict: `snd_check` asserts `report >> 32` alone — three separable bits, 941 for the conformance suite, 942 for the fed stream, 943 for the abandoned one — and prints the failing one as `check {which} failed`. The low half is explicitly not asserted (`kernel/kernel-aarch64/src/snd.rs:199`). So the check named the failing claim on the wire both times and neither note kept it, which is why two observations of the same flake carry no information about whether they were the same flake. Five periods played satisfies a bound of three, so whatever failed, it was not the count that was written down. **What is NOT known:** which claim failed, whether the two were one defect or two, and whether the residual timing dependence — a client supplying one period per syscall round trip against a device consuming in real time — is the cause or an unrelated survivor. The full matrix passes today, 27 for 27, which is the evidence a flake is best at producing. Recorded here rather than left in the prose of two unrelated entries, because a gate nobody can read is the thing this ledger exists for, and one written up twice in someone else's paragraph is one nobody will find by looking. | The next occurrence recorded with its check number, so the claim is named rather than the count; then either the residual real-time dependence removed — which is the out-of-line grant the audio contract lacks and the block class has (D158) — or the check run under deliberate load enough times to say the dependence is not reachable. Until one of those, this is a known-flaky gate that is being watched rather than trusted. |
| D216 | **A page was left writable while its service was saving it, and marked clean when the service answered.** `write_back` maps the dirty page read-only into the *service* for the duration of the request, which is the half that was thought through — a service that could write the page would be changing one the kernel is in the middle of saving. Every other mapping stayed writable. **The request blocks**: `call_service` parks the asking thread and other threads run while the service reads, so a store from one of them landed through a writable PTE, took no fault, reached `memory_mark_dirty` never, and was then marked clean by the reply — a write dropped by the next eviction with nothing having gone wrong anywhere. The page's own record had no state for the window: `PageEntry` carried an offset and a dirty bit. **The order is now request-first**, and a store that arrives anyway is recorded rather than inferred: `pager::WriteBack` is `Idle`/`InFlight`/`Redirtied` on the entry, armed before the request and read when it returns, and `write_back` marks clean only if the service persisted *and* nothing overtook it. **Where the record is set is the whole of why it can be trusted.** A pager-backed page is supplied read-only (`vm::supply_page`) and the only thing that ever grants the write is the fault path, so a store during the window cannot reach the page without passing `mark_dirty` — and it is set on the **already-dirty** branch, because a page under write-back is dirty by definition and a detector watching clean→dirty transitions would watch nothing. **Arming is idempotent in one direction only**: a second window over the same page keeps what the first has seen, since `drain_one_dirty_page` and `reclaim_one_page` can both choose one page and the second clearing the first's record is exactly how a store gets forgotten. The trailing re-protection was **deleted rather than moved** — after the reorder the page has been read-only since before the request, and anything that regranted the write would have shown up as the re-dirty. **The rule was already written down.** `docs/kernel/03` says the pager is sent "a snapshot of the page contents"; a page that can change under the request is not one, so this is the code arriving at the normative sentence rather than a rule invented for it. **A second, smaller inversion of order:** `evict_page` unmapped a page from every process and then asked whether it could be evicted. The refusal is reachable rather than defensive — `reclaim_one_page` writes a dirty page back first, and that request blocks long enough for the page to be dirty again — so a refusal took the mappings away from a page that then stayed resident. It asks first now, and holds the object's frame reference across the unmap so nothing is freed early. Nine tests, five inversions, each failing the test that names its claim: not recording the store fails three, a second window clearing the first's record fails the overlap test, the old evict order fails the refusal test, ignoring the re-dirty on the reply fails the lost-write test, and re-protecting only after the reply fails `a_write_back_takes_the_write_away_before_it_asks`. That last one drives the real `write_back` against a real reply, pre-queued from the peer end the way `call_harness` does. **What is NOT proven: nothing boots this.** The property needs two threads writing one object across a blocking write-back, and every writer in every boot check is alone with its object, so this is held by host tests only — a rare shape here, and the reason the defect survived to be found by reading. The store is placed in the window explicitly because the host round-trip is collapsed; the arrangement is real (two write-backs of one page overlap) but the *timing* is not reproduced. **The same hazard survives on the service-driven path**, untouched: `page_written_back` marks a page clean on a service's say-so, and a store can land after the service read the bytes — there the kernel never held the page still at all. Closing it is a protocol decision rather than a reorder, because nothing today declares when a service *began* reading; the only kernel-visible candidate is the `MemoryDirtyPages` query, which is a query. And **the window is a single-CPU window**: it exists because the call parks a thread and others run. Under SMP the same page is writable on every other CPU at once, and taking the write away stops being a page-table write and becomes one plus a shootdown that must complete before the request can be said to have taken anything (D37 family). 550 kcore tests, 27/27 boot checks on all five ports, arch-lint findings unchanged. | A boot check with two ring-3 writers on one object across a blocking write-back, which is the first thing that would have caught this and the only thing that can hold it; the service-driven path closed once there is a declared instant at which a service starts reading; and the shootdown when SMP makes the window concurrent rather than interleaved. |
| D217 | **Single-core is enforced and counted, not assumed (SMP Phase 0).** Both reference machines now boot with `-smp 2` and the kernel *says* what it is not using: AArch64 counts `/cpus` from the device tree, x86-64 asks the boot protocol, and `kcore::smp` reports present-vs-online plus a `CPU_TOPOLOGY` event, asserted by `claim smp.single` / `claim smp.counted`. Two defects the flag exposed, both invisible at one core and both fixed here. (1) **The GICv2 target register was never written.** It is read-as-zero/write-ignored on a uniprocessor distributor and delivers to the only core there is; with two CPU interfaces it becomes real and resets to a list naming nobody, so every shared peripheral interrupt stopped being delivered — `-smp 2` failed on a device IRQ that never arrived.
