(2) **Asking the boot protocol for a CPU count starts the other cores**, into a wait loop living in memory the same protocol reports usable; the kernel then built its page tables over an instruction stream another core was executing, triple-faulting it tens of thousands of instructions later. `kernel/kernel/src/secondaries.rs` now moves every application processor into kernel text before the first frame is allocated, then onto the kernel's own root once it exists. **What this is not:** no CPU is started, no per-CPU state exists, no IPI and no shootdown — the parked cores `hlt` and nothing dispatches to them. Scope, phases, and the architecture-dependent/independent split are `docs/roadmap/02-smp-bring-up-plan.md`. | Phase 1 (the neutral per-CPU substrate and the `Executive` split) and Phase 2 (bring-up), after which D8's exit criterion is what closes this. |
| D218 | **The AArch64 port starts every CPU the machine has, and they stop at the door (SMP Phase 2).** `CpuOps::hw_id` gives the hardware's own identifier (D218 supersedes the old `cpu_id`, which meant the assigned index on one port and a truncated affinity field on three others), `karch::CpuBringUp` is the porting-layer mechanism for starting a CPU other than the one asking, and AArch64 implements it with PSCI `CPU_ON` — conduit and function identifier read from `/psci`, each CPU's identifier from its `cpu` node's `reg`. The entry stub is the boot stub minus the three things a second CPU must not do: it does not normalize its exception level, does not build page tables, and does not clear `.bss`. It comes up on the coarse boot tables (the only ones addressable with translation off), adopts the kernel's real roots from the high half, takes its vector base and its dense index, and halts. `kcore::smp::start_secondaries` is the neutral driver: it assigns indices, skips the boot CPU by identifier, starts one CPU at a time, waits for each with a bound, and reports — asserted by `claim smp.started`. Verified at 1, 2, 4, and 8 CPUs; inverted two ways (arrival never announced, firmware interface never installed), each of which withholds the claim and names a different cause. **What this is not:** nothing dispatches to the arrived CPUs, there is no IPI, no per-CPU scheduler, and no shootdown — `smp.single` still holds and D8 is untouched. An arriving CPU sets one bit in an atomic bitmap and touches nothing else, because the per-CPU registry's mutable path cannot be shared by convention; the boot CPU records the arrival. x86-64 does not implement the trait yet: its arriving CPUs need a per-CPU block before they can install an index, which is the next Phase 2 item. Above eight CPUs QEMU's `virt` supplies a GICv3 and this port's GICv2 driver faults — true before this change too, and the gate pins `gic-version=2`. | Phase 3: per-CPU schedulers, the reschedule IPI, and the shootdown, after which D8's exit criterion closes both this and D217. |
| D219 | **x86-64 starts its application processors too, and its descriptor tables became per-CPU (SMP Phase 2, completing D218's trait).** The bootloader's per-CPU entry pointer is a one-shot — writing it is what took a core out of the wait loop — so this port's `CpuBringUp` is not a firmware call but a store to a cell the core is already spinning on. The parking stub (D217) grew a third stage: a core claims a slot by atomic increment on arrival, takes a stack at that slot once it is on the kernel's root, and calls into Rust, where it publishes the local-controller id it reads for itself, waits, and on release takes its own GDT/TSS/fault stacks/per-CPU block and syscall MSRs, reports the descriptor-table base it loaded, announces itself, and halts. The bootloader's own list of identifiers is captured before its memory is reclaimed and kept separately, so a start request that matches no core says so instead of starting whichever was first. **Per-CPU GDT/TSS landed here** because a task-state segment holds a CPU's privileged stack and its interrupt-stack-table entries, and two CPUs sharing one take faults onto the same stack; the IDT stays shared, being read-only after init and identical everywhere, with its IST slot numbers resolving through whichever TSS the reading CPU loaded. Arrival proves a CPU loaded *a* table — a bad descriptor triple-faults it first — but not that no two loaded the same, so each CPU reads its table base back out of the hardware and the boot CPU compares: `claim smp.own-tables`, inverted by pinning every CPU to slot zero (the pre-change behaviour). `CPU_TABLE_SLOTS` is the port's own number rather than the configuration's `MAX_CPUS`, because the porting layer does not read kernel configuration; the boot glue sees both and asserts the relationship at compile time. Verified at 1, 4, and 8 CPUs. **What this is not:** nothing dispatches to the arrived CPUs, there is still no IPI (D87 unchanged), no per-CPU scheduler and no shootdown — `smp.single` holds and D8 is untouched. | Phase 3, with D218: per-CPU schedulers, the reschedule IPI, and the shootdown. |
| D220 | **One AArch64 CPU can interrupt another (SMP Phase 2).** `karch::Ipi` takes the kernel's dense index, not a hardware identifier, because a CPU is named three ways on one machine and no two agree — the affinity register it reports for itself, the index the kernel assigns, and a bit position in the interrupt controller's own numbering, which a CPU can only learn by reading a register that answers differently depending on who reads it. Each CPU records its bit on the way up and the GIC module does the translation. `IpiReason` maps to an interrupt id rather than a payload: the controller has ids to spare, and making every recipient read shared state with interrupts masked to find out what it was woken for re-asks a question the controller already answered. **The distributor/CPU-interface split landed with it**, because those registers are banked — the boot CPU cannot enable anyone else's interface, and a CPU whose interface was never enabled takes no interrupt and reports nothing about it. Each arriving CPU now enables its own, records its bit, enables the one id this kernel sends, and only then unmasks. Verified at 1, 4 and 8 CPUs; asserted by `claim smp.ipi-targeted` and `claim smp.ipi-broadcast`, separately because they are different register writes with different addressing. Inverting the index-to-bit translation fails the first and leaves the second passing. **The naming is load-bearing:** a boot check matches a claim as a substring, and the first version used `smp.ipi`, which the broadcast's own line satisfied — the check passed with the targeted send aimed at the wrong CPU until the two names stopped sharing a prefix. **What this is not:** nothing is sent on a scheduling path, the receiving CPU only counts, and no CPU has a run queue to reschedule (D8). x86-64 has no IPI at all — it has no local interrupt controller (D87). | Phase 3 sends these on the cross-core wakeup path; x86-64's half lands with D87. |
| D221 | **The kernel can say how far an invalidate reaches, and prove it (SMP Phase 2).** `AddressSpaceOps::invalidate_local` and `const INVALIDATE_IS_BROADCAST` are on all five ports plus the mock, and each port's `map`/`unmap`/`protect` now invalidate through the trait method rather than beside it — a constant describing a function nothing runs would be worse than no constant. `kcore::vm::invalidate` returns the CPUs still holding a stale entry, which is the whole of what a shootdown has left to do; the decision is a free function so both branches are host-testable without a second architecture. **AArch64 is the only `true`, and ARM 32 is not:** `TLBIALL` is the local form and the inner-shareable one is a different coprocessor operation, so two ports of the same family answer differently — which is why this is a per-port constant. A constant asserted against itself proves nothing, so the AArch64 boot maps a page, has a secondary read it (putting the translation in that CPU's TLB), remaps it while invalidating only on the boot CPU, and asks the same CPU again: `claim smp.invalidate-reaches`. Dropping the `is` from the instruction fails that and nothing else, and QEMU models the shareability domain faithfully enough for the result to be real. **The probe's first version was wrong and four CPUs found it:** it broadcast, and a broadcast IPI has no completion — the boot CPU learned only that *a* CPU answered, unmapped the page, and a slower CPU faulted at the probe address. It targets one CPU now, and waiting for that answer is waiting for all of them. | Phase 3 turns the returned mask into interrupts on the ports where it is non-empty; the mask itself is exact only once D8 closes. |
| D222 | **The x86-64 port's legacy interrupt and timer path is gone (D87's first half, and SMP Phase 2's last mechanism).** The local APIC runs in x2APIC mode — registers as MSRs, so there is no page to map and therefore no uncacheable mapping to get wrong, the command register is one 64-bit write rather than two ordered writes with a status poll between them, and the identifier is 32 bits rather than 8. Device lines arrive through the I/O APIC, whose redirection table this kernel programs, so "line 3 means vector 35" is now a choice rather than an inherited remapping. The tick is the local APIC timer, calibrated against the HPET — the reference D87's own text names for where TSC-deadline is absent, and it is absent under TCG. The 8259 pair is masked once at boot and never written again. **x2APIC is required, not preferred:** a CPU without it fails the boot with a named reason rather than falling back, and `tools/qemu/smoke_boot.sh` names `+x2apic` the way the AArch64 script names `gic-version=2`, so the requirement is in the invocation rather than in a default. `karch::Ipi` is implemented on the command register, completing Phase 2's IPI row; `claim irq.apic`, `claim smp.ipi-targeted` and `claim smp.ipi-broadcast` assert it, verified at 1, 4 and 8 CPUs. Inverting the index-to-identifier translation fails the targeted claim alone; refusing x2APIC fails the boot. **The unclaimed-interrupt count went from seven to zero** — the legacy path had been delivering interrupts nothing owned on every boot, which is what that counter was for. **What remains of D87:** the BIOS half. `kernel/image` still builds a BIOS-bootable ISO and the UEFI path is still untested; that is independent of SMP. | Make the UEFI boot path the tested one and drop the BIOS artifacts from the image. |
| D223 | **The tick is per CPU, in name and in storage (SMP Phase 2 complete).** `TimerControl::start_periodic` became `start_periodic_this_cpu`, and the tick counter became one per CPU with `ticks_on(index)` beside it. Four of the five ports already had a per-CPU tick source — the timer is part of the core on all of them — and only x86-64's was a device for the whole machine; that one exception was enough to make the old name read as a sentence about the machine and the old counter a single number. Each arrived CPU now starts its own tick, and `claim smp.tick-per-cpu` asserts that each one advanced: a machine-wide count would advance on the boot CPU's tick alone, so a secondary whose timer never started would be indistinguishable from one whose did. Leaving a secondary's timer interrupt disabled fails that claim and nothing else. **Only the boot CPU runs the tick hook**, guarded in each port's dispatcher: every CPU ticks and counts its own, but the hook drives the one scheduler this kernel has and a CPU with no run queue has nothing to preempt. Phase 3 removes the guard by giving every CPU something to preempt. With this, every row of the bring-up plan's Phase 2 table is done on both ports — the kernel can start, identify, tick, interrupt and invalidate across CPUs, and uses none of it: the started CPUs halt and `smp.single` still holds. | Phase 3: per-CPU schedulers, the cross-core wakeup on these interrupts, the shootdown on the mask `invalidate` returns, and epoch reclamation. |
| D224 | **A wakeup posted on one CPU reaches another (SMP Phase 3 begins; D17's exit path).** `kcore::wakeup` is a per-CPU **bitmap**, not a queue: a wakeup carries no information beyond which thread, it is idempotent, and it has no useful order because the receiving CPU consults its own run queue anyway — three facts that make the right structure a set, and a set of small integers a bitmap. A queue would need a compare-and-swap to reserve a slot, a published-marker per slot, and a policy for full; a bitmap needs one `fetch_or` to post and one `swap` per word to drain, neither of which can fail, with duplicates collapsing on their own. It is also what keeps the module identical on all five ports: `kcore::atomic::AtomicU64` offers no compare-and-swap because on a 32-bit target it is a pair of words. **The bit is the message and the interrupt is only a prompt**, posted in that order, so a target that misses the prompt still finds the bit. Asserted by `claim smp.wakeup-crosses` on both ports; stopping the target from draining leaves every IPI claim passing and fails this alone, which is what shows the two are not the same check. **What this is not:** there is nothing at the far end to hand the slot to — no CPU but the boot CPU has a run queue (D8), so the drain discards. The mechanism is complete and its consumer is not. | Per-CPU schedulers, so a drained slot names a thread that can then run; then the shootdown (D8) and epoch reclamation (D14). |
| D225 | **A CPU other than the boot CPU runs threads off a run queue of its own — D8's exit.** `kcore::secondary` gives each started CPU a `Scheduler` **on that CPU's own stack**, not in a static array indexed by CPU: the runner never returns, so the scheduler's lifetime is the CPU's, and a local on a stack no other CPU can name is unreachable by construction rather than by convention — `PerCpu`'s borrowing obligation does not arise. One word per CPU is published, a pointer to that scheduler, written by that CPU and dereferenced only by it, because a kernel thread that wants to exit holds no reference to what dispatched it. The boot CPU builds the thread and hands it over, being the only CPU with an address space and an allocator; the handoff is re-checked every pass of the run loop rather than read once, because a secondary reaches its loop before the boot CPU has a mapper — a one-shot read finds nothing and idles for ever with work waiting. Verified at 1, 2, 4 and 8 CPUs on both ports, with all eight coming online; stopping the secondary from taking the handed thread fails `claim smp.second-cpu-runs` and leaves every other cross-CPU claim passing. **`smp.single` is retired**: it said this kernel dispatches to one CPU and that is no longer true. `smp.all-online` replaces it, asserted after bring-up rather than at the survey, because how many CPUs a kernel runs work on is not something a survey taken before bring-up can know. It is a separate claim from `smp.second-cpu-runs` because a machine where one of four CPUs failed to start earns the second and not the first. **What a secondary still does not do:** channels, ports, page faults, syscalls — those are machine-wide tables two CPUs must take turns over, and the turn-taking is next. | The machine-half lock, so a secondary can do IPC; then the shootdown's cross-CPU half on x86-64 and epoch reclamation (D14). |
| D226 | **The recorded memory-safety argument stopped saying "single-threaded", and a gate keeps it from coming back.** Thirty-six inventory entries and 291 in-code `// SAFETY:` comments rested on there being one thread of control — true when written, false since D225. The dangerous case is not the one that became unsound; it is the one that stayed sound *for a different reason* and still says the old one, because a reader auditing it re-derives an argument the code no longer makes. Every claim now names the CPU that reaches the state, or the exclusion that holds. The premise for the bulk of them is one invariant, written down in `kernel-aarch64/src/secondaries.rs`: **no device interrupt reaches a CPU but the boot CPU**, because `gic::enable` writes the calling CPU's own bit into the distributor's target register and the boot CPU is what enables every device line — a secondary enables only its banked timer interrupt and the one software-generated id this kernel sends. The x86-64 I/O APIC routes the same way. Three claims were genuinely stale rather than merely stale-sounding: `Scheduler::switch_to`'s fences (a secondary runs them now — the argument is per-scheduler, and a scheduler belongs to one CPU), and `karch::atomic`'s split `AtomicU64`, whose non-linearizable `fetch_add` is sound *only because* the two 32-bit ports start no second CPU — now recorded as a condition on whoever changes that, not as a fact about the kernel. Rule (e) of the unsafe-inventory gate rejects the phrases in both the manifest and any `SAFETY` comment outside tests. **No code changed:** every edit under `kernel/` is a comment, save two expressions that `rustfmt` reflowed in files the sweep had to touch. The serial logs are identical on both ports, and the 52 bytes that differ in the AArch64 image are `core::panic::Location` records at 24-byte strides — line numbers shifted by the comment lines added, columns unchanged. | Phase 4's remaining half: the justifications that are *unsound* rather than stale once a secondary reaches the machine-wide tables — which is what the executive lock is for. |
| D227 | **An unmap on one CPU reaches the others — D8's shootdown half.** `kcore::shootdown` is a generation, not a queue of addresses: a requester takes the next number and interrupts its targets, each target drops every translation it has and publishes the number it reached, and the requester waits until every target's is at least its own. A per-address queue would need a bound, an overflow policy, and a decision about what a target does on overflow — whose only correct answer is to drop everything anyway, so starting there gives one behaviour instead of two, and makes a late target harmless: a CPU servicing two requests as one satisfies both, correctly. `IpiReason::TlbShootdown` is its own id rather than a flag inside the reschedule, because a reschedule is advisory and this has a sender blocked on the answer. **The active-core mask was worse than the plan assumed.** A CPU joins it by calling `AddressSpace::activate`, and a secondary adopts the kernel tables in its entry stub before any such object exists — so for the kernel space it named the boot CPU alone and *under*-reported, which loses a shootdown rather than wasting one. A space every CPU runs on is now marked as such and its target set is the online CPUs, exact and needing no bookkeeping; other spaces still use the mask and over-report. On x86-64 the flush is a `CR3` reload with `CR4.PGE` cleared across it — the kernel's pages are global so they survive a plain reload, which would leave exactly the mapping a kernel-range shootdown is about. Asserted by `claim smp.shootdown`; telling nobody leaves the other CPU reading the old frame. AArch64 needs no counterpart: its set is empty by construction, which `smp.invalidate-reaches` (D221) already shows. | Epoch reclamation (D14), and the executive lock so a secondary can reach the machine-wide tables at all. |
| D228 | **The mandated epoch-reclamation facility exists (D14's half, SMP Phase 3).** `kcore::epoch` is the quiescent-state flavour rather than the pinned-epoch one, which is what lets `docs/kernel/08`'s "a handle check on the syscall path performs no shared writes" be true: a reader takes no atomic and writes nothing another CPU reads, and only refrains from reporting itself quiescent while it holds a reference. A writer bumps a counter and waits for every **online** CPU to quiesce at or after it; offline CPUs are not waited for, and could not be, since one that has never run holds nothing and waiting for it would stall reclamation on every machine with a spare slot. A CPU coming online attaches at the current value rather than at zero, so it does not make every grace period since boot look unfinished. An idle CPU is quiescent by construction, so the secondaries' run loop is where it goes — nothing is in hand there and the declaration is one store. Asserted by `claim smp.grace-period` on both ports, across real CPUs idling in their own loops. **The inversion found a defect in the check rather than in the facility:** holding a read section open on a secondary must fail the claim, and with the arrival bound it made the boot CPU spin longer than the harness allows the whole run, so the check timed out instead of reporting. A bounded wait whose bound outlives the run is not a bounded wait; the grace period has its own, sized against the success path. **What remains of D14:** the lock-free multi-core handle table that rides this. | The handle table on this facility, which needs the executive lock first. |
| D229 | **An interrupt reaches its target and only its target, and the boot checks run four CPUs (SMP Phase 5).** `claim smp.ipi-targeted` says every CPU that was named took one, which is also what a send that names nobody and wakes the whole machine earns — on AArch64 one misplaced bit in `GICD_SGIR`, on x86-64 the command register's shorthand. Acknowledgement counting cannot see that defect, so the round now reads every CPU's counter once for the whole round and requires each to end **exactly one** higher: extra interrupts become the ones nobody was sent, and they have nowhere to hide. `smp.ipi-only-target` is its own claim because it is a different question about the same write, and inverting either port's send to a broadcast fails it while leaving `smp.ipi-targeted` passing — which is what shows the two claims are not the same check. **The check and `-smp 4` are one change, not two.** With one other CPU there is no address left to get wrong, so the kernel withholds the claim below three rather than earning it vacuously, and a run at `-smp 2` fails on the missing marker. Both smoke checks moved; the other twenty-five boot checks still run a single CPU, so nothing gives up that path. **A stray needs waiting out.** Nothing paces an interrupt nobody waited for: counting the moment the last target answered found one stray where a settle of an eighth of the delivery budget found three. The settled figure is still what the machine did rather than what the defect implies — three sends waking three CPUs is nine by arithmetic and was six on both ports, because a controller holding this interrupt pending for a CPU from this sender does not queue a second. That is why the claim turns on the count being zero and not on it matching a prediction. **What this is not:** a broadcast has no such check. Its own risk is the opposite one — reaching a CPU the kernel has no index for — and no counter of the kernel's can see a CPU it does not know about. | The remaining Phase 5 cases: a channel call crossing cores, which needs the executive lock, and D36's cross-core benchmarks. |
| D230 | **Thirteen threads hold a `&mut` to the executive at once, and the boot says so — measured before the lock, not after.** The recorded argument was that the executive is "re-borrowed per operation" and that "there is never more than one live borrow in flight". `kcore::exec::occupancy` counts them, and the second half is false: the blocking methods suspend the calling thread *inside* their own `&mut self`, so a server parked in `receive` or `reply_receive` holds its borrow for as long as it is parked — which is for ever. Both ports end a boot with **thirteen live borrows and a high-water mark of fourteen**, in different distributions (2/6/4/1 across receive, reply, reply_receive and wait_on_address on x86-64; 7/1/2/3 across receive, receive_any, reply and port_wait on AArch64), which is what shows the equal totals are a coincidence and the mechanism real. This is aliasing `&mut`, which the language forbids; it is sound in practice only because a suspended frame reads nothing until it resumes and one CPU runs one thread at a time. **The finding changes the lock's design rather than confirming it.** The prediction from reading `call` was a transient nesting of two during a round trip, which a lock could have been dropped in around. Thirteen permanent holders means a guard taken on entry to `receive` would be held by every parked server, so the *first* one to park would stop the machine — the machine-half lock cannot go at the method boundary at all. It has to sit inside those methods, around each access to the machine-wide tables, released before the thread parks. **`claim exec.one-cpu` is the observable the lock will be judged by.** What keeps the unlocked tables consistent today is that no CPU but the boot CPU reaches them — a sentence that was unchecked until now, and exactly the class D226 found stale elsewhere. Letting a secondary call `note_visit` reports four CPUs and fails that marker and nothing else, on both ports. The claim means the same thing before and after the lock, so it outlives the deviation it currently records. `EXEC_OCCUPANCY` (kind 51) carries the same numbers as an event. **What this is not:** no lock, and no behaviour change — the counters are instrumentation, and the eight suspending methods are the only places instrumented. | The lock itself, inside those methods rather than around them; then D14's handle table and D17's cross-CPU call. |
| D231 | **The machine half of the executive became a type, and cost 12 KiB less rather than 428 KiB more.** Phase 1 of the SMP plan tried this and backed out: naming `Machine` created a nested initializer whose temporary is over 400 KiB, which overflowed a host test's stack and hung the AArch64 kernel at 28 lines of boot; a `const` constructor avoided the temporary at a measured +428 KiB of image, and the plan recorded "the machine half becomes a type when it is small enough to be one". Both halves of that objection turned out to be about *where the constant lives*, not about its size. `const { Machine::new() }` forces the evaluation to compile time and the stack overflow goes away — the exact test that used to abort now passes. And putting the machine half in a `static` rather than in a field means the constant **is** the storage: nothing is copied into anything, the field-by-field construction code `Executive::new` used to emit disappears, and the image ends up **12,288 bytes smaller** (`memmap` agrees: three more usable frames). The +424 KiB reproduced exactly when it was a field, so the earlier measurement was right about the arrangement it measured. **One machine, which is what a machine is.** A second `Executive` never brought a second set of channels with it in any meaningful sense; it only looked that way. The one place that mattered is that the boot re-creates the executive between demos — dozens of times on AArch64 — and relied on that to clear the tables. `Machine::reset` does it explicitly, field by field rather than `*self = Machine::new()`, because the whole-value form needs the 400 KiB constant to exist somewhere to be copied from and that is the +424 KiB again. Removing the reset fails **25 of 27 boot checks**; the two that survive are the ports with no executive. **Host tests keep their own.** Under `cfg(test)` the tables are a field, because the harness runs tests in parallel threads and each has to be able to assume its channels start empty — and kcore is `no_std`, so there is no thread-local to reach for. The two builds differ in one field and one accessor. **Proved by the serial log, not by byte-identity.** All 39 AArch64 claims are identical before and after, the occupancy numbers are unchanged at 14/13 with the same per-method breakdown, and the only other differences are the smaller image in `memmap` and perf timing variance. **What this is not:** no lock. This gives the lock something to own, which is what D230 said it needed. | The lock itself, around each region of machine access in the nine suspending methods. |
| D232 | **The executive's machine-wide tables have a lock, and a check that says nobody parked holding it.** `kcore::machine_lock` is **re-entrant**, because the two things that have to be true at once pull in opposite directions: the section that matters is the *method* — enqueue a request, set the pending caller, signal an arrival is one update another CPU must not see half of — while the tables are reached from 173 places, most of them one expression inside such a method. So a method takes a hold at its boundary and the accesses inside take nested ones that only bump a depth. That is 130 one-line insertions instead of auditing 173 call sites for which may release. **The rule the type cannot express is the whole difficulty.** D230 measured thirteen threads suspended inside executive methods at the end of a boot; a hold that survived one of those parks is a hold nobody ever releases. `machine_lock::park` puts this CPU's *whole* depth down, runs the parking operation, and picks it back up where the thread next runs. `Executive::run` is the one method deliberately given no hold at all — it is the dispatcher and does not return, so a hold there would span every thread it dispatches, which is the same failure in its purest form. **The check is at the park, not at the release.** `Scheduler::block_current` and `handoff_to` call `machine_lock::assert_released`, so a park that was never routed through `park` is *counted* rather than assumed away — a facility that only checked the parks it was told about would report zero for exactly the bug it exists to find. It needs no second CPU to be useful: with the 130 holds in and before the eleven parks were converted it read **119**, and un-converting the single handoff in `call` reads **47 parks and 37 contended holds** and fails `claim exec.lock-released-at-park` alone. The first inversion tried was `reply`'s handoff and correctly changed nothing — `reply` delegates its table work to `deliver_reply`, which takes and releases its own hold, so by the time it hands off it holds nothing. That is the shape the design wants, and it was found by the check disagreeing with the guess. **Uncontended by construction while `exec.one-cpu` holds**, so `acquire` is a store and not a test-and-set — `kcore::atomic::AtomicU64` has no compare-and-swap to offer, since on a 32-bit target it is a word pair. The contention count is what would say that assumption had stopped holding. Proved by the serial log: the only differences on AArch64 are the two lines this adds. **What this is not:** no CPU but the boot CPU takes it yet. | The cross-core channel call, which is the first thing that contends it. |
| D233 | **The executive has one scheduler per CPU, and nothing yet can tell.** `Executive` held a single `CpuLocal` — a run queue, a transaction counter, and two per-thread arrays — so a secondary calling `Executive::call` would have used the *boot CPU's* scheduler to decide who was running and who to block. It now holds one per CPU, reached by `Executive::cpu` on the index of whoever is asking, the same way `Executive::machine` reaches the shared half. No signature changed and no port changed: 69 accesses became a call. **The split is lopsided and that is the point.** `CpuLocal` measures 2,024 bytes against `Machine`'s 446,120 — eight CPUs' worth of per-CPU state is 16 KiB, which is 0.4% of one executive. Almost everything in an executive is shared, which is why the shared part is the one that needed a lock (D232) and the per-CPU part needs none. `docs/kernel/08`'s "per-CPU by default" is cheap here precisely because so little qualifies. **The inversion does not discriminate, and that is the finding.** Making `cpu()` ignore its index and always answer with the boot CPU's half leaves all 576 host tests and all 27 boot checks passing, because no CPU but the boot CPU reaches the executive yet (`claim exec.one-cpu`). What *is* pinned is the indexing itself: `cpu_at` is split out so a host test can ask for CPU 1's half without installing a per-CPU index source — that source is one process-wide store, and a test pointing it at CPU 1 would move every other parallel test's per-CPU state with it. That test holds two CPUs' halves apart and pins the out-of-range fold to the boot CPU rather than a panic; inverting either fails it. **Proved as a refactor:** every AArch64 claim is identical and the serial log differs in nothing but the image size and perf variance. **What this is not:** no CPU but the boot CPU asks yet. This is the piece a cross-core call needs and cannot supply its own proof of. | The secondary running its thread through the executive, which is what makes `cpu()` answer differently and retires `exec.one-cpu`. |
| D234 | **The machine lock actually excludes, and the thing blocking its first contender is the boot's own habit.** D232's `acquire` was a plain store: correct while `claim exec.one-cpu` held, and a silent double-entry the moment it did not, because a store cannot fail and so cannot notice somebody else is inside. It is a `compare_exchange` now, on `core::sync::atomic::AtomicU32` rather than `kcore::atomic::AtomicU64` — that type exists because two of the five targets have no 64-bit atomic instruction, and what it cannot offer as a result is exactly the compare-and-swap a lock *is*. Thirty-two bits is not a compromise: every target has a 32-bit atomic, a CPU index fits in a byte, and `kcore::atomic` exists for 64-bit *values* whose width is part of an ABI, which an owner word is not. Waiters spin read-only until the word looks free, and contention is counted once per wait rather than once per spin. **Tested through the word, not through threads.** A thread-based test cannot express this: the lock is re-entrant *per CPU*, and every host thread answers `current_index()` with the boot CPU, so two of them would both be let in — correctly — and the test would be measuring the re-entrancy instead of the exclusion. Reverting `try_take` to a store fails it. **What it revealed about the next step.** A secondary cannot yet use the executive's scheduler, and not for want of a lock: the boot re-creates the executive between demos — **35 sites on AArch64, 24 on x86-64, 8 on RISC-V 64** — and since D233 each of those rebuilds *every* CPU's half, which would wipe a running secondary's run queue dozens of times a boot. The per-CPU array has to stop living inside a value the boot replaces. It cannot simply move to a `static`: `Scheduler::new` calls `C::empty()`, a trait method, so it is not `const` and the array cannot be const-initialized — which makes this a fourth boot-installed hook alongside the event clock, the interrupt control and the per-CPU index source, exactly the fourth `kcore::percpu`'s header said would be worth gathering them for. | That hook, then `Executive::new` resetting only the calling CPU's half, then the secondary. |
| D235 | **The boot restarts the executive instead of replacing it, so a secondary's scheduling state can survive a demo.** Seventy sites across three ports built a fresh `Executive` between demos and dropped the old one — the cheapest way to get a clean start, and harmless until D233 gave each CPU its own half. From then on a fresh executive brought fresh halves for *every* CPU, so a secondary running out of its own would have had its run queue rebuilt underneath it dozens of times a boot. `Executive::restart` clears the machine tables and returns the **calling** CPU's half to its starting state, leaving every other CPU's alone. That is also the more honest reading of what a demo wants: it is restarting its own scheduling, not the machine's other processors. **One helper per port rather than seventy edits to the shape of the code.** Each port grew an `unsafe fn ..._restart(quantum)` that restarts the executive if it exists and creates it if it does not, and each site's expression was swapped for a call — every `unsafe` block and every `// SAFETY:` comment left exactly as it was, because the helper is `unsafe` too and the obligation is unchanged. The one hazard was mechanical: the substitution matched inside the helpers it had just written, turning each `None` arm into a call to itself. **Two measurements fell out.** The AArch64 image is **16,384 bytes smaller** — sixty-seven inlined constructions, each building eight `CpuLocal`s, became three. And the boot stack stopped carrying a 16 KiB temporary at every one of those sites. Proved as a refactor: every AArch64 claim identical and no other serial-log difference. The host test holds a value in CPU 1's half across a restart and finds it intact; making `restart` rebuild every half — the behaviour this replaces — fails it. **This is also the check D233 could not supply for itself**, since a `cpu()` that ignored its index would put CPU 1's value in the boot CPU's half and lose it. | The secondary running its thread through the executive, which is now safe to attempt. |
| D236 | **A CPU other than the boot CPU dispatches out of the executive, and `exec.one-cpu` is retired by being made false.** `kcore::secondary` gave each started CPU a `Scheduler` on that CPU's own stack (D225) — unreachable by construction, and unreachable by the executive too, which is what made it wrong: `Executive::call` asks *this CPU's* scheduler who is running and who to block, and finds it through `Executive::cpu`, so a secondary doing IPC out of a scheduler the executive had never heard of would block a thread in one run queue and look for it in another. There is no way to hold two in step; they have to be the same object. The runner now takes the executive and uses the half its index names, which is what D233 built one per CPU for. **The trade is named rather than glossed:** D225's guarantee was about addresses — no other CPU can name a local on my stack — and what replaces it is a convention, since `cpu_at` will hand any index to anyone. What the convention buys is the check. **Where the check had to go.** `smp.second-cpu-runs` cannot tell the two arrangements apart: a thread that ran advances the same counter either way. So each CPU publishes the scheduler it actually dispatched from — `Executive::scheduler`, selected by its own index — and the boot CPU compares that against `scheduler_at(index)`, the half that index owns. Both mistakes fail it: a stack-local run queue publishes a stack address (**0/3**, `claim exec.second-cpu-scheduled` withheld and nothing else), and an `Executive::cpu` that ignored its index publishes the boot CPU's half from every CPU — **which does not merely fail the claim, it kills the boot**, three secondaries dispatching out of one run queue taking an instruction abort at address zero. That second inversion is **D233's own**, which D233 recorded as not discriminating; it discriminates now, and violently. **Two claims, and the first is an inversion of a retired one.** `exec.multi-cpu` replaces `exec.one-cpu` rather than re-reading it: "one CPU" and "more than one" are different sentences, and a new key stops an old log being mistaken for a new kernel's. Both ports report **4 CPUs inside the executive, 3/3 secondaries dispatching out of their own half, 6 switches** — two per CPU, in and out again. Withheld on a uniprocessor, where one is what there is to be. **What made it safe was D235, and what it needed on top was one line of ordering.** The executive is built once, before any CPU is started, instead of lazily by whichever check wanted it first; every later call restarts the one that exists, and only the branch that builds one touches another CPU's half. A secondary adopts *its* half through `Executive::adopt_cpu` — split out of `restart` precisely because `restart` also clears the machine tables, which belong to the machine and are in use by the boot CPU at the moment an arriving CPU runs. A host test pins the difference by binding a channel object, adopting, and finding it still there. **What this is not:** still no contention — **0 contended holds** on both ports — because a secondary reaches only its own per-CPU half and `machine_lock` covers the shared one. The cross-core channel call is what will take it. | The remote-wake paths, where `Scheduler::index_of` returns `None` for a thread that lives on another CPU rather than one that exited (`call`, `reply`/`deliver_reply`) — after which a secondary can be a callee, which is the first contender for the lock. |
| D237 | **A synchronous channel call whose callee is on another CPU, and the wake path that makes it possible.** Every wake site in `kcore::exec` resolved an identity to a local slot with `Scheduler::index_of` and read `None` as "that thread exited" — true while one CPU ran everything, and silently wrong the moment a thread could be somewhere else: the wake simply did not happen and the thread went on waiting for an event that had already come. `Executive::locate_thread` returns **three** answers where `index_of` returns two, and `Residence::Elsewhere` is the one that was missing. **Where the map lives.** A machine-wide `Residents` table inside `Machine`, so it is reached under `machine_lock` like everything else shared — plain fields, no atomics — and so a host suite running tests in parallel threads gets one per test instead of one per process. A `ThreadId` already carries the CPU that minted it, so the lookup is one row of sixteen and not a scan of the machine; a full scan would also keep working after threads started migrating, which is the wrong kind of robustness. **The ordering took a deadlock to see, and it is not where it looks like it belongs.** A method publishes this thread's identity into a machine-wide table — a blocked receiver, a pending caller — under one hold of the lock, and parks under a later one. A whereabouts record written *at the park* is therefore behind the identity: another CPU that reads the identity in between finds no record, resolves it to "exited", and does not wake it, while the thread parks for ever on a request already in its queue. So the record is made by `Executive::enter_blocking`, at the method's first line, paired with the `occupancy` marker that already names exactly the set of methods that can suspend a thread. **A slot is not enough at the far end either.** The bitmap is keyed by slot, and a slot is reused the moment its occupant is reaped, so a wakeup taken after that would make a *stranger* runnable — `index_of`'s staleness arriving by a different road. Each bit now carries the identity it was posted for, stored before the bit; `Scheduler::unblock_thread` checks it. Still a store and a `fetch_or` to post, still no compare-and-swap. `kcore::wakeup::install_prompt` is how the executive sends the interrupt without naming a port's `Ipi` type — the same shape `percpu::install_index_source` has. **Two defects found on the way, both invisible before D236.** The IPI handler on both ports drained the wakeup bitmap and threw the slots away ("nothing to hand them to yet"); with secondaries dispatching, that *loses* wakeups — and draining them properly there is worse, since it reaches a run queue from an interrupt that may have landed inside a context switch. It does not drain at all now; the run loop it returns to does. And the boot CPU was the one CPU on the machine that never collected its own wakeups: `Executive::run` drains at the top of each pass. **The check counts crossings, not the answer.** A round trip completes identically with both ends on one CPU — which is what every other IPC check in this tree does — so `claim exec.cross-cpu-call` requires the request *and* the reply to have crossed, and the boot waits for the server to register itself before calling, or the request is merely queued and only one direction crosses. Both ports: request and reply arrive, **exactly 2 crossings**, server on CPU 1 (x86-64) and CPU 2 (AArch64) — claimed by whichever secondary gets there first rather than a hard-coded index. **The machine lock has its first contention**: 2 holds on AArch64, 4 on x86-64, against 0 before. **One inversion discriminates and one does not, and the second is the honest half.** Making `locate_thread` never answer `Elsewhere` fails `exec.cross-cpu-call` alone, with the boot line naming the cause (`request LOST, reply LOST, 0 wakeup(s) crossed`) — and it first exposed a defect in the *check*, whose pump had borrowed the arrival spin count for a bound where one iteration is a whole scheduling pass, turning a failed check into a two-minute hang. Moving the residence record back to the park **passes the boot check three times running**: the window is real but far too narrow for QEMU to land in, especially with the boot waiting for the receiver first. A host test sees it instead, by receiving a message that is already queued — a `receive` that never parks records nothing under the inversion and records correctly under the fix. **What this is not:** priority inheritance does not cross CPUs — it is a per-CPU scheduler operation, so a remote callee runs at its own priority (D18's seam, widened and named). Causality does cross, in the message header. Cross-CPU *termination* is untouched: `kill_job` still finds only local threads. | D14's handle table, D15's counter sharding, and the per-CPU event rings (D57) — the shared-state debt a second CPU doing IPC makes real; then priority inheritance across CPUs. |
| D238 | **The counter that could not be atomic is three types now, and one of them is a sum of per-CPU parts (SMP Phase 4).** `karch::atomic::AtomicU64` offered a `fetch_add` that the split representation could not honestly provide: the low half is incremented atomically and the carry is a *second* operation, so a reader between the two sees a value 2^32 short. It was recorded as tolerable because the two targets without a 64-bit atomic are the two 32-bit ports and neither starts a second CPU — a condition about the machine standing in for a property of a type any neutral code could reach. **Split by what the counter is for.** `AtomicU64` keeps load/store/swap/`fetch_or` — every one honest split in two — and has no arithmetic, so the carry window cannot arise. `CpuCounter` is a counter one CPU increments: split-safe by construction, and it returns no previous value, because a counter with one writer has nobody to race and returning it would invite a read-modify-write built from two operations. `SharedCounter` is a counter any CPU increments, serialized on a sequence word where the target has no 64-bit atomic. **The plan predicted that last one "simply does not exist on a target that cannot implement it"; it can, and the reason was found later** — D234 established while looking for a lock word that a 32-bit compare-and-swap is available everywhere. Non-existence would have been the wrong answer anyway: `kcore` compiles for all five targets, so a type missing on two is a type `kcore` cannot use. **And a third kind the plan had as a separate line item.** `SharedCounter`'s sequence word carries a rule — a writer must not be interrupted by another writer of the same counter — and several of these tallies are bumped from interrupt paths (a dropped console write, a contended lock, an unstamped event). Those become `kcore::counter::Sharded`: one `CpuCounter` per CPU, summed on read. That is Phase 4's "counters shard per CPU with lazy aggregation" arriving as the *answer to* the atomic question rather than beside it. Twenty sites were classified; the compiler asked, because the unsound operation stopped existing. **A defect found while classifying, and it was live.** `kcore::exec::occupancy` counted threads per blocking method with one `AtomicU64` per site raised by a load and a store — not an atomic read-modify-write. Two CPUs entering `receive` at the same moment both read *n* and both store *n+1*. Unreachable until D237 put a secondary into the executive; reachable now. It is two monotonic `CpuCounter`s per (CPU, site) and a subtraction, and the AArch64 boot reports the same 7/1/2/3 distribution across receive/receive_any/reply/port_wait it did before. **The check for a race no single-threaded test can see.** The split `SharedCounter` is hammered by real threads from `kcore`'s test build — `karch` is `no_std` and has no threads, so the protocol is exported `#[doc(hidden)]` to be tested from a crate that has them. Every add wraps the low half (the addend is `u32::MAX`), so the carry window is open on essentially every operation instead of once per 2^32; a reader thread asserts the counter never goes backwards. Restoring the two-half `fetch_add` fails it **3 runs out of 3**. Inverting `Sharded::total` to read one shard fails its own test — and notably **does not** change the boot's contention line, because the CPU that waits for the machine lock is the boot CPU, whose shard is the one a broken total would read. **What this is not:** no behaviour change intended and none observed — the AArch64 boot differs in nothing but image size and perf variance. | D37's wait-on-address (per-bucket locking and physical-frame keying) and D57's per-CPU event rings, the two Phase 4 items left; then D15's object refcounts. |
| D239 | **The event ring is one per CPU, and the order is rebuilt on the way out (SMP Phase 4; D57's exit criterion).** `kcore::event` kept one `SpinLock<EventRing>` for the machine. What that cost was never correctness — the lock was always taken — but two things a shared lock always costs under SMP: every CPU emitting contends for one cache line, and a CPU waiting for it waits **with interrupts masked**, because that is what `SpinLock` does. Events are emitted on the busiest paths in the kernel, which makes it the worst possible place for a machine-wide rendezvous. `docs/kernel/08` asks for per-CPU rings directly and D57 wrote it down as its own exit. **What one ring gave for free was ordering, and that is what had to be rebuilt.** Every record has carried a timestamp since D57, so `drain` and `tail` merge the rings oldest-first rather than concatenating them — a tie goes to the lower CPU index, which leaves each ring's own order intact. Concatenating is the obvious wrong answer and it is worse than it looks: with the output buffer shorter than the total it drops the *later CPUs entirely* instead of the globally newest. `tail` starts each ring at its own last `out.len()` records, because a record in the globally newest `n` must be within the newest `n` of the ring it came from — without that bound a crash dump walks a whole boot's emission to render a dozen lines. **Tested against pollution rather than around it.** The rings are process-wide and the host harness runs tests in parallel threads, so the check writes into rings 2 and 3 — which nothing else in a host test reaches, since every thread answers `current_index()` with the boot CPU — stamps its records with a cookie, and asserts only on those. Whatever another test emitted meanwhile is data. Interleaving four records across two rings so ring order and time order *disagree* is what makes it discriminate: draining ring-by-ring returns 1,3,2,4 and fails, and a `tail` that reads only the boot CPU's ring answers 5,7 where the machine's two newest are 6,7. Both inversions fail that test and nothing else. **The cost is 192 KiB of RAM**, measured: eight rings of `EVENT_RING_CAPACITY` where there was one, and seven of them nearly empty because a secondary emits a handful of records. Sizing them differently was considered and rejected — it needs two ring types to put in one array, and it bakes in which CPU does the work, which is the assumption this phase exists to remove. **What this is not:** no behaviour change; the AArch64 boot differs in the image size, in which CPU happened to serve the cross-call, and in perf variance. | D37's wait-on-address — the last Phase 4 item — and then Phase 5. |
| D240 | **Wait-on-address reads the word under the lock that enrolls, and keys on the memory rather than on a name for it (SMP Phase 4 complete).** Two defects, one entry, because they are the same sentence from different ends. **The compare was outside every lock.** The syscall entry read the futex word and passed the value in; the executive compared it and enrolled. Between those two another CPU can write the word and wake the key — finding nobody enrolled — after which this call parks a thread on a condition that has already been signalled, for ever. D37 recorded it as "atomic only by single-core cooperative execution", which was true and stopped being true at D237. The entry now supplies a **way to read** rather than a value, and the executive calls it inside the same hold of `machine_lock` that enrolls. kcore still never dereferences a user pointer: the closure is the entry's own validated read, invoked at the moment that makes it mean anything. **The plan asked for "a per-bucket lock or preempt-disable" and neither was the missing piece** — the lock has been there since D232; what was missing was doing the compare under it. Bucket granularity is left undone deliberately: `MAX_WAITERS` is a small flat pool with no buckets, and sharding an unmeasured lock is speculation. **The key named the address instead of the memory.** `(address-space root, virtual address)` names the same word differently in every process that maps it, so two processes sharing a page could not wake each other and one process mapping a page twice could not wake itself through the other mapping. A futex is a lock on a *word of memory*, and the only name every holder agrees on is where that word physically is: the key is `(frame, offset)` now, supplied by the caller — kcore does not translate, for the same reason it does not read. A property does go: the old key looked like an ambient capability check, in that a waker needed the target's root. It never was one — the root was a number the caller passed — and a real one belongs in the handle table. **The demo is the proof and it was already written.** The ring-3 futex check has a *kernel* thread wake a word in a *user* process; before this, that only worked because the demo told the kernel waker the process's root and hard-coded the user's virtual address into it. Keying physically, the two sides agree without either being in the other's address space, and `WAIT_DEMO_SPACE` — a static that existed only to carry that coupling — is gone. Making `futex_key` skip the translation fails the boot with `wait-demo: FAIL woken=false wake_count=0`, and nothing else. The read-under-lock half is pinned by a host test whose read closure asserts `machine_lock::held_here()`; moving the read above the hold fails it. **What this is not:** still no deadline on a blocking wait, no bitset, no requeue, no guaranteed FIFO among waiters on one key, and no owner-aware lock with priority inheritance — the rest of D37 stands. | Phase 5: the cross-core benchmarks D36 wants, and the verification end of the plan. |
| D241 | **A log line's width stops depending on what was measured.** The boot checks reject any rendered line over 150 characters and `//tools/checks:logging` enforces the same bound on format strings — but the static gate cannot see interpolated values, which is why the runtime one exists, and nothing bounded a value's width. AArch64's `perf: B7 ctx-switch` line sat at **147** with `max=84064ns`. That maximum is a fact about the *host's* scheduler, not the kernel's: under load it gained a digit, the line tipped past 150, and the check failed on a different random subset of boots each run — observed at 2, 6 and 9 failures in one session, and reproducible at HEAD. A gate whose subject is a measurement is not a gate. Two changes, and neither is more headroom. The line ended in `(2 switches/rt, QEMU-only)` — 26 characters of prose, which is what the logging gate's own error message means by "the prose belongs in a comment"; both facts were already stated three lines above it in the source. And the five durations render through **`kcore::bench::Nanos`**, which is never wider than nine characters for any `u64`: exact nanoseconds below ten milliseconds, because `1872ns` is the number a reader acts on and `1.9us` is a different one; scaled beyond that, where the value is an outlier from outside the kernel and precision in it buys nothing; and past ten thousand seconds reported as `>9999s`, because a benchmark sample that long is not a measurement and clamping it silently would say it was. **147 → 123 measured**, and the worst case is now arithmetic rather than luck: 32 fixed characters, five fields of nine, and the console envelope. The bound is checked directly — the widest value in each range is rendered and its length asserted — so making `Display` print raw nanoseconds again fails on `u64::MAX` at 22 characters. **What this does not fix, and it is worth knowing:** the next-longest lines are `relay:` at 147 on AArch64 and `m16-step4:` at 145 on x86-64. Both are bounded by construction — their interpolated fields are masked or hex-formatted — but three characters of headroom is not a margin, and the console envelope's own timestamp grows over a boot. | Phase 5: D36's cross-core benchmarks. |
| D242 | **The cross-core synchronous call is measured, against a same-core baseline taken in the same breath (SMP Phase 5; B24 and its B3 comparand).** D36 has said since M-early that B5/B24 "need SMP"; the call landed in D237 and this measures it. Two hundred cross-CPU round trips through the same resident server the cross-core *check* uses — the check proves the mechanism, then the same pair times it — with `reply_receive` at the far end, because a bare `reply` blocks the replier and a server looping back to its own receive hangs after one exchange (D85, D91). **The absolute microseconds are the emulator's, so the deliverable is the ratio.** `docs/prototypes/01` asks for B24 "reported alongside BM-3 so the same-core/cross-core ratio is tracked explicitly — growth in that ratio is the signal that services need sharding". Measured sequentially, that ratio read **1.5x, 2.9x, 3.1x** on three runs of one image: an emulated machine's host does not hold still for the length of a boot. **Interleaved** — one cross-core sample, then one same-core sample, all the way through — nine of twelve runs land in **2.2–2.4x** against a budgeted 3.0x, with outliers at 1.5 and 2.6. Tighter, not tight: the two paths inflate differently under load, because a cross-core round trip waits on the far CPU coming out of `wfi` and a same-core one does not. That is the honest reading and it is what the line says. **A percentile cannot say what it measured**, so `claim perf.cross-call-crossed` counts the wakeups that actually crossed. The same-core half must cross **none** — it hands off directly — and a "cross-core" benchmark that had quietly run both ends on one CPU reports plausible numbers and zero here. The cross half is asserted **between once and twice per round trip, and the range is the finding**: the exact-two form held 400/400 on a quiet machine and read **399** under load, because a `call` posts a wakeup only if its callee is *already* parked — a server that has replied but not yet re-registered gets its request queued instead, and nothing is lost. The reply direction cannot be raced away (D240 records the caller's whereabouts before the request is enqueued), so at-least-once is the invariant and exactly-twice would have been a check that passes idle and fails busy. **Two predictions, both wrong, both measured.** The boot CPU's wait was assumed to be dominated by `Executive::run`'s port and page-in bookkeeping — a tight drain-and-dispatch loop was written for it and measured **identical** (923k vs 939k counter ticks), so it was removed: what dominates is the far CPU's wake, not this one's polling. And the local server's completion count was stored after its final `reply`, which hands the CPU to a local caller and blocks the replier — the flag never landed and the boot hung. Third encounter with that shape, first where the thread was *finishing* rather than looping. Costs **0.28 s** of boot (8.61 → 8.89 s) and takes the machine lock's contention from 2–4 holds to **~640**, which is the first time anything has leaned on it. Inverting `wakeup::wake` to post without counting withholds this claim *and* D237's, each naming the cause on its own line. **What this is not:** B5 (cross-core notify), and B19–B21, which need a port waiter on a secondary and several threads per CPU respectively. | B5, then the scaling condition. |
| D243 | **The cross-core notification is measured too — budget B5, and it is the steadiest number on the machine (SMP Phase 5).** BM-5's shape: a thread parked on a port on one CPU, a signal from another, and the two timestamps taken on *different CPUs* — the sender stamps before `port_signal`, the waiter the instant `port_wait` hands it an event. Subtracting them is meaningful only because AArch64's `CNTVCT_EL0` is one counter for the whole machine, which is why the clock is passed in rather than read in `kcore`. **The sender waits for the waiter to be registered before it signals, and that wait is the benchmark.** A port coalesces: a signal arriving while nobody waits is remembered, and the next `port_wait` returns from the queue. Removing the wait produces a *faster-looking* result — p50 **13.5 µs against 37 µs** — while crossing a CPU **7 times out of 200** instead of 200. That is the whole argument for counting crossings rather than trusting microseconds, demonstrated rather than asserted: the wrong measurement is the one that looks better. `Executive::port_has_drainer` is the observable it waits on, the port's counterpart to D240's `endpoint_receiver` and there for the same reason — a CPU cannot read another CPU's scheduler, but a port is machine state. **Exactly one crossing per notification, and here that really is invariant**, unlike D242's round trip where the request direction can lose its crossing to a race. The sender has already established that a drainer is registered and that drainer is necessarily elsewhere. **Three runs: p50 37.7, 37.9, 39.3 µs — ±2%, against B24's 63–98 µs over the same runs.** The difference is the return leg: a B24 sample ends when the *boot* CPU notices the reply, and B5's ends the moment the far CPU stamps. That also cross-validates the two: twice B5 is ~76 µs and B24's median is 71–98, so a round trip is about two one-way wakes plus the call's own work — two independently built benchmarks agreeing on the mechanism between them. **Two Stage-0 gaps, stated rather than papered over:** BM-5 wants the waiter running in *user space* and the event to be a device interrupt; this waiter is a kernel thread and the event is a bare signal, because a secondary has no address space of its own and no device routed to it. Both understate the real path, and both are the same missing piece. **What this leaves:** B19–B21, which need several threads per CPU and per-CPU faulting — D36 stays open for those. | The scaling condition (B19–B20), which needs a secondary to host more than one thread. |
| D244 | **The scaling condition: independent IPC pairs against independent fault loops, and the two are each other's control (SMP Phase 5; B19 and B20, closing D36).** One client/server pair per CPU calling over a channel nobody else touches, and one zero-fill fault loop per CPU over a scratch address space nobody else maps — run at one worker, then two, then all three, with the boot CPU driving and taking part in neither. Parallel efficiency collapses to `window₁ / window_N`, because every worker does the same work in every phase: **perfect scaling is three CPUs finishing in the time one took.** **The result is the point, and it is a failure.** B19 reads **34–76% across seven runs, never once reaching its budgeted 85%**, while B20 reads **78–113%, at or about its budgeted 80% every time**. The percentages are noisy under QEMU/TCG and the number that is not is beside them: the machine lock was waited on **1306–1408 times** in B19's widest phase and **zero** times in B20's, on every run. Nothing in either benchmark is shared, so what B19 loses is the kernel's own serialization — and `crate::machine_lock`, which D232 put in front of every channel operation on the machine, is named directly rather than inferred. `docs/prototypes/01` asks a failing scaling number to arrive with a profile naming the contended structure; a lock-wait count is that, and a better answer than a coherence counter would be here, because an emulator has no coherence to count. **B20 is what makes B19's number mean anything.** Same harness, same phases, same CPUs, same clock — and it shares nothing, so it cannot lose to contention. A single benchmark falling short under an emulator proves nothing; two, differing only in what the kernel serializes, do. **What it took to get there.** `Handoff` carried one thread per CPU and now carries a queue of [`PER_CPU`], because a scaling instance is a *pair* and half an instance is not an instance; the threads are handed over only after the cross-core benchmarks are done, which is what the queue buys. `kcore::pmem::FramePool` is a frame source over frames drawn in advance, because the boot CPU owns physical memory and a secondary cannot share an allocator reached by `&mut` — and a benchmark whose workers contended for one allocator would be measuring the allocator. B20 needs no hardware fault and no address-space switch: `resolve_fault` walks a scratch space through the direct map, which is how the x86-64 rig has measured B8 all along. **The completeness check earned itself immediately.** Pools sized exactly to the data pages ran dry partway through the first fault phase, because `resolve_fault` also allocates the page-table levels it walks. `complete` refused, the claim was withheld, and the boot said so — a check written to catch a percentile computed over a prefix caught a real shortfall on its first run. It now names the shortfall as well as withholding. Shorting one worker by a single round trip withholds the claim and nothing else. **What this leaves:** B21, which `docs/prototypes/01` puts in Stage 1 behind the VFS service, and the contended load condition, which needs a soaker. D36's cross-core and scaling benchmarks are done. | Stage 1, and whatever the machine lock's 1300 waits per run are worth doing about. |
| D245 | **The shootdown's callers — a mechanism reached only by its own probe (SMP Phase 3, completing D227).** `AddressSpace::invalidate` is the only function that works out which CPUs still hold a translation, and it had exactly one caller in the tree: the x86-64 boot check that proves the mechanism. Every real removal path — `unmap_range`, `protect_range`, the device-window pair, `reclaim_range`, `teardown`, `evict_page` — went straight to `arch.unmap`, which invalidates locally and tells nobody, so a CPU running on the space kept its entry and `claim smp.shootdown` passed regardless. **That is the shape worth recording.** D227 built the mechanism, checked it, and inverted the check, and all three stay true of a path nothing calls: an inversion proves the check can see the mechanism, and says nothing about whether anything reaches it. **Once per operation, not once per page.** A target drops every translation it has rather than the one it was named, so a single request covers a whole range — which is what makes the bulk paths affordable instead of one interrupt and one blocking wait per 4 KiB. **The sender is installed, not named.** An `AddressSpace` is generic over its page tables and nothing else and cannot reach a port's `Ipi`, which is `kcore::wakeup`'s problem exactly, so the port installs a function once at boot together with the bound it waits within — the port being what knows its own scale. Only x86-64 installs one: the other four either start no secondary or broadcast their invalidate, where the target set is empty by construction and the whole call is a branch the optimizer removes. **The frame-freeing paths needed restructuring rather than a call.** `reclaim_range` and `teardown` unmapped and freed page by page, and a frame back in the allocator while another CPU can still translate to it has two owners — whoever is handed it next writes memory somebody else is reading, and nothing faults. They accumulate into a 32-entry batch, shoot down, and only then free; the batch size trades stack against interrupts and is not a correctness knob, because nothing is freed ahead of the request that covers it. **One line made any of it reachable.** `active_core_mask` has no writer at all — `AddressSpace::activate` has zero callers and every port activates its architecture space directly — so the kernel space is marked as one every CPU runs on at boot rather than inside the check that first wanted it. x86-64 now reports **59 shootdowns acknowledged and 0 unanswered**, against nothing from these paths before; AArch64 reports **0 and 0** and earns the same claim, which is why `vm.shootdowns-answered` names the failures rather than the successes — a broadcasting port completes none of these and is entirely correct. **Two inversions, and the split between them is the part that generalizes.** Dropping the request from `unmap_range` fails the host test with neither CPU asked, and moves the boot count from 59 to 58 — which nothing asserts, so the boot line is an observable and the host test is the check. Freeing before telling fails the ordering assertion, with two frames already back in the allocator by the time the sender ran. Schema: `kernel_event.isl` gains `TLB_SHOOTDOWN = 52`, appended, nothing renumbered. | `AddressSpace::activate` gaining its callers, so a user address space's target set is its own rather than empty: 61 sites across the five ports activate an architecture space directly, and until they are converted only the kernel space shoots down. Then the fault paths, `cow_copy` and `demand_fill`. |
| D246 | **A thread can be taken off a CPU that never asked to leave it, on a CPU that is not the boot CPU (SMP Phase 3).** Both ports' timer dispatch ran the tick hook only on the boot CPU, with a comment saying Phase 3 would remove the guard. Phase 3 did not: a secondary has had a run queue since D236, and nothing could preempt a thread on it. **Deleting the guard was never the fix.** The hook it gated is boot-CPU demo state — a `static mut Option<Scheduler>` on x86-64, a tick counter on AArch64 — so a secondary running it drives the wrong scheduler. There are two hooks now: the boot CPU's, which whichever check is running owns and replaces, and a secondary's, installed once, which dispatches out of the half that CPU's index names. **What made it safe is a mask, and the invariant it repairs was already written down.** `Scheduler::switch_to`'s own safety argument says the scheduler is not reentered across `C::switch` because the CPU is inside the function until the switch returns — untrue the moment a tick preempts, and the module thirty lines above already noted ticks landing there. Interrupts are now masked across the switch and restored by whichever context resumes, and the fresh-thread case needed nothing: every one of the five ports' thread trampolines already enables interrupts as its first instruction (`sti`, `msr daifclr, #2`, `csrsi sstatus, 2`, `cpsie i`), which is the same fact stated where it belongs — the port work this looked like it needed had been done years of milestones ago for its own reasons. **The mask is scoped to CPUs other than the boot CPU, and that was forced rather than chosen.** Masking every switch regressed the AArch64 ring-3 filesystem checks, reproducibly but only under full-suite parallel load — which is why four runs of those checks in isolation passed and said nothing, and why the baseline had to be re-established with `--nocache_test_results` before the regression was even visible. It buys nothing on the boot CPU, whose tick drives whichever scheduler the running check installed and never the executive's. Why the wide mask breaks that path is not known and is what preempting the boot CPU has to answer first. The scheduler's switching prologues are covered by a state check instead of a mask: each sets the current thread's state before touching the run queue, so a tick landing before that store preempts a Running thread harmlessly and one landing after finds it Blocked or Exited and declines. **What is refused rather than fixed, and why that is the smaller claim.** `crate::machine_lock` is owned per CPU and not per thread, so preempting a thread inside an executive method hands the hold to whoever runs next — free access to tables the outgoing thread is halfway through, and a release of a hold it never took. `crate::epoch`'s read depth is the same shape, and a thread that carried it away would leave that CPU unable to ever quiesce. Both are per-CPU counters a switch does not carry, so `crate::preempt` declines to preempt while either is raised and counts the deferral. Making them per-thread means teaching five ports' switch paths to carry them, and nothing needs preemption badly enough yet to buy that. **The check is the only one in the tree a cooperative kernel fails.** Every other secondary thread blocks — a server in `receive`, a client in `call` — so all of them pass on a kernel that preempts nothing. Two CPU-bound threads on one secondary do not: the first spins waiting to see the second, and the second cannot start until a tick takes the first off the CPU. Both ports report **2/2 ran, 2/2 saw the other**, with 1227 preemptions on x86-64 and 482 on AArch64 and **zero deferrals** on either. Restoring the guard reports **1/2** — the cooperative signature, the second worker seeing the first and never the reverse — and withholds `claim smp.secondary-preempted` in 8 seconds. **Two things the bounds cost.** The give-up is the boot CPU's and not the worker's, because a worker counting its own iterations spends the whole budget on exactly the run where the check fails; and `WAIT_SPINS` is two million rather than the tree's usual arrival bound, which under QEMU/TCG costs more than a whole boot and turned the inversion from a report into a timeout. The first version also picked a stack slot one past the cross-call pair and landed inside the scaling benchmark's range, taking `perf.scaling-replicated` down with it; the slot is derived now. Schema: `kernel_event.isl` gains `PREEMPTION = 53`, appended. | Preemption on the boot CPU, whose executive is still driven by a tick hook that belongs to a demo; then per-thread machine-lock and epoch state, which is what would let a tick preempt inside the executive rather than deferring past it. |
| D247 | **The kernel is not stopped by hardware from touching user memory, on any of the five ports.** x86-64 never set `CR4.SMAP`, AArch64 never arranged `PSTATE.PAN`, and RISC-V sets `sstatus.SUM` once at boot and leaves it set — three spellings of the same absence, and the only one of the three that was written down was a comment in `karch-riscv64/src/trap.rs` saying the other two ports did not enable theirs either. A gap recorded in a source comment is a gap nothing tracks, which is what this row is for. **The whole safety argument therefore rests on D22**: every user pointer the kernel follows is validated up front against the caller's tracked mappings, so a copy is rejected before it starts rather than faulting. That argument is sound for the copies that go through `read_user`/`write_user` and is not a backstop for anything that does not — which is a live category, since a port-local map arm reached the page tables with an unbounded user address until 6ead01b. **What is enabled is the execution half, which needs no per-thread state at all.** `CR4.SMEP` is on from this milestone, programmed inside `init_cpu_tables` rather than beside the other paging features, because `CR4` is per CPU: a bit set in the boot path alone leaves every other CPU able to execute a user page, and no CPU can read another's `CR4` to notice. Each arrival is counted and the boot compares the count against the CPUs that took their own tables, so the claim is `smep.all-cpus` rather than `smep.on`. It costs nothing here — the kernel never executes a user page; it enters user code by dropping to CPL 3, and the one page it executes that it mapped itself is the architecture battery's sentinel, mapped `rx()` with no user bit. **Measured rather than asserted.** A boot claim proves the bit is set, not that the CPU honours it, so the same image was booted twice with a probe that maps the sentinel `rx().user()` and calls it from ring 0: with `+smep` it takes `#PF` error code `0x11` — present page, instruction fetch — and with the feature absent it returns `0x5e17c0de` and boots clean. The CPU model is the only variable. `smoke_boot.sh` asks for `+smep` for the same reason it asks for `+x2apic`: a feature CI never exercises is a feature CI cannot defend. **Four ports have the access half now, and the fifth cannot.** x86-64 sets `CR4.SMAP` and AArch64 sets `PSTATE.PAN` with `SCTLR_EL1.SPAN` cleared so the CPU re-establishes it on every exception entry from EL0; `kcore::useraccess` carries the permission per thread across a context switch, because a validated copy can fault on a page its pager has not supplied and that fault blocks the copier. Each was measured rather than asserted: the same image reads a mapped user page fine inside a window and takes a fault outside one (`#PF` error `0x1`; `ESR` data abort, permission fault level 3). The AArch64 boot checks moved from `cortex-a72` to `cortex-a76` because `FEAT_PAN` is ARMv8.1 and a v8.0 part reports it absent — a feature CI never exercises is one CI cannot defend. Both RISC-V ports needed no feature probe — `SUM` is in the base privileged specification — and no boot-glue audit: it had been setting `SUM` once per demo and leaving it, eight calls each removing the backstop for a whole run rather than for the copy that needed it, and deleting all eight left nothing that reaches a `U` page outside a window. It needs no counterpart to `SPAN` either, because a trap does not touch `SUM` and the kernel closes every window before `sret`. **What is left is the access half on the other three, and on one of them it is not a bit.** `SUM`/`SMAP`/`PAN` cannot be scoped to a copy, because a syscall that copies from user memory may then block — a channel call hands off to another thread inside that window — and all three live in per-hart, per-CPU registers, so a scoped enable would be set while an unrelated thread runs and cleared on a return that no longer corresponds to it. Narrowing it safely means making the permission per-thread state the context switch carries, which is the D81 class of change on five ports. | Nothing on the four ports whose architectures have the control. ARM 32 has none — privileged-access-never reached AArch32 in ARMv8.1-A and this port targets ARMv7-A — so its copies are counted by `kcore::useraccess::unprotected_copies` and the fact is recorded in `karch-arm32::trap`. That number is what an ARMv8.1-A port of this code would drive to zero; there is no bit to set here. |
| D248 | **The system call surface is written down once, generated into a reference, and gated against drifting again (composition roadmap Phase 0).** `api/isl/examples/syscall_abi.isl` declared **6 calls of the 50** that had arrived and carried a header saying the bindings were "ready to wire when user-mode ABI stabilizes" — true when six was the whole set.
