The layouts moved into ISL in D54 and the call surface did not, so the accurate reference became 209 lines of doc comment on `SyscallNumber`: good documentation, readable only from inside the kernel by somebody who already had the source. `docs/api/01` described about twenty families and marked none of them, so a reader could not tell which existed. **The language had to grow a construct, which `docs/roadmap/03` predicted and `docs/api/03` now specifies first.** A `syscall` is a trap number, a register frame and one result word, and spelling it as a `protocol` method would put a response payload where there is a result word and a channel where there is a trap — so it is its own declaration. `argN: T` carries a value; `argN: SomeArgs` carries a **user pointer**, which is the whole difference between the eighteen calls reading scalars out of registers and the thirty-two taking a struct. Required rights ride on the handle types, because that is where they are enforced. **`extern struct N from L` instead of an import**: the argument structs belong beside the subsystems they describe, a register slot needs a name and never a layout, and building a module system to write down a call surface is the tail wagging the dog — so the dependency is declared and `//tools/checks:surface_test` resolves all 28 of them across the schema set, which it has to walk anyway. **Comments stopped being discarded.** The reference is generated from the schema, so prose the lexer threw away was prose the reference could not carry; a run of comment lines above a declaration is now its documentation, a blank line ends the run, and the file header documents the library. That cost nothing at the schemas — **not one of the other 31 was edited to gain a doc**, because a `//` block above a declaration was already how every one of them was written — and the generated Rust bindings carry the prose too. **`codegen_docs.rs` is the third backend, and it covers the other 31 schemas for free**: **7,210 lines across 32 pages**, of which the syscall surface is 1,085. That is the argument for doing this in ISL rather than by hand — it was never fifty syscalls of work, it was the whole interface surface. **The gate is the item that matters**, holding four agreements: every `SyscallNumber` variant is a `syscall` at the same number with a description **and the reverse**; the schema's own `enum Syscall` matches its `syscall` declarations; every `extern` resolves to an `@abi struct` in the library it names; and every family in `docs/api/01` opens with a status from a fixed vocabulary — which needed a fourth word, `partial`, that the schema has no use for: "Memory" lists twenty operations of which thirteen exist, and a family forced to choose between implemented and designed would have to lie either way. **Writing it down found two calls with two argument forms in one tree.** `HandleDuplicate` and `PageSupply` are read as registers by the shared dispatcher (`kcore::dispatch`, D79) and as `@abi` argument structs by the x86-64 single-process demo handler that predates it — and `SyscallNumber`'s own doc comment describes the struct form for both, so the one reference anybody had was wrong about two calls. The schema states the shared dispatcher's, because that is the path a ring-3 program takes on four of the five ports; recorded rather than resolved, since resolving it is a change to a boot check. **Three inversions, each measured.** A 51st `SyscallNumber` variant with no schema entry fails with the variant named; pointing an `extern` at the wrong library fails naming the library; deleting one family's status line fails naming the family and its line. A `syscall` with no `@status`, a gap in its register slots, two calls at one number, and a bounded collection in a register are each rejected by the compiler with their own `ISLxxxx` code. **What this is not.** It does not check that a call's *arguments* match what the kernel decodes — that would mean parsing `kcore::dispatch`, and the two divergences above are the evidence that it is worth wanting. It does not require a doc on every declaration in every schema, only on the call surface. And `tools/ci/docs.sh` builds the reference and `cargo doc` in the continuous gate but publishes neither. | The argument shapes checked as well as the names, which closes the `HandleDuplicate` and `PageSupply` divergence rather than recording it; a doc requirement extended from the call surface to every declaration; and somewhere for `tools/ci/docs.sh` to publish to. |
| D249 | **A process holds what its parent gave it: `ChannelCreate` implemented and `ProcessGrant` added, driving a root task that is a compiled program (composition roadmap Phase 1, first landing).** `docs/roadmap/03` says Phase 1 needs no new mechanism — *"every syscall it needs has existed since D42"* — and its own "what this plan is likely to get wrong" section says Phase 1 *"will turn out to be capability plumbing"*. The second sentence was right. **Two calls were missing and both were named as gaps years of milestones ago.** `ChannelCreate` was deferred because a success has to hand back two handles and the result word carries one (D45), so every channel in this system's history was wired by kernel boot glue; version 2 of `ChannelCreateArgs` answers it the way every other two-answer call already does — the caller says where to write a `ChannelCreateRecord` and the result word stays a status. A **version-1 caller is refused rather than served with one end**, because half a channel leaves a peer nobody holds and the caller finds out only when its messages go nowhere; the record is written *before* either handle is installed, so a caller whose buffer is unwritable gets an error and no channel rather than a slot nothing can close. And nothing could put a capability into a child: `docs/api/01` has listed "install the initial handle set into a created process before start" since it was written and nothing implemented it, so **every service in this tree got its handles from boot glue reaching into its table** — `bring_up_device_host` installs five across two processes. `ProcessGrant` (call 50) is one capability per call, deliberately: a vector would install a set atomically and this ABI has been bitten by hand-decoded vectors before (D101), while a loop makes each grant separately refusable and separately auditable. **Four refusals, and each is a different mistake that cannot be made quietly**: the kernel narrows and never expands, as `HandleDuplicate` does; `TRANSFER` on the source, because handing a capability on is itself an authority; `MAP` on the target, because composing a child's handle table and composing its address space are one authority; and the target must still be `Created`, because once a process is running what it holds is its own business. Granting to yourself is refused by name — it is `HandleDuplicate` with extra steps, and it was the one way a caller could satisfy the state check with its own process. **The root task is Rust, and 167 lines of `global_asm!` are gone**, along with the twenty-instruction child blob inside it and `roottask.ld` (whose only difference from the shared script was the `.bss` and `.got` it forgot). It walks a real ELF — bounds-checked at every field, refusing a W+X segment and a segment claiming more file bytes than it has, because this parser reads bytes off a build today and off a disk in Phase 2 — maps each `PT_LOAD` with a second zero-fill call for a `.bss` tail, grants, and starts. **It tells the child where its capability landed**, in `ProcessStart`'s `arg`, so the child assumes no number the kernel and it agreed on out of band. Its receive sets the do-not-block bit: the child has already exited, so a message either is queued or never will be, and a root task that parked there would hang the machine. **The kernel's own observation is the event record, not the child.** `ProcessStart` reclaims a process that has exited — slot, address space, and the parent's handle to it — so by the time the check runs there is no handle table left to read, which is exactly why a capability handed down has to be recorded when it is decided. `kernel_event.isl` gains `CHANNEL_CREATED = 56` and `PROCESS_GRANTED = 57`, appended; `arg2` of the latter is the rights the **child** got, because the interesting mistake is a grant wider than intended. **Two inversions, both measured, and the second found something.** Skipping the grant: the child exits **72** (`send refused`), the parent's receive answers `WouldBlock`, and no `PROCESS_GRANTED` event exists — three independent failures. Granting `READ` where the source holds `WRITE\|TRANSFER`: the *grant itself* is refused, because `READ` is not a subset of what the source carries — a narrowing rule that only masked bits would have accepted it. **What this does not do.** It starts one child, not the device manager, and no demo died: `device_manager_demo`, `driver_host_demo` and `component_manager_demo` are Phase 1's next step and are all still there. The child's ELF is linked into the root task's `.rodata`, which is the kernel's own compromise moved up one level. x86-64 only — the other four ports answer `ENOSYS` for the loader trio, and moving it into `kcore::dispatch` is what a second port needs first. `root_syscall_handler` is the shape that migration wants: everything uniform goes to the shared dispatcher and four calls stay local. The arch-lint baseline rises 169 -> 171 on x86-64, both `deref_addrof` on a `static mut` reached through `&raw mut` — the one class that file permits — and both funnelled: eight call sites became `root_processes()` and `root_objects()`, and the pair goes when the loader trio takes its tables as arguments. | The device manager started by the root task rather than by `bring_up_device_host`, which is what retires the three demos; the loader trio moved into `kcore::dispatch` so a second port has it; and the child's image read from the verified store instead of from `.rodata` (Phase 2). |
| D250 | **A start that does not wait, and a root task that is the component manager (composition roadmap Phase 1, second landing).** `ProcessStart` handed the CPU to the child and came back with the child's exit code, which is a spawn-and-wait: a parent could hold **one** running child, so a root task could not start a server and then start something to talk to it. `docs/api/01` has listed "start process" and "wait for process or thread termination" as two operations since it was written; this splits them. `ProcessStart` returns as soon as `add_thread` has left the child `Ready`, and `ProcessWait` (call 51) blocks for the code. **A process that has already exited returns immediately**, because otherwise every supervisor is a race against its own child: a short-lived service can be gone before its parent's next instruction, and a wait insisting on seeing the transition parks for ever. The code comes back as a `uint32` bit pattern — a code is signed and this ABI spells failure with the sign. **The reclaim moved with the wait**, since there is no longer a moment inside a start at which the child is finished. **Making the start asynchronous broke an assumption that was written down.** The child kernel-stack window was ONE memoized VA, and the comment said why: *"safe because supervision is synchronous — one child alive at a time"*. Two children at once, and the second `spawn_user` mapped a window the first was standing on; the boot failed at the second start with a mapping error. It is a pool of four now, taken at start and given back at reclaim, so a supervisor restarting a service forty-one times still uses one — the property the memoization existed for — while concurrent children get their own. Exhaustion is refused: two threads on one kernel stack is not a shortage, it is corruption. The child ASID went the same way, from a memoized tag to one per child. **Three demos died.** `component_manager_demo`, `cm_budget_selftest` and `cm_reclaim_stress` were a manager blob and a service blob in `global_asm!`, reading a countdown out of a hand-patched data page at a fixed VA, because a blob was all the loader syscalls could reach and a blocking start was the only wait there was. The root task does all three in Rust: it supervises a service to a clean start over **41 launches**, gives up on one that never comes up after **3**, and the supervisor is nine lines. Their `DemoId`s stay — `demo_verdict.isl` ordinals are append-only — and reaching one now prints FAIL rather than a line that reads as a passing demo. **41 is the reclaim proof and not a supervision result**: process and thread slots are both capped at 16, so a seventeenth launch fails unless every exited instance gave back its slots, its kernel stack and its frames. The boot check asserts the launch count **exactly** — a supervisor that restarted more than its policy allows is as wrong as one that stopped early — and bounds the frame draw at 192 against a measured 90. **The root task moved late in the boot**, where the retired demos ran, and had to be given fresh process and object tables: forty-five launches need slots, and by that point the earlier checks have filled both. It is also the only thing in this image that spawns a thread *from inside a thread*, so it is the only producer of correlation-link events carrying a parent — `correlation_demo` reads those out of a 256-entry ring and failed with `links=0` until the move. **Two inversions.** Restoring the handoff in `ProcessStart`: the root task never runs again, `launches=1`, and no report is printed. Removing the process-table reclaim from `ProcessWait`: `launches=15` — the sixteenth `ProcessCreate` refused with a resource error — and the frame draw at **199**, past its bound. **What this does not do.** It still starts probes rather than the device manager, which needs device capabilities seeded into the root task; the loader trio is still port-local, so the other four ports answer `ENOSYS`; and `MAX_LIVE_CHILDREN` is four, which is a number this check does not press. The arch-lint baseline falls 171 -> 166 on x86-64: nothing was fixed, five findings went with the deleted demos. | The device manager started by the root task, retiring `driver_bind_check`'s assembly; the loader trio moved into `kcore::dispatch` so a second port has it; and a wait that can name a process the caller did not create. |
| D251 | **The process lifecycle stops being one port's (composition roadmap Phase 1, third landing).** `ProcessCreate`, `AddressSpaceMap`, `ProcessStart` and `ProcessWait` lived in `kernel/kernel/src/main.rs`, so the other four ports answered `ENOSYS` and a root task was a thing exactly one architecture could have — while the driver framework it is meant to start lives on two of the others. `kernel/boot-checks` already stated the rule this violates: *"A port's `main.rs` is its composition root: it knows a boot protocol, a trap frame and an exit mechanism, and nothing else should."* Creating a process is none of those three. They are `kcore::loader` now. **The seam is six methods and nothing on it is there for convenience.** A fresh user address space, because `new_user` is an *inherent* method on each port's page tables with a **different signature on each** — x86-64 takes no address-space tag, the RISC-V and ARM ports take one — so it is not on `AddressSpaceOps` and cannot be called from `kcore` at all. The kernel half a child's stack is mapped into, the kstack windows, and the two stack sizes: all address-space layout, all the port's. Where a child's user stack *goes* is not on the seam — that is the caller's, in `ProcessStartArgs::stack`, because the loader that placed a program's segments is what knows where they left room. Everything else — the create-process authority gate, W^X, the copy validation, the double-start refusal, the reclaim, the waiter bookkeeping — is written once. **`DispatchEnv` was not touched.** The loader needs a port-lent seam, so the port routes these four arms to `kcore::loader` directly rather than through `dispatch()`; widening the shared environment would have meant editing 33 construction sites and raising its `ContextOps` bound to `UserContextOps` for four calls. Routing is what a composition root does. **Eleven host tests, and the reason they matter is arithmetic.** While the lifecycle was port-local the only thing that could exercise it was one port's boot check: every rule it enforces was checked once, on one architecture, through QEMU. They run against the mock in milliseconds now. **Three inversions, and the third found a test that could not see its own subject.** Removing the create-process check fails; removing the double-start refusal fails; removing the W^X check **passed** — because `AddressSpace::protect_range` refuses a writable-executable range too, so the refusal still happened, just later. What a loader without the check actually does is map the pages *writable*, then fail to re-protect them, leaving the child holding a writable mapping at an address the request was refused for. The test asserts the child's mapping count now rather than the error code, and fails when the check goes. **What is not tested, and it is stated rather than left as coverage:** a create that runs out of frames, because the mock builds an address space without drawing one — a test asserting that refusal would pass against a loader that never checked, and would be measuring the mock. **What this does not do.** The other four ports still route nothing to it: each needs a `LoaderSupport` impl of its own, which is the point of the seam but is not this row. x86-64's older single-process handler answers `ENOSYS` for all four, which is honest — its ring-3 programs are wired by boot glue and create nothing. The arch-lint baseline falls 166 -> 163: three more `deref_addrof` sites left with the code, and `kcore` is linted by the aspects that cross the whole graph, so nothing stopped being checked. | A second port implementing `LoaderSupport`, which is what turns this from a refactor into a portability claim; then the device manager started by the root task on the port where the driver framework runs. |
| D252 | **A second port composes a system, from the same root task (composition roadmap Phase 1, fourth landing).** D251 moved the process lifecycle into `kcore::loader`, which proved nothing on its own — a facility with one caller is a facility shaped like its caller. AArch64 implements `LoaderSupport` now and runs the **same root-task source** x86-64 runs: same channel, same grant, same supervisor, same 45 launches. Every relevant detail of the port differs and none of it reached `kcore`: this machine builds a user space with `build_low_space` (a third signature, after x86-64's `new_user` and the RISC-V ports'), links programs at `0x1000_0000_0000` rather than `0x400000`, and gives them twelve pages of user stack against four. The seam is still six methods. **Three `cfg`s in the program, and they are the honest ones**: the ELF machine it refuses, where a child's stack goes, and nothing else — the two probe images keep their crate names across architectures (`tessera_embedded_elf` grew a `crate` argument) so the source `use`s one name and the build picks which. **The hazard the tree had already written down, found again.** `ProcessCreate` builds a `Process` inside a syscall; a `Process` is **30,024 bytes** — a 1024-entry handle table — and this port gave its EL0 programs an 8-page kernel stack. The overflow lands in the *callee's prologue*, so there is no statement to blame, and this port's EL1 abort path resumes the faulting instruction — which turns it into a **silent hang** rather than a report: no fault line, no output, a 120-second timeout. x86-64 had sized its loader parent at 32 pages for exactly this and written the arithmetic down; `ring3_host_spawn_with_stack` is the same number for the same reason. Diagnosis took four instrumented boots, and the useful probe was the one that printed *nothing*: a print at the first statement of `create` that never appeared put the fault before the first statement. **A second defect the move exposed: an incomplete teardown is only visible to the next check.** The run reaped the root task's thread and left its `Process` in the shared table — and whichever EL0 check ran next failed, with the *same* error whether that was `relay` or `firmware`. Moving the check proved it was ordering rather than resources (38 frames for 45 launches, and the reclaim gives them back on this port too); the fix is a complete teardown, after which position stops mattering at all — which is the property worth having and is now stated where the check sits. **The event-based grant assertion is gone from both ports.** `PROCESS_GRANTED` is emitted three syscalls into a run that then makes forty-five launches, each emitting events, so the record is pushed out of a 256-entry ring: it survived on x86-64's boot and not on AArch64's `fs_boot`, which is luck rather than a property. What the pass condition rests on instead is stronger — the root task exits non-zero unless the child's message arrived on the endpoint it was granted, and an audit record says a grant was *made* where a message says the capability *carried authority*. The value is still printed. **Two inversions, and one did not fire.** Lending a single kernel-stack window instead of the pool fails at `launches=1`. Lending x86-64's four-page user stack instead of this port's twelve **passed** — the two probes are small enough not to need twelve, so this check does not discriminate that seam value, and saying so is better than implying it does. The arch-lint baseline rises 236 -> 237 on AArch64: thirteen `deref_addrof` sites became one, the accessor for the loader seam, by routing the rest through the `kcore_exec`/`kcore_processes` helpers this port already had and one new `kcore_objects`. | The device manager started by the root task, on this port, which is what retires `bring_up_device_host`; and the three remaining ports, each of which needs one `LoaderSupport` impl and nothing else. |
| D253 | **The root task starts the device manager, which binds a driver, which serves its class (composition roadmap Phase 1, second bullet).** The sequence existed in kernel code three times over — `bring_up_device_host`, `relay_pair`, `driver_bind_check` — each of them boot glue creating a channel, spawning two programs and reaching into their handle tables. It is user code now: the root task creates the channel, loads both ELFs, decides what each holds, and starts them. **Boot seeds two capabilities and no more**: a job carrying `create-process`, and a *bus* carrying `READ \| DERIVE \| TRANSFER`. Every device the manager binds it derives from that bus; every capability the driver ends up with arrived by transfer. The root task narrows `TRANSFER` away when it hands the bus on — a manager derives children and passes *those*, so it never needs to pass the bus itself. **The manager is never waited for**, because it is a resident server that parks on its endpoint and does not exit; what ends the run is the driver having reported, which is the shape a start that no longer blocks made expressible (D250). **A partial `bits` catalog makes a capability unpassable, which is the finding.** `ProcessGrant`'s `rights` field is a strict `bits Rights`, and `process_abi.isl` declared six of the seventeen catalog bits — enough for the calls that existed when it was written. A grant carrying `DERIVE` therefore failed to *decode*, and the refusal arrived as `Protocol` from the generated decoder rather than as anything about authority: a schema quietly saying "this capability cannot be handed on". It carries the whole catalog now, because a call that hands on *any* capability must be able to name any right. **`HandleQueryRights` was missing from the shared dispatcher entirely** — implemented on one port, absent from `kcore::dispatch`, so four ports answered `Unhandled` for a call `syscall_abi.isl` marks implemented and D248's gate could not see. A composed program has to be able to ask what it holds: a root task that must know whether this machine gave it a bus cannot assume, and that is how the gap surfaced. **One program, two machines, no `cfg` on the composition.** The framework phase runs when `HandleQueryRights` says a bus is there and is skipped when it says nothing is — so x86-64, which seeds none, runs the same binary and composes what it can. **Two inversions, and the first version of the check failed one of them silently.** Handing the manager a bus the root task cannot pass on (no `TRANSFER` at the seed) fails at `exit=115`, naming the grant. Handing it a bus it cannot derive from (no `DERIVE`) **passed** — because the check tested a tag bit, and `blk-probe` packs its answer into the same word it returns a failure code in: `0xdead...` has bit 61 set and a success does not, so the predicate read every failure as a pass. It asserts the bind's status and hop count now, and the inversion fails with `status=1 hops=0`. A third mistake came out of the same run: the synthetic hubs were given the storage vendor, and a manager charges a data path by the hubs it can *identify* — an unidentified one is refused `PathUndeclared`, not treated as free, which is a claim `relay_check` already makes and this check accidentally re-derived. **The failing step is in the exit code now** (`100 + step`), because this port's `DebugWrite` records the argument register rather than the buffer behind it, so a root task's report line has nowhere to go here; an exit code reaches every port. It is what turned `exit=2` into `exit=115` and named the grant. **Measured, as D231 requires**: the root task grows 66,816 -> 250,824 bytes and the kernel image 6,774,208 -> 6,968,616, because the manager and the driver are now in the image twice — once in the kernel's component list and once inside the root task, which has nowhere to read a program from. Phase 2 removes both copies at once. **What this does not do.** It does not retire `bring_up_device_host` or `relay_pair`: those bind against the machine's real virtio devices and an interrupt route, and this composes a synthetic bus so that a machine with no disk attached still answers the question the check is about. Retiring them needs the root task to hold a real device and its interrupt, which needs `PortCreate`. | `PortCreate`/`PortBind` reaching the shared dispatcher, so a root task can build the ports a driver host waits on — which is what retires `bring_up_device_host`; and the images coming from the store rather than from two places in one image (Phase 2). |
| D254 | **A process can make its own port, and reach a third argument register (composition roadmap Phase 1).** `PortCreate` and `PortBind` were port-local on x86-64 and absent everywhere else, so a program that wanted somewhere to be woken had to be handed one — the seeding this phase exists to stop. Both are in `kcore::dispatch` now, and the port table mints object ids from `0x3000` the way `ChannelTable` mints from `0x2000` and `MemoryTable` from `0x1000`, so a ring-3 caller cannot land on an id boot glue chose. **`PortCreate` takes no arguments and needs no authority**, because a port is a place to be woken rather than a thing to be woken *by*: it carries nothing until something is bound, and `Rights::BIND` on the port is where that is checked. It surfaced as `InvalidMapping` from the signal-width guard: the register was never written, so the kernel read whatever was in it. **Used, not merely implemented.** The root task makes a port, binds it to one source, and grants it to its child with `SIGNAL` and nothing else; the child raises an edge on it and the parent's wait finds the event queued. A capability to *wake somebody* is a different authority from a capability to talk to them, and the child now holds one of each, neither put there by the kernel. | D308 | **A memory object costs what it uses, and a full table says which one.** The object table had been the binding constraint on every composed path for three rows — D304 raised it from 8 to 16 to fit a filesystem client, D307 dropped a leg to fit a compiler — and the reason was its *shape* rather than its size. An object carried its frames **inline**, `[Option<PhysFrame>; MAX_OBJECT_PAGES]`, so every entry cost the largest object's storage whether it held one page or all sixteen. That coupled two limits which have nothing to do with each other: the table could not be made wider, because each entry was hundreds of bytes, and it could not be made *deeper*, because raising the per-object cap multiplied by the number of objects. A 151 KB program could not be opened at all. **The frames are a shared pool now.** An object names a contiguous run of slots and costs a descriptor; `MAX_FRAME_SLOTS` is how many pages every object may hold between them, `MAX_MEMORY_OBJECTS` is how many objects there may be, and `MAX_OBJECT_PAGES` is a per-object cap rather than a per-object price. **Runs are contiguous on purpose**: `frame_at` is on the page-in path and stays `slots[base + page]`, where a linked list would have made the hottest lookup a walk; the cost is a first-fit scan on create, which is rare. The table went from 16 to 64 and its declared ceiling from 64 to 1024, which is now nearly free. **Exhaustion says which pool.** `KError::OutOfFrameSlots` is distinct from `OutOfMemory` — a full *table* is a machine with gigabytes free — and `FsError::NO_OBJECT` is distinct from `NO_BUFFER`, which used to be the answer and is about *this request's* transfer buffer. That mattered immediately: the refusal that had read as a buffer problem now reads as `NoObject`, verified on the machine. **And the measurement that says the wall was never where it looked.** D307's dropped leg was restored to see whether the wider table let it back in. It does not: at **256 objects and 2048 frame slots** — four and eight times anything the composition can want — it fails **identically**. So the refusal was not exhaustion, whatever it was reported as, and three rows of raising this budget were treating a symptom. What is left is narrower and stated where the next reader will find it: a file written by one client and opened by another is refused, where every file a program opens that it wrote itself works. **`MAX_OBJECT_PAGES` is still 16, and that is a finding too.** It is no longer a storage price, so it looked free to raise — but `kernel-aarch64`'s writeback and dirty-page checks *derive their page counts from it*, so raising it does not add headroom, it makes those checks do something several times larger. A trial value of 192 was caught by the dirty-page check failing, which is nothing to do with memory. Raising it is affordable now and is its own change. **Five tests and an inversion**: a destroyed object returns its run, the pool is reused across sixty-four lifetimes, two objects never share a page, exhausting the pool names the pool, and a fragmented pool refuses a run it cannot place while still serving one that fits — and a `destroy` that frees the frames and keeps the run fails three of them, which is the failure this change was most at risk of introducing and the hardest to see. `bazel test //...` green, both lint gates still zero. | **The wall this row set out to remove is still there, and is now known not to be the table.** The budget is four times what any composition wants and the same leg is refused, so the next step is the file-written-by-another-client case rather than any number in `config/kernel.config`. Two smaller things the fix does not do: the *kernel's* error codes are ABI and are the one part of the result word not published in a schema a program may read, so a service cannot tell "too large for one object" from "the pool is full" and answers `NO_OBJECT` for both — the surface gate exists to stop exactly this for syscall numbers and wants the same treatment here; and a run is contiguous, so a pool with room in several pieces refuses a request longer than any of them, which is a fragmentation the current callers cannot reach but a long-running one would. |
| D307 | **There is a compiler now, not just a machine that can compile (self-hosting roadmap Phase 5).** **First, what that phase's exit criterion says and what it was worth.** *"A program compiled on the machine runs on the machine"* — which D304 had already made true, three rows earlier. A criterion met by the phase before it is not a criterion, and it is the same defect the composition plan's Phase 4 had: narrower than its own subject, which is "the toolchain runs on the machine". That plan predicted this one too — *"'the compiler' is doing a lot of work in one noun"* — and the honest reading, a port of somebody else's very large program, is out of reach here and belongs in the second repository the composition plan wanted it in. So what this row does is the achievable half: make the thing D304 built into a compiler *you run*. **`userspace/tsmc` is that.** What to compile arrives in its **arguments** (D302), the input and output are files it opens through the **filesystem** for itself (D294), and what went wrong is a sentence naming a line, sent over **`diagnostic.isl`** (D303) — `tsmc: /bad.tsm:3: unknown operation`, which is the shape every compiler has said since `cc` because a build system parses the first two fields and a person reads the third. Smaller: the compiler reads a source no larger than the transfer buffer, and takes two arguments with no flags. |
| D306 | **A program that is not written in Rust runs on this machine (self-hosting roadmap Phase 4, first landing).** Every ring-3 program here had been `#![no_std]` Rust with a hand-written `_start` that never returns — which is not a shape a C toolchain emits, and a ported compiler, a shell and every core utility are written as `int main(void)`. `userspace/libc` is the floor that makes that shape runnable: `tessera/syscall.h`, the C counterpart of `userspace/uabi` — the trap instruction and its register convention, and nothing else — and a `crt0` whose `_start` calls `main` and exits with what it returned. **The syscall numbers come from the published ABI**, never from a literal: the header includes `<tessera/syscall_abi.h>`, which D305 generates and `//tools/checks:surface_test` holds to the kernel's own `SyscallNumber`. | D304 | **A program this machine made, off a source this machine read (self-hosting roadmap Phase 2 closes).** Every artifact that had ever run here was cross-compiled by the host toolchain. `/source.tsm` is six lines of text on the ext2 volume; `//userspace/tsm` parses it and emits AArch64; `fs-client` reads the source through the filesystem service, writes the image it produced back to the same volume, `Sync`s it, **reads it back**, and runs what came off. Raised to 16, and two genuine leaks were found on the way and fixed: **`fs-service` kept a closed file's memory object**, so every open leaked one for the rest of the boot, and **`fs-client` left two files open** it had finished with. A second port needs a second back end and nothing else. Three bounds, all refused rather than clamped: 64 operations, a 512-byte image, and operands the width of the immediate field each instruction actually has. And the compiler reports its errors as a packed failure word rather than through `diagnostic.isl` (D303) — the contract exists and this composition has no collector in it, which is wiring rather than a gap. What Phase 3 needs first is a target the toolchain can name: this generator emits for a machine it knows by being compiled for it, which is exactly what a target specification replaces. |
| D303 | **A program's output is addressed to something, and it is not the kernel (self-hosting roadmap Phase 1 closes).** `DebugWrite` is the kernel's console — the facility that exists so a machine which cannot yet run programs can say what happened — and every ring-3 program in this tree was still using it to talk about things the kernel has no stake in. `diagnostic.isl` is the contract instead: a severity, a bounded message, a `truncated` flag the **sender** sets so a sentence that stops mid-word is distinguishable from one that meant to, and two one-way methods. `userspace/log-service` holds one endpoint speaking it, and the root task composes three processes over two channels — the probes report, the collector forwards, and the composer checks the bytes. **`Report` is one-way and that is the whole design**: a program that has already failed must not then block on whoever is collecting the report, because a collector that stopped would turn every diagnostic into a hang and the programs most likely to emit one are the ones least able to wait. Making `DebugWrite` honour its declared buffer form is the obvious next step and is an ABI change to a call every port implements differently, which is why it is not folded in here. A compiler cannot be written that way: the file it compiles is the one thing about it that changes on every run. And `ProcessWait` has returned an exit code since D250 with **nothing saying what one meant**, so a parent could tell zero from non-zero and no more. `StartupArgs`, `StartupArg` and `ExitStatus` are those two gaps. **The argument vector composes `StartupHandles` rather than extending it**, which is the design decision worth recording: appending argument fields to that struct would put five hundred bytes of path in front of every child that only wanted to know where its endpoint landed, and a second struct at a remembered offset in the same page would be worse — "a layout both sides remember" is exactly what D261 replaced when it made the startup message a schema.
