Both cases are host tests on the compiler. **The ABI machinery ran for real, twice.** `//tools/checks:abi_test` refused the schema change until `surface.lock` was updated, then refused user space compiled against ABI 1 while the lock published 2. The change is **additive** — nothing moved, and `StartupHandles` is byte-identical — and the version moves anyway, because `docs/api/02` versions the surface rather than the breakage. **Three launches, on all five machines**, and one program produces all three outcomes because its parent said so: a path it echoes back intact, no arguments at all, and a path it will not resolve. The 32-bit ports are the ones that would catch a shape that only worked at 64 bits — the startup message exists because the startup *word* is 32 bits wide there (D259, D261). **Two inversions, discriminating at different steps**: the child using a compiled-in path instead of its argument fails the echo at `STEP_ARGS`, and the two refusals collapsed into one status fails at `STEP_ARGS_STATUS` while every other leg still passes — which is what makes the vocabulary load-bearing rather than decoration. `roottask.arguments` and `roottask.exit-status` are asserted in all five smoke boots; `bazel test //...` green, both lint gates still zero. | **Phase 1's second bullet is not done**: output is still `DebugWrite`, a kernel call made for a kernel's benefit, and a program that emits diagnostics is speaking to something above it. That is the next row and it needs a service to speak to. And *"reads that path"* in the phase's exit criterion is met in the weaker sense — the child reads the **argument**, not the file it names, because the filesystem composition is `fs-client`'s and this leg runs under the root task, which has no filesystem. Opening the file a parent named is where Phase 2 begins. Three smaller bounds, all in the schema: four arguments of 128 bytes, no environment, and an `ExitStatus` the kernel does not interpret — it is a contract between a parent and a child, which is the same thing the startup message is. |
| D301 | **A ring-3 program allocates, which is the first thing self-hosting needs (self-hosting roadmap Phase 0).** `docs/roadmap/03` closed Phase 5 as *"a consequence rather than a project"*, on the premise that Phase 4 would leave a POSIX tier underneath it. Phase 4 closed on its "Done when" — the ABI artifact — with `docs/api/04` tier 1 unstarted, so the premise never landed and `docs/roadmap/04` is the plan the stub was standing in for. **Measuring first found that four of the five things Phase 5's sentence names already exist and are gated**: reading a source off the volume (`fs.read`), writing bytes back durably (`fs_crash_aarch64.sh` kills the machine with `SIGKILL` the instant they appear and requires them already on the medium), loading and running a program off it (D294, `fs.exec`), and a published ABI to target (D296). The fifth is that **no program on this machine has ever produced a program**, and underneath it that there was no heap: `grep -rn 'global_allocator\|impl GlobalAlloc'` over the tree matched **nothing**, and `extern crate alloc` matched one file, a host test double. All 38 user-space source files are fixed-buffer — which is right for a driver, whose working set is a property of its device, and impossible for a compiler, whose working set is a property of its input. **`//userspace/ualloc` is the free list, and its metadata is out of line on purpose.** A conventional allocator stores bookkeeping in the memory it manages, which makes every operation a raw pointer write and none of it testable without real memory — the trap D294 recorded when it moved the ELF parser out of a `no_main` binary to give it a host test target. Here the free extents live in a fixed array beside the heap, so the whole algorithm is `usize` arithmetic: `#![deny(unsafe_code)]` holds on it, and **20 host tests exercise it against no memory at all**. `GlobalAlloc::dealloc` is handed the `Layout` it allocated with, so the array tracks holes — of which there are few — rather than allocations, of which there are many, which is what makes out-of-line metadata affordable. **It refuses rather than degrades, three ways**: a free list too fragmented to describe another hole refuses the free instead of dropping an extent no counter could find; an overlapping free is `NotAllocated` rather than a merge, because coalescing a double free hands one address to two callers; and a split with nowhere to put the tail is refused *before* the array is touched, so a refusal leaves the list as it was. **The finding is that a heap here is many objects and not one, and it came from running.** `MAX_OBJECT_PAGES` is 16, so no memory object may exceed 64 KiB — a bound set against the harder one that `MAX_FREE_FRAMES` is 256 and an object too large to reclaim is a limit that cannot be honoured on the way out. The first boot failed with the grow code for *`MemoryCreate` refused*, because the probe asked for 128 KiB in one object. Growth is capped at the kernel's ceiling and repeated instead, and a request larger than one object is served only because consecutive objects are mapped adjacently and the free list coalesces them without being told to. **`userspace/heap-probe` is the claim, and it is given no handles at all** — no channel, no device, no bus — because the authority to ask for memory is one a process has by being a process, and a probe that needed a capability would be measuring the capability. It grows a vector past any buffer it could have declared, reads every element back across the seam between two mappings, holds a second live beside it, then **frees the large one and allocates its size again with the heap not growing** — which is the claim, because a `dealloc` that did nothing would satisfy every step before it. **The unsafe gate refused the first justification, correctly.** Three `SAFETY` comments rested on "single-threaded", which stopped being true of this kernel at D225; the exclusion that actually holds is the **thread count, not the CPU count** — one thread cannot race itself on any number of cores — and saying so is what makes the `Sync` impl reviewable. **Two inversions, both discriminating**: `dealloc` made a no-op leaves `heap.allocated` and `heap.grown` standing and fails at the step that records the heap having grown to serve what the freed space should have covered; and growing once rather than in a loop fails with the still-short code, which is the shape this milestone had before the object ceiling was found. `heap.allocated`, `heap.grown` and `heap.reused` are asserted in `smoke_boot_aarch64.sh`; all 27 boot checks re-run, `bazel test //...` green, both lint gates still zero. | **AArch64 only, and no policy.** The probe and the layout constants cover the three 64-bit ports' address maps and the check runs on one; the two 32-bit ports have no `HEAP_BASE` and no program that needs one yet. Three larger gaps, all named in `docs/roadmap/04`: the heap **never shrinks** — giving a region back means unmapping it, and a program that unmapped while the free list still described the range would hand out addresses that fault, so `HEAP_MAX_BYTES` is a ceiling and not a policy; **how much a program may have** is a question this system has never been asked, and answering it needs the pager and the reclaim path to have an opinion, which that plan predicts is where Phase 0's real cost sits; and the `GlobalAlloc` shim lives in the probe rather than beside the arithmetic, because this tree extracts a second copy rather than anticipating one (D294's rule) — it moves when a second program allocates. |
| D300 | **The single-process family joins the substrate, and the port has one syscall handler.** D299's own next column named what was left: `user`, `dpage`, `pagein`, `waitaddr` and `perf` *"run one process with no process table and cannot build a `DispatchEnv` at all"*. They ran on `USER_PROCESS` and `USER_SCHEDULER` — one `Process` and one `Scheduler` beside the machine's, held in statics — and that, not any decision about the ABI, is why each carried a syscall handler of its own. They build their process through the same `chan_build_process` every other ring-3 check uses now, insert it into `PROCESSES`, and run on `EXEC`; both statics are gone, and with them **seven registered handlers** (`user_syscall_handler`, `pager_syscall_handler`, `wait_syscall_handler`, `perf_b1_syscall_handler`, `fs_syscall_handler`, `driver_bind_syscall_handler`, `root_syscall_handler`) and a duplicate fault handler. **Eight registered handlers become one**, which is the shape AArch64 has had since D79 and RISC-V since D101. What each check kept is an observer — `user_observer`, `wait_observer`, `perf_b1_observer`, `fs_observer`, `root_observer`, `bind_observer` — told what was called and what it answered. **Two syscall numbers gained a shared implementation on the way.** `WaitOnAddress` and `WakeAddress` existed nowhere but that x86-64 demo handler: the only implementation of two numbers in the tree lived in a check, so no other port could answer them. They are `kcore::dispatch`'s now, keyed on the physical page so a kernel waker with no user mapping and a ring-3 waiter agree, with the word read inside the executive's hold rather than before it (D240's rule, restated where every port gets it). **The move fixed two latent bugs that nothing was going to report.** `user_fault_handler` terminated `USER_PROCESS` and yielded `USER_SCHEDULER` — the single-process pair — and the channel, device-manager and driver-host checks all installed it: a fault under any of them would have exited a process from an earlier check and yielded a scheduler it had never populated, silently, with nothing printed. And the five checks recorded their process's thread identity as `thread_id_of(idx)` read from **`EXEC`'s** scheduler for a thread they had added to `USER_SCHEDULER` — the identity of whatever occupied another scheduler's slot `idx`. Neither had a symptom, because no check that could hit them faults. **The pagers stopped being told which process to fill.** `FS_PENDING` carries the faulting thread now, so the in-kernel pager and the ring-3 filesystem service each resolve the client through the process table; before, both supplied into "the one ring-3 process", which is why there could only ever be one. **The gate is the fifth agreement in `surface.rs`: one implementation of the shared surface per port.** A port may keep arms only its machine can serve — a console write, an exit, an `in`/`out` instruction, a loader reaching its own page tables — and may not answer a call `kcore::dispatch` already answers. The shared surface is read from the dispatcher rather than listed, so a call that moves into `kcore` becomes a call the ports may no longer implement with nothing to update. **The inversion for it passed, and the edit had landed.** A second registered handler answering `Null` was invisible: both this gate and D298's frame agreement matched `SyscallNumber::X =>` and not `Some(SyscallNumber::X) =>`, which is how three of the eight handlers were written — so the frame agreement had never read them either. Both scanners skip the pattern's closing parentheses now, and `check_frames` was narrowed to arms inside a **registered** handler so an observer matching on a call name is not judged as one; the arms it judges were counted at **54 before and 54 after**, so the narrowing removed exactly what the widening added. **B1 moved from roughly 700 to roughly 7,000 cycles, and that is the benchmark being fixed rather than the path being slowed.** It used to reach a handler written for the benchmark whose `Null` arm returned a constant; it times the entry stub, the one handler, the `DispatchEnv` it builds and `kcore::dispatch` now — what a null syscall costs here. The line still says these are QEMU/TCG cycles and not an R1 measurement, which is the budget's own condition — and the emulator's numbers vary by a third between runs (645, 764 before; 5,514, 7,306, 7,633 after), so the shape of the change is the finding and the magnitude is not. **Three inversions, all discriminating**: a second registered handler answering `Null` (after the scanner fix, named by function, call and file); `WaitOnAddress` returned to `Unhandled`, which fails the futex check with `woken=false wake_count=0` and exits 65; and `syscalls::shared` answering nothing, which now panics the isolation check on `handle duplicate did not succeed` — a check that had its own `HandleDuplicate` arm until this milestone and would have passed. All 27 boot checks re-run from cold, all passing; `bazel test //...` green (123 tests); both lint gates still zero. | `PageServe` and `PageSupply` stay local on this port and `PageSupply` is the one arm whose number the shared dispatcher also answers: the M18 check's object is one the boot glue minted and mapped by hand, and `kcore::dispatch::page_supply` fills a paged object the executive registered through `MemoryCreatePaged`. Moving it moves the check onto the executive's pager registry, which changes what it demonstrates. Two smaller ones: the other four ports each still register a pre-substrate bring-up hook beside their dispatcher — none answers a shared call, so the gate passes them, and none is on the substrate either; and `check_frames` only understands `frame.argN`/`args[N]`, so of the five ports it reads x86-64 alone. |
| D299 | **The port stops having sixteen ABIs, and the checks that had them start watching instead.** D298 fixed five argument-shape divergences and named the cause in its own next column: x86-64 installed **sixteen syscall handlers over eight functions**, each answering the trap with its own subset of the surface, where AArch64 has had one `el0_dispatch_hook` since D79. None of those was a decision to answer a call differently — each was a second implementation drifting from the first, which is what a second implementation does. The largest of them said so in a comment: the channel arms stay local for their instrumentation *"until the observer seam lands"*. **This is that seam**, and it is what the checks actually wanted. `crate::syscalls` builds one `DispatchEnv` — three copies before, each able to get a field wrong on its own — lends one boot allocator through one static where two checks published through two, and offers a two-phase [`Observer`] that is **told** what was called and what it answered. Two phases, because some of what a check measures is a difference: a round trip's cost is a switch count before and after, and an observer told only the answer could not compute it, which is exactly the kind of thing a check used to reach for by writing its own copy of the call. **Nine duplicate implementations are gone** — `chan_channel_call`, `chan_channel_recv`, `chan_channel_reply`, `chan_build_message`, `driver_port_create`, `driver_port_bind`, `driver_port_wait`, `driver_resolve_port`, and the env each of them needed — and the handler serving eight of the sixteen registrations answers four numbers instead of fourteen: `DebugWrite`, `ProcessExit`, and the two `DeviceIo` calls, which are `in`/`out` instructions and so port-local the way `IrqComplete` is on AArch64. **The claims survived and their evidence improved.** `ping` and `pong` were bytes peeked at in the kernel on the way past, so they held whether or not the kernel ever delivered anything; they are the bytes *delivered into the receiver's own buffer* now, which meant giving four ring-3 blobs a writable landing area and moving each caller's request out of read-only code — a call's inline buffer is in **and** out, which the old local copy never had to notice. `moved` and `granted` were "the kernel installed a handle" observed from inside the install; they are the kernel's own installed-handle report now, read against a sentinel the receiver seeds so that *nothing arrived* is distinguishable from *handle 0 arrived*. **What was not deleted, and why.** The roadmap's rule is that a demo goes when the composed path reaches what it asserts, and it does not: these two are still this port's only ring-3 device I/O and ring-3 interrupt delivery. Deleting them would have deleted coverage; deleting their handlers deleted the defect. **Two inversions**: `shared` forced to answer nothing fails nine demos, so they genuinely run on the shared dispatcher; and the server's receive asking for zero bytes fails `ping` while leaving `pong` true, so the claim rests on delivery rather than on a peek. All 27 boot checks re-run from cold, all passing; both lint gates still zero. | The single-process family — `user`, `dpage`, `pagein`, `waitaddr`, `perf` — still has its own handlers, because those checks run one process with no process table and cannot build a `DispatchEnv` at all. Unifying them means moving each onto the executive substrate, which changes what the check *is* rather than how it answers a trap. |
| D298 | **One syscall number, one ABI — the last of D248.** D248 closed Phase 0 with two findings, and only one of them: the gate held a call's name and number and *"not its argument shapes"*, which is why `HandleDuplicate` and `PageSupply` were *"recorded as having two argument forms in one tree"*. A published ABI (D296) makes that a live defect rather than a note: the artifact states one shape per number, and two of fifty-three numbers meant something else on one port. **Both are converged onto the schema.** `HandleDuplicate` reads two registers in the x86-64 demo handler now, as `kcore::dispatch` always has, and its ring-3 blob passes them instead of a `DuplicateArgs` block — which the schema no longer imports, because nothing reads it. `PageSupply` was the harder one and worse than "two shapes": the x86-64 M18 handler took an endpoint handle and a source address in two registers, derived the offset from an in-kernel pending-fault slot, and **returned the next offset** from a fused reply-and-wait — different arguments, different meanings, and a return value the schema declares none of. It reads a `PageSupplyArgs` through `arg0` now; the service holds a `SUPPLY` capability to the object it fills, because `memory` names one and a pager that could fill any object it happened to have a handle to is not a capability system; the offset is checked against the one the kernel asked for, because decoding a field and ignoring it is reading the schema's shape while implementing something else; and the reply moved to `PageServe`, which is where a serve loop's reply belongs. `reply_receive` was kept whole rather than split into `reply` then `receive` — a resident server that replies and loops back to its own receive is the hang this tree has debugged twice (D85, D91). **The gate is the fifth agreement in `surface.rs`**: every `SyscallNumber::X =>` arm in `kernel/` reads exactly the argument registers `syscall_abi.isl` declares. Text, like every tier-0 gate, and it needed three refinements to be right rather than merely strict — a braced arm has no trailing comma and a scanner that only looked for one read the *next* arm's registers as this one's; a register reached through `let args_ptr = frame.arg0` is still read; and an arm inside a helper that cannot see the frame is a re-dispatch whose caller is the arm that matters. **It found five more of the same class, all in x86-64 demo glue, and one was a latent bug.** `PortWait` ignored `arg1` — where the schema says a `PortEventRecord` goes — and one blob reached it with `PortBind`'s source id still in the register, so a kernel honouring its own ABI would have written a record to `0xc02`. `ChannelCall` ignored the deadline; `ChannelRecv` ignored the args struct the message is supposed to land in; two `DebugWrite` handlers read the pointer and not the length. Each reads every declared register now, answering the documented zero case and refusing what this substrate cannot do rather than ignoring the register — and the five receiving blobs carry a real `ChannelMsgArgs` with `inline_len = 0`, which `channel_msg.isl` already describes as a receiver wanting the wakeup and not the bytes. **Four inversions, all discriminating**: a handler that stops reading `arg1` (named by file and call), a handler that reads an `arg3` nothing declares, the supply blob naming the endpoint instead of the object (exit 65), and a supply for an offset the kernel did not ask for (exit 65). **And the D296 gate earned its keep on its first real change**: dropping one unused `extern` from `syscall_abi.isl` moved the schema's IR digest, `//tools/checks:abi_test` reported both the schema and the surface digest, and the lock was updated deliberately — `abi-version` stays 1, because no call's frame moved. | Nothing in D248 is open. The x86-64 demo handlers remain a second implementation of mechanisms the composed path also has, which Phase 1's close flagged and Phase 3's did not resolve. |
| D297 | **The lint gates are green, and the budget that hid them is gone.** Two invocations report the tree's formatting and clippy findings — `bazel build //... --config=lint` for everything the host platform can configure, and `tools/ci/arch-lint.sh` for the five ports behind the platform transition the aspects cannot cross (D183) — and neither runs under `bazel test //...`. So they had accumulated: **10 host targets failing** (14 rustfmt diffs across 7 files and 8 clippy findings) and **512 findings across the five ports**, against a baseline file that said 502. **This was not toolchain drift, which is what it looked like.** Four of the five baselines were exact to the finding, so the pinned 1.97.0 had not moved under the tree; what had happened is that nobody ran the two commands that ask. **445 of the 512 were one lint, and it was wrong.** `clippy::deref_addrof` fires on `(*(&raw mut STATIC)).method()`, the only way this kernel reaches a `static mut`, and its suggested fix — name the static — is `error: creating a mutable reference to mutable static`, denied by `static_mut_refs` in edition 2024. **Measured rather than argued**: the suggestion was applied to one site in `smmu.rs` and produced exactly that error. A finding whose fix does not compile is not a budget item, so it is allowed at each port's crate root with that reasoning beside it, and the arch baseline drops from 502 to **zero on every port** — which is what the baseline file had said should happen the moment a count reached zero. The line per architecture stays at 0 because it is also the list of platforms the gate walks; dropping one now removes an architecture from the gate rather than declaring it clean. **The other 67 were real, and three were defects rather than nits.** `crypto.rs` loaded the same report into the same binding twice, one line shadowing the other. `perf.rs` carried a truncated doc comment — *"Reports a fatal exception and ends the run. Before this existed a kernel"* — orphaned above a section banner by an item that had moved away. And `kstack.rs` called `expect()` inside a crate whose own header says `#![deny(clippy::expect_used)]`, which is a rule that had been unenforceable for as long as nothing ran clippy on that crate. The rest: 20 redundant `*const u8` casts, eight unused imports, five `%` where `is_multiple_of` says it, six elidable lifetimes, a wildcard covering the pattern beside it, a `u32::from` on a `u32`. **What is deliberately kept, and says so.** Register maps whose last entry nothing reads, densely-numbered object-id and kernel-stack blocks where a hole would be the bug, `TCR_BASE`'s `0 << 14` term for a field whose zero *is* its encoding, and the guard-page self-test's unconditional recursion — each an `#[allow]` at the site with the reason, never a number in a file. **And the RISC-V blob stopped restating its constants.** `irq.rs` spelled the RTC's register offsets and the alarm delay as literals with the constants' names in comments beside them, which is two records of one number that cannot disagree out loud; they are `const` operands now, so the assembler takes the constant. **Three inversions, all discriminating**: clippy's `deref_addrof` fix applied (does not compile, which is the whole argument); one unused constant added to `arm32` (`FAIL: arm32 has 1 lint findings, up from 0`); and `RTC_ALARM_DELAY_NS` moved to 600 seconds, which exits 65 instead of 33 and proves the blob reads the constant rather than a literal that happens to match. All 27 boot checks re-run from cold, all passing. | The structural half: `bazel test //...` still runs neither gate, so this can accumulate again between one `tools/ci/presubmit.sh` and the next. |
| D296 | **The ABI is an artifact, and user space builds against it — Phase 4 closes.** Phase 4 is *"done when the user-space tree builds against a published ABI artifact with no path into `kernel/`"*. D295 met the second half. This is the first, and it is also the half of D248 that has been standing since Phase 0: the reference was *"generated, gated, and published nowhere"* — `tools/ci/docs.sh` built 32 pages on every run and dropped them, so the surface could be generated for somebody without the kernel source and not fetched by them. **What it takes to hand over is not a document.** `//api/abi:abi_bundle` is 1.6 MB and 148 files: the 33 schemas, their compiled IR, 8,251 lines of reference, 15,933 lines of generated Rust bindings **and a `BUILD.bazel` declaring them**, the wire runtime those bindings call, `userspace/uabi`, a `VERSION`, and a `MANIFEST` measuring all of it. **The BUILD file is the difference between an artifact and a directory.** With it, `//api/isl:process_abi_bindings` — the label a program already writes — resolves against the release instead of against a schema compiler, so nothing in a program changes; if a program had to be edited to build against the published ABI, the published ABI would not be what the program was written against. **The proof, run.** `//userspace/...` builds to completion — 260 targets — in a tree with `kernel/` deleted **and** `api/isl` reduced to the artifact: no schemas, no `islc`. The tar is byte-identical across a `bazel clean --expunge`, because an artifact whose digest depends on when it was built cannot be the thing a version refers to. Deleting one library from the artifact's BUILD file fails that build at analysis, so the artifact is what is being compiled against rather than something left behind. **The gate is the ABI diff `docs/api/03` has specified since it was written** and nothing implemented: *"the ABI diff tool operating on compiled schema IR, not source text, so formatting changes cannot mask semantic ones"*. `api/abi/surface.lock` records SHA-256 over each schema's compiled IR and one digest over the set (`886ab0ce…`), and `//tools/checks:abi_test` recomputes both through the ISL compiler. **The lock is source, not build output**, for the reason D146 gives about the store's anchor: a build that emitted both the artifact and the record certifying it would certify whatever it happened to produce. **IR and not source text, measured in both directions.** Swapping two fields of `ProcessCreateArgs` moves `job` from offset 16 to 24 and fails the gate by name; rewording a comment three lines above it changes nothing, and the gate stays green. A digest over source text gets both of those wrong. **And the two ends are held together**: `uabi::ABI_VERSION` is the version a program was compiled against, the lock is the version the artifact publishes, and a tree where they differ fails — publishing one surface while compiling another is exactly the drift D248 measured between D54 and Phase 0. **Four inversions, all discriminating**: the field swap (names the schema and the surface digest), the comment rewrite (must *not* fire, and does not), `ABI_VERSION` bumped to 2 (names both numbers), and a library removed from the artifact (the build against it fails). **What the gate does not decide, said out loud.** Whether a change deserved a new version is a compatibility judgement and only a person can make it; the gate holds the mechanical half — the lock matches the tree, every schema is accounted for in both directions, and the compiled-against version is the published one. **And one imprecision the artifact carries rather than hides.** `HandleDuplicate` and `PageSupply` still have two argument forms in this tree, and the published reference says so on its own first page, because D248 recorded that divergence rather than resolving it and resolving it is a change to a boot check. A published surface that names where it is imprecise is worth more than one that is silently wrong about a call. | Resolving those two argument forms, which is the last of D248 and the only thing left that makes the published surface state one of two truths. |
| D295 | **A user-space program's only path to the kernel is the ABI, and a gate holds it there (composition roadmap Phase 4, the intermediate step).** Phase 4's condition for splitting a repository is that the interface across the boundary is frozen, generated and gated, and its available-now step is to make the boundary enforceable and *"prove it by building all 34 user-space components with the kernel tree absent"*. It would not have built. `bazel query 'filter("^//kernel", deps(//userspace/...))'` answered with **nine kernel packages** — `virtio`, `nvme`, `pl061`, `xhci`, `sdhci`, `sdhci-mock`, `pci`, `devicetree`, and `karch` behind the last of them — reached by nine user-space packages. **None of them was kernel code.** Six have no dependencies at all; every one puts register access behind a trait, forbids `unsafe`, and is host-tested against a mock. They were under `kernel/` because the kernel was the first thing that needed them, which is a fact about history rather than about layering — `docs/architecture/01` puts driver hosts *above* the kernel, and the same virtio core drives a disk from inside the kernel on one port and from ring 3 on another. So they move to `drivers/`, and nothing they do changes. **The one real coupling was the Device Tree reader**, which normalized into `tessera-karch`'s `MemoryRegion`. It now reports its own `Region` — a base, a length, and usable-or-not, which is the only distinction firmware draws — and the four ports that read a device tree widen that into the kernel's eight kinds through one `MemoryRegion::described`, exactly as the x86-64 glue already does for Limine's map. A reader of firmware's word needed no dependency on the kernel to be understood by it. **The gate is a reachability question, not a list of forbidden edges** (`tools/checks/src/boundary.rs`): no package under `userspace/` may reach a package under `kernel/` **by any path**. The direct edge is the obvious break; the one that actually happened is a hop further out — a bus driver on a device-tree reader on the architecture seam — and a gate reading `deps` a line at a time would have called it clean. Visibility lists are not edges, because they are the arrow pointing the other way, and comments are not edges, because a documentation tree has to be able to name what it describes. **The proof the phase asked for, run**: `//userspace/...` builds to completion — 260 targets, 736 actions — in a tree with `kernel/` deleted. The same command against the tree as it stood at D294 fails in analysis: *"no such package 'kernel/virtio'"*. **Four inversions, all failing**: a direct dependency added to `blk-client` (the violation names `//userspace/blk-client -> //kernel/kcore`); `karch` restored to the device-tree reader (`//userspace/platform-bus -> //drivers/devicetree -> //kernel/karch`, the shape of the original defect); the visibility strip removed (36 false violations, so the strip is load-bearing rather than decorative); and the region-kind conversion inverted, which turns every RAM bank into a reservation and exits 65 instead of 33. **The aarch64 image is 5,260,032 bytes before and after** — Phase 4's own prediction list says to measure the image at each phase rather than predict it, and a move that was only a move should not move it. **What this does not yet claim.** The boundary is real; the artifact is not published. `userspace/uabi` is 550 lines and the ISL reference is still generated on every CI run and dropped, which is the half of D248 that D294 also left standing. Phase 4 is *"done when the user-space tree builds against a published ABI artifact"*, and this is the first half of that sentence. | Phase 4's second half: publish the reference and the ABI as artifacts, so the tree builds against a version of the surface rather than against the tree that generates it. |
| D294 | **A program off the volume runs, which closes Phase 2's third bullet and Phase 2.** The exit criterion is *"a binary placed on the ext2 image by the build runs without being linked to anything"*, and D293 left it half met: the bytes were delivered and judged loadable, but nothing ran them. **What was missing was a composition, not a mechanism.** `exec` needs one process holding both a job — `Rights::CREATE_PROCESS` — and a path to a real filesystem; the root task had the job and the tree's only ELF loader, the fs check had the volume and no job. So the fs check seeds its client a job, publishes the same `ROOT_LOADER` seam the root-task check publishes, and `fs-client` reads `/program.elf`, creates a process, maps its segments and starts it. The seam was never the root task's — only its use of it was, which is what a `None`-outside-a-run static made easy to mistake for ownership. **The ELF parse moved to `//userspace/elfload` and got its first tests.** A second loader is what makes extracting a hundred lines of header arithmetic right rather than speculative, and the extraction is what makes it *testable*: a `no_main` ring-3 binary has no host test target, so every refusal in that parser had been exercised only by booting a machine that never presented a malformed image. Seven tests now present them — bad magic, wrong class, wrong byte order, `ET_REL`, another machine, a segment past the end, `memsz < filesz`, a writable-executable segment, no `PT_LOAD`, truncation at four lengths — and removing the W^X refusal fails them. That is the same lesson as the code generator D290 moved: **the fix for logic no test can reach is to move the code, not to add a test around it.** **A paged file must be touched before the kernel is asked to read it.** The loader hands the kernel addresses in its own address space and the kernel copies from them at EL1, where a missing page is not a request to a pager but a data abort in the kernel — `far` one page past the mapping, translation fault, no line to blame. One volatile read per page before the load is the whole fix, and the shape generalises: **the user fault path is the only thing that can service a paged object, so anything the kernel will read must be resident first.** **The client's kernel stack is thirty-two pages, not eight.** D252 wrote down that a `ProcessCreate` builds a 30 KB `Process` inside a syscall and that a child wanting to start processes of its own would need the same; this is that child, and the inversion is a guard-page write abort rather than a wrong answer. Its reclaim uses its own count, because reclaiming eight pages of a thirty-two-page window leaves twenty-four mapped and their frames unaccounted. **The strongest claim is checked from outside the machine.** The program carries a marker string in its own `.rodata` — a string and not the report constant, because at `-Copt-level=2` an integer immediate is built in registers and never reaches `.rodata` at all, so grepping for its bytes finds nothing in either artifact and the check passes for the wrong reason. The boot script requires that marker **in the ext2 image and absent from the kernel image**: a boot that ran a program it was carrying all along passes every in-guest marker and fails this one. **Four inversions, all failing**: the program parsed but never run (the sink differs by exactly the child's report), the volume built without it, the client's kernel stack back to eight pages, and the parser's W^X refusal deleted. **Phase 2 is closed.** The program store is still linked into the image and that is now a size decision rather than a capability gap — this kernel has run a program it was not carrying. | Phase 4, and the ports: the loader seam, the job seed and the paged-image rule are all `kcore` and all portable, but only aarch64 composes a filesystem to run anything off. |
| D293 | **A program that is in no kernel image is delivered by the filesystem.** Phase 2's third bullet is *`exec` from the filesystem*, and its exit criterion is *"a binary placed on the ext2 image by the build runs without being linked to anything"*. This is the first half: `userspace/disk-program` is built, placed on the ext2 volume, and is in **no store, no accessor and no image** — `//components` does not name it and no kernel links it — and `fs-client` reads it back through the composed filesystem path and establishes that what came off is a loadable image for this machine. **What is not here is the running**, and the reason is a composition that does not exist rather than a mechanism that is missing: `exec` needs one process holding both a job — `Rights::CREATE_PROCESS` — and a path to a real filesystem, and today those live in two different checks with two different process sets. The root task has the job and the tree's only ELF loader; the fs check has the filesystem and no job. Closing that gap is the next milestone, and it is composition work. **The image builder gained one optional file, and the invariant it has is now written down rather than assumed.** `mkimage.sh` says the host tests' image and the boot check's cannot differ — they now differ by exactly one file, because that ELF is a Bazel artifact and the cargo inner loop cannot produce one. The same script, the same tool, the same layout rules, the same pinned UUID, and every path the host tests look up is in both; what the rule forbids is two *builders*, which this still refuses to be. **The header check needed a negative before it meant anything.** Reading a valid ELF and confirming it is one cannot fail: with the only file it is ever shown a good one, deleting the check entirely passes. So `/hello.txt` goes through the same path and must be **refused** — a real file on the same volume, fetched the same way, rejected for what it is. **And the three sub-checks needed distinct failure codes**, which is the second thing that made an inversion pass: magic, machine and loadability all answered `fail(0xe5, 0)` for a short file, so the negative could not say which had refused and disabling any of them left it satisfied. **The architecture check has no negative and is kept anyway**, unlike the guard D292 removed — the difference is that without it a wrong-architecture image is *accepted* here and fails inside a loader later, which is a worse outcome than the one the check produces, where D292's guard produced the same outcome either way. **Three inversions, two failing and one stated**: the program absent from the volume, and the ELF-magic check removed. The architecture check's is the one nothing on this volume can pose. | The composition `exec` needs: one process holding a job and a filesystem at once. That is the rest of the third bullet, and with it Phase 2. |
| D292 | **The aarch64 image carries no system store at all, which finishes Phase 2's second bullet.** D291 built the delivery — a component offers a container, the kernel measures it against anchors in its own source — and left the image's own copy behind as a fallback for boots with no device. That copy is gone. `//store:system_store_image` is off this port's dependency list, `host::system_store()` no longer exists, and every container this kernel sees came off a medium. **The claims moved with it, and that is the substance of the change.** `store.ok`, `store.refused` and the four firmware claims were made in the *diskless* smoke boot, against the container that image carried. They are made in the ring3-host boot now, against the one `blk-client` read off the disk through the composed block path — so they say something they could not say before: that the format holds for a container the kernel did not carry, and that firmware policy runs against it. A boot with no medium prints that it has no store and skips, which is a fact reported rather than a gap filled from `.rodata`. **One bug, and the shape of it is worth keeping.** A component reading a container in fixed-size blocks hands over whatever the last block padded it to — sixteen sectors for a container of thirteen. The kernel kept all of it, which measures identically, because the anchor covers the header and directory and the padding is past both. What noticed was the store's own self-check: it flips a byte to prove a changed container is refused, the byte it flipped landed in the padding, and the tampered open *succeeded* — `TamperedBlobOpened`, exactly the error that check exists to raise. The region is trimmed to `Store::byte_len` now, which is the header's own answer to where the container ends. **A store that is padding and calls itself a store measures the same and behaves differently**, and the only thing that could tell was a check designed to distrust it. **Seven inversions across the two rows, all failing**: no copy, no latch, no anchor-id check, verification against the container's own measurement, a client that does not deliver, a container changed after signing on the disk, and now an untrimmed region. **What Phase 2 still owes is the third bullet alone.** The program store is still linked into the image — that is what `exec` from the filesystem removes, and it is the capability the rest of Stage 1 is blocked on. The other four ports still link a container for their own format check; they compose no block path at all, which is the same reason every driver milestone in this tree has been aarch64-first. | `exec` from the filesystem: the ELF loader taking bytes from `fs-service` rather than from `.rodata`, which is the last of Phase 2 and the one the sequencing document says everything else waits on. |
| D291 | **The system store comes off a medium, carried by a component the kernel does not believe.** Phase 2's second bullet: *"Once the block path is composed, the container is read from the device it was always meant to come from."* `SystemStoreInstall` (54) is how it arrives — a component offers bytes, and the kernel measures them against `TRUSTED_ANCHORS`, which is its own source and cannot be replaced at run time. **The authority a caller holds is delivery, never trust**, which is the whole point of a verified store: it can come from somewhere untrusted precisely because nothing about it is taken on the word of whoever carried it. **Three rules, each with a test that can only fail if it is enforced.** The bytes are *copied* into the kernel before being measured — a store read through the caller's memory is one the caller can rewrite between the check and the use, which is `docs/kernel/04`'s validate-then-use race and the reason the out-of-line modes exist. The container must carry the **system store's own anchor id**: every anchor this kernel holds is a real one, so without that check a container signed for another purpose — the machine's program store, say — would be installable as its firmware source. And it installs **once**, a second call refused whether or not it would verify, because a root of trust that can be replaced while the system runs is one whose replacement is the attack. **The obvious shape of the copy was the wrong one.** Reading the caller's bytes into a local put sixteen kilobytes on the kernel stack of a ring-3 thread — a few pages with a guard page under it, which is the overflow this ledger already records from a `Process` built by value in a syscall, arriving by a different route. The caller fills the destination through a closure instead, so nothing container-sized is ever on that stack; the ring-3 side needed the same treatment for its own eight kilobytes. **`BlockReadReply.data` is 64 bytes, and finding that out was the integration.** The inline read exists to prove the contract, not to move a sector, so the client fetches the container through the *out-of-line* path — a buffer it hands over and gets back, thirteen times. A `copy_from_slice` of 512 bytes out of a 64-byte reply is a panic in ring 3, which presented as a boot check failing with no fault line and no syscall ever reaching the kernel. **What is proved end to end**: the ring3-host boot puts the container on the test disk at sector 8, `blk-client` reads it through the composed block path — device host, block class, out-of-line buffers — and installs it, and the firmware check that runs later in the same boot uses *that* store. A new claim, `firmware.store-from-medium`, is what says so, and the diskless smoke boot prints the fallback instead. **Two inversions on the delivery, both failing**: a client that does not deliver (the kernel falls back and the claim is absent), and a container changed after it was signed on the disk (refused, and the claim is absent). Four more on the kernel seam: no copy, no latch, no anchor-id check, and verification against the container's own measurement. **The bullet is half met and the half is named.** The store the *firmware syscall* reads now comes from a medium. The image still carries a copy, used when no component delivered one — which is every diskless boot, including the one where the store's own format check runs. Removing it means moving those claims onto a boot that has a device, which is a rearrangement of what each check asserts rather than a mechanism that is missing. | The `store.verified` boot claim onto a boot with a device, which retires the last linked `SYSTEM_STORE` use on this port; then Phase 2's third bullet, `exec` from the filesystem, which is what finally removes the program store from the image too. |
| D290 | **The programs a machine boots come out of a signed store, not out of thirty linked symbols.** Phase 2's first bullet says the store should be *"finally carrying its subject"*, and this is that: `//components` builds one container per machine holding every ring-3 program it starts, signed with the key D289 made possible, and the generated `tessera_components` accessors read from it by name. **What changes is not where the bytes are but what is known about them.** They are still in the kernel image — the container is embedded as one symbol where thirty were — so this is not yet the second bullet, which takes the store off a block device. What it is, is the first time a program this kernel starts is one something vouched for: each entry carries its blob's digest, checked on every read, and the directory carries a signature, checked once. **The anchor is a key and had to be.** A pinned measurement changes whenever any program does, which would leave a constant nobody could keep current — the fork D289 recorded and answered. So `TRUSTED_ANCHORS` now holds two entries under two ids: the system store's measurement, and the program store's public key. Two ids rather than one, because anchors are looked up by id and a verifier that accepted either container where the other was meant would make the distinction decorative. **The signature is verified once and the measurement remembered.** Ed25519 is a curve operation and there are thirty programs; what is cached is the expensive half, and every `open` still measures the blob it returns — which is the half that has to run per program anyway. A `static mut` rather than an atomic, because this is reached on the boot CPU before anything else is scheduled and a lock here would be a lock on the path that starts the first process. **A refused store yields empty slices, not unchecked bytes**, which is the absence every boot check already reports rather than the linked-symbol behaviour wearing a store's clothes. **The composition map now names binaries.** It named `*_image` targets — embedded-bytes crates — and the store needs the ELFs themselves; each `tessera_user_binary` gained `//components` visibility, and the per-program embed targets are now unreferenced. **A gate holds the seed and the anchor together** (`//components:program_anchor_test`), because nothing derives one from the other: a seed changed without the key being updated would produce a kernel that refuses every program it carries, and the only symptom would be a boot with nothing started. It prints the `mkstore pubkey` command to run. It also asserts the refusal — a container altered after signing, in a field the reader carries and never judges, because a byte flipped in a length fails the parse and never reaches the signature. **The signing key is a development key and is in the build file in plain sight**, which is what it is: anyone reading this repository can sign with it. What it buys is that the mechanism runs end to end, not that the signature means anything against an adversary; `docs/security/02`'s custody, rotation and revocation are untouched (D173, D289). | The store off the block device rather than out of `.rodata`, which is Phase 2's second bullet and the first time the kernel image stops carrying the programs at all. The now-unreferenced `*_image` targets go with it. |
| D289 | **A container can be authenticated by a key, not only by a pinned measurement.** Phase 2's first bullet moves the ring-3 program images into the verified store, and it has a fork in it the bullet does not name: the store's integrity today is a digest checked into kernel source, which works because its four blobs are generated from fixed seeds and never change. Program images change on every userspace edit — so the moment they go in, that constant is stale on every build and the boot refuses to mount until somebody pastes a new one. **The format already anticipated this.** `image_store.isl` documents `anchor_id` as a *key* identifier and points at `docs/security/02`'s trust anchors; what existed underneath was the degenerate case. An `Anchor` now holds a `Trust` — a `Digest` or a `Key` — and a container carrying `signature_offset` (header version 3) can be verified by Ed25519 over its own anchor. **The two are not equivalent and the weaker one is named as weaker.** A pinned digest says *a human approved these exact bytes*; a key says *whoever holds the secret produced them*, which on a build machine is the build itself. So the split was chosen deliberately: the existing store keeps its pinned anchor, because its content is fixed and there is something stable for a human to have approved, and the program store to come uses a key, because there is not. **Three structural rules, each with a test that can only fail if it is enforced.** The signature is over the *anchor*, so it covers the header and every entry's digest and stays one fixed-size operation however large the container is. It lives **after** the measured region — a signature inside what it attests to would be signing itself, and an offset pointing there is refused rather than read. And an unsigned container meets a key anchor with `Unsigned`, **never a fallback to comparing measurements**: an artifact that could choose to be checked the weaker way by omitting the signature would make the stronger check optional for whoever omitted it. **The tamper test corrupts a field nothing validates**, the first entry's `flags`, and the two earlier attempts are why: a byte flipped in a length, a name or a reserved word fails the parse and never reaches the signature, so a test written that way passes with verification stubbed out entirely. **Three inversions, all failing**: an unsigned container falling back to a digest comparison, verification that always succeeds, and a signature allowed inside the measured region. **The signer is a test-only dependency of `api/image-store` and a real one of `mkstore`**, which is the same line `tools/ed25519-signer` already draws: this crate verifies and must never be able to sign, and reaching the positive path at all needs something that can. The seed on `mkstore --sign-key` is a build-machine key and stated as one — `docs/security/02`'s custody, rotation and revocation are untouched, exactly as D173 says. The system store's pinned anchor moved once, because the header grew a field; the gate printed it and it was pasted, which is the procedure working rather than an exception to it. | The program store itself: a per-machine container of the ring-3 images, signed, mounted by the kernel, with `tessera_components` reading from it instead of from thirty linked symbols. That is the rest of Phase 2's first bullet. |
| D288 | **A frame stops being an object in the other direction too.** D287 did transmit; this does receive, and the asymmetry is the whole design. The transmit region is the client's because the client fills it. Frames *arrive* by DMA, so the receive region has to be memory the device can reach — device-visible contiguity, an attachment, an address the NIC was told about — and none of that is a client's to arrange. **The driver creates it, attaches it once, and lends a read-only view**, which `AttachReceiveRegion` (9) answers with a share in its reply. `OnFrameAt` (24) then names a slot instead of carrying a capability, and `ReleaseFrame` (10) gives the slot back. **Zero-copy stays zero-copy, which is why the ownership is not inverted.** The NIC writes into the region and the stack reads the same bytes. A client-owned region would mean the driver copying out of its DMA pages into the client's — a copy per frame added to remove an object per frame, which is not a trade worth making. **The region is one page, and the kernel decides that, not this driver.** `DmaAttach` refuses a multi-page object where there is no IOMMU, and says why in as many words: physical frames are not contiguous, so a multi-page object has no single base. The slot count is therefore *derived* — one page over one buffer's worth — and comes out at two, which is enough for the thing one could not do: the driver posts the second while the client still holds the first. A machine with an SMMU gets more without anybody re-deriving it. **The ceiling falls again, 133 -> 115, and less than transmit did because this buys something back.** A frame arriving stops costing an object created by the driver, mapped by the stack and closed by it — three — and starts costing one `ReleaseFrame`, because a lent slot has to be returned. Net two per frame against transmit's four. That call is the price of doing flow control by message instead of by producer and consumer indices in shared memory, which is the trade `docs/lifecycle/04` asks for by forbidding ad hoc lock-free. **The lending is bounded at both ends and neither bound is reachable from ordinary traffic.** A client returning an arbitrary offset frees a slot it was never lent, or the same slot twice — which would have the driver post two buffers into one place. The stack only ever returns an offset it was just given, so `net-client` asks instead, beside D287's transmit-side probe and out of the conformance transcript for the same reason. **Two bugs found by running it, and both were about mixing the two paths.** A completion from a buffer posted *before* the client attached was reported as a slot — the frame delivered from an address it was not in, which is worse than a frame lost; the route now follows what is actually posted rather than whether a region exists. And the transferred buffer, handed away and deliberately not replaced, was still described as present: the next frame detached a handle that had left the process. `Option` is what makes that unrepresentable rather than unlikely. **A third was the window collision D279 already taught once**: the region's permanent mapping was given the address the per-datagram payload window recycles, so the first copy-out failed and every one after it. Mapping is not idempotent, and a permanent window sharing an address with a reused one is that fact waiting to be rediscovered. **Four inversions, all failing**: no region asked for, slots never released, the release offset unvalidated, and the route taken on whether a region exists rather than on what is posted — which is the first of the two bugs above, kept as the check that would have caught it. | The rate, which needs the R1 hardware D56 has been waiting for and is now the only thing between this phase and its exit criterion as it was written. What is left in this path is the client's own payload object per datagram, which is the flow contract's `transfer handle` and a deliberate part of it rather than an overhead. |
| D287 | **A frame stops being an object.** D285 measured the same sentence twice from two directions: every segment this stack puts on the wire is a memory object created, mapped, handed away and closed, whatever the segment is for. It is no longer true of the transmit direction. `network_driver.isl` gains `AttachTransmitRegion` (7) and `TransmitAt` (8): the stack shares one region with the driver at startup and every frame after it is a message naming an offset. **This is what D286 was for**, and the schema said so before it existed — `NetTransmitBufferRequest`'s note names `SHARED_FOR_CALL` as the mode it wanted and records that `TransferMode::SHARE` was decoded and then refused. **The data-path ceiling falls, for the first time.** 193 -> 133, and the arithmetic is exact: fifteen frames at four syscalls each — 15 objects, 30 mappings (the stack's and the driver's), 16 closes — against one extra call to attach the region. Sixty fewer. A ratchet that has only ever risen is a ratchet nobody has tested falling. **The bound is the security-relevant half, and nothing in ordinary operation exercises it.** An offset and a length from a client are exactly the two numbers that name memory outside what it lent, and the stack always sends offset zero with a frame it just built — so a driver that dropped the check would serve every real run correctly and read past its mapping on a malformed one. `net-client`, the class-conformance probe, now shares a region and asks for a frame one byte past its end, out of the transcript for the same reason its DHCP exchange is: it asks one driver about one optional method rather than judging the class. With the bound removed the driver reads unmapped memory and dies, which is the harm demonstrated rather than described. **The validate-then-use warning the schema compiler emits on a `share` field is correct and does not bite here**, which is worth saying rather than suppressing: this driver parses nothing out of the region — it checks a `length` that arrives inline and copies that many bytes into its own DMA page, so there is no validated fact about the contents for a later write to invalidate. **A pre-existing leak was found by the restructuring**: the send path's frame builders ended each arm with `?`, returning before the transmit object was closed, so every frame that failed to build leaked a page. Invisible while nothing failed. **Both methods stay optional and both paths stay live.** A driver that answers `NOT_SUPPORTED` is conformant and the stack falls back to handing over an object per frame — which is what makes the region an addition rather than a replacement, and what the fallback path's own bug above was hiding in. **Four inversions, three of which fail**: no region attached (the claim bit goes, *and* the ceiling catches the cost), the region sent as a transfer rather than a share (the stack loses the memory it is building frames into and dies), and the dropped bound. The fourth passed and the code went rather than the check: a hand-written "already attached" guard produced the same status as the kernel's own refusal to map twice at one address, so nothing could tell them apart — an untested branch on a driver's request path is worse than no branch. | The receive direction, which is still an object per frame in the other direction and is the larger half of what is left; then the rate, which needs the R1 hardware D56 has been waiting for and is the only thing between this phase and its exit. |
| D286 | **Two processes can hold one memory object, and its frames go when the last of them lets go.** D131 shipped `TRANSFER` and deferred `SHARE` with a reason: sharing needs both holders' references counted, and three of the five ports had no `ObjectTable` to count in. The reason has been stale for a while — all five carry one now — but the more useful discovery is that **it was the wrong table**. What a shared memory object needs counted is which *processes* hold it, and that is known where the object already lives: `MemoryObject.owner` becomes a bounded set of holders, and no object table is involved at all. **Three paths decide the lifetime and all three had to change.** `handle_close` compared a single owner and now asks whether this process is *a* holder, lets go, and frees only when none remain. The exit sweep did the same for a whole process. The receive side adds a holder instead of substituting one — and it can only do that because the message carries the mode: by the time a receiver adopts a capability there is nothing left to derive it from, and the sender's still-holding-a-handle is a race rather than a fact. The sending side does the rest: `share_narrowed` takes `&self` and removes nothing, which is `take_narrowed`'s undoability problem answered by construction rather than by ordering, and a share revokes no mapping, which is the whole of what sharing means. **Sharing is delegation, so it needs `TRANSFER`.** A capability granted without it may be used and not passed on; a share that skipped the check would make every non-delegable grant in the system delegable by asking the other way. **Bounded, and refused rather than partially recorded**, with `LimitExceeded` — a holder the kernel forgot is memory freed while somebody is still reading it, which is the one failure this whole mechanism exists to prevent. Membership is per process rather than per handle, matching the question `handle_close` was already asking. **Five inversions, and two of them passed first.** A share that recorded no holder was invisible until a test drove the *receive* path through `ChannelRecv` rather than calling the table; and an exit sweep that freed a shared object out from under its other holder was invisible until a test held one with two processes and watched the free list. Both are now covered, and both fail. The other three — a share that takes the sender's handle anyway, a close that frees regardless, a holder set that counts one process twice — fail as written. **No ring-3 program shares anything yet**, and that is stated rather than hidden: the first will be the shared frame buffer between the stack and the driver, which is what this unblocks and which is a network change rather than a kernel one. The SDK carries both modes now (`Transfer::moved`, `Transfer::shared`) and every existing hand-over says `shared: false` at its call site, because the mode is a real decision and the safe answer should be the visible one. `docs/kernel/04`'s out-of-line section moves from three described modes to two implemented and one refused. | The shared frame buffer: one object the stack fills and the driver drains, replacing the object-per-segment the flow path pays today — which the ceiling has now said twice is the largest cost in it. Snapshot stays unimplemented and needs copy-on-write, which is a different milestone entirely. |
| D285 | **More than one segment can be in flight, and the buffer's limit is the peer's window.** D284 ended with the sentence this row makes false: a send buffer, which is what lets a connection move more than one segment per round trip. `Connection` now holds `MAX_UNACKED` segments oldest-first instead of one. An acknowledgement is **cumulative** and releases a prefix — one `ack` retiring several is the ordinary case once more than one is out, and a release that only looked at the front would leave the rest outstanding forever. A timeout retransmits **the front and nothing else** (RFC 6298 (5.4)), because sending the whole buffer again turns one loss into a burst and a burst into the next loss. There is **one timer, and it belongs to the front** (5.1): a segment queued behind inherits it rather than starting a second, and the timer restarts for whatever is left when an acknowledgement retires part of the queue (5.3) — a deadline carried over from the segment just acknowledged would fire against data that has been in flight for a fraction of its timeout. **The peer's advertised window is now read and honoured**, from the handshake and from every acknowledgement after it. With one segment outstanding this could be ignored, because one segment is under any window a peer would advertise; with a buffer it cannot, and sending past a receiver's window is how a sender makes a receiver drop what it asked for. A write that would overflow the buffer or the window is refused, which is what a full send buffer means. **`Connection` stops being `Copy`, and that is the point of the change rather than a consequence.** It is about a kilobyte now, and `net-stack` was copying it in and out of its own state twice per frame — affordable while it held one segment, not on a per-packet path. Every site borrows it instead, scoped so the borrow ends before the transmit that needs the stack mutably. **The end-to-end leg does not discriminate, and that was measured.** The client writes twice with no read between and both echoes come back — but putting the old refusal back (the service rejecting a write while one is unacknowledged) leaves the check passing, because the peer's acknowledgement arrives between the client's two calls and nothing is outstanding by the time the second lands. On a link this fast, whether two segments are in flight together is not something a client can arrange, so it is not asserted; the buffer is proved in `api/net`, where the peer is written by the test. The leg is kept for what it does cover — a wrong length back, a read loop that mishandles a coalesced echo, a sequence that does not advance — and the honest label is on the constant. **Four inversions fail and the fifth is the one above**: a buffer of one, an acknowledgement that releases only the front, no timer restart for what is left, and an ignored window. `tcp::write_with_window` is added so a test can model a receiver that is filling up; this end's own window is still fixed, and becomes that function's caller the day it moves. The data-path ceiling moves 141 -> 193 — two round trips at the 18 D277 measured, and acknowledgements, which are frames and cost what frames cost. That is the retransmission's 23 arriving from the other direction: **every segment this stack puts on the wire is a memory object created, mapped, handed away and closed**, whatever the segment is for. | The shared buffer that makes the previous sentence false, still blocked on the object table D131 named — it is now the largest single cost in this path and the only one whose fix is a kernel change rather than a network one. Then a receive window that moves, and reassembly. |
| D284 | **A lost segment is sent again, and a peer that says nothing is given up on.** D280 shipped TCP with the gap named in its own row: no retransmission timer, so *"over a link that loses a segment it stalls, and it stalls silently"* — and that was not deferred by preference, it needed a clock that did not exist. D281 built the clock and D282 gave a wait a deadline; this is the transport that was waiting on both. `api/net`'s connection now holds the segment it sent until it is acknowledged, measures the round trip, derives the timeout from it, doubles on every miss, and gives up loudly in a new `State::Aborted` rather than leaving a caller waiting for a byte that is never coming. **RFC 6298, with three departures, each stated where it is made.** The floor under a computed timeout is 200 ms where (2.4) says a second — Linux's `TCP_RTO_MIN`, and for Linux's reason: that SHOULD is sized for the open internet and every link here is local. The give-up bound is 8 s where RFC 1122 asks for at least 100, on the same argument. And the give-up is a **time** rather than a count, which is what RFC 1122 actually specifies `R2` to be: a pure count of three would end an unanswered handshake at 15 s, waiting out a fourth doubled timeout to deliver a decision already made at 8. Karn's algorithm is honoured — a segment sent twice measures nothing, because the acknowledgement may answer either copy and guessing wrong shortens the timeout on a link that has just proved it loses things. The arithmetic is checked against values worked out from the RFC's text rather than recomputed with the module's own expressions, which would check nothing. **What the timer costs is a rule about what may be sent.** A write larger than the connection can hold, and a second write while the first is unacknowledged, are now **refused** — a segment that cannot be sent again is one the timer cannot protect, and sending it anyway would restore the silent stall with a working timer standing in front of it. `net-stack`'s per-call limit falls 512 -> 256 for that reason. A `Connect` also loses its own 100 ms deadline: it is answered by the handshake or by the transport giving up, and a fixed second timer was a worse answer to the same question that reported `UNREACHABLE` while the SYN was still in flight. **The check needed a black hole, and finding one was the work.** QEMU's user-mode network terminates TCP on the host and answers everything: a closed port with a reset, an outside address by opening a host socket whose failure depends on the host's routing table. Two candidates were captured on the wire rather than reasoned about. `restrict=on` does give silence — and stops slirp answering DHCPv6, killing an existing leg. An unassigned address in slirp's own subnet was silent *on this host*, which is the environment dependence D280 refused. What works is the guest's **own** address: slirp forwards it nowhere, drops it, and involves no host socket at all. So the give-up leg is deterministic on any host with no flag and no filter. **Five inversions, all run.** Holding nothing for retransmission, and a serve loop that waits on its client's deadline while ignoring the connection's — that second one was the blind spot before the black-hole leg existed, and on a lossless link nothing could have caught it. Then measuring a retransmitted segment, and not clamping the deadline to the give-up horizon. One more passed and was rewritten: swapping the two assignments in the RFC 6298 update changes nothing, because the difference is taken from a local binding — so the code has no ordering to get wrong, and the comment now says that rather than warning about a trap that is not there. **A report-bit collision, for the second time in three rows.** A fourth bit here reached bit 20 and met the client's; the sink XORs, so both claims would have vanished. The regions are now written down — the service owns 8 to 23, the client 0 to 7 and 24 up — instead of being rediscovered by whoever adds the next one. The data-path ceiling moves 118 -> 141, and the 23 are worth reading: three calls for the leg, and then the same SYN built and sent four times, because **a retransmission costs exactly what a transmission costs**. The boot check's pump is now bounded by the clock rather than by a count of wake-ups, which a run that waits eight seconds for a timer had made meaningless. | A send buffer, which is what makes the previous sentence false and lets more than one segment be in flight; then a receive window that moves, and reassembly. Congestion control after that, which is the first thing here that needs more than one connection to be worth anything. |
| D283 | **A client can outlive a service that stops.** D282 bounded the *receive* and named this as what was still missing: a client blocked in `ChannelCall` waits for its answer until the machine is switched off, and a service that is wedged, looping, or never scheduled again is indistinguishable from one that is merely slow. `ChannelCall` takes a deadline in `arg2` now — monotonic nanoseconds, **zero meaning none**, the convention D282 set — and `Executive::call_until` carries it. The thread's deadline field stops being `recv_deadline` and becomes `blocked_deadline`, because one expiry pass now serves two different waits and it does not care which call a thread is parked in. **The kernel's own callers keep the unbounded form deliberately**: a deadline is a policy about how long *this* client will wait for *this* service, and the thing holding that policy is the program making the call. **The call is abandoned, not cancelled**, which is the part that is easy to get wrong and impossible to see afterwards. The request was delivered and the server may still be working on it, so the endpoint records that one reply is owed to nobody and discards it when it arrives — without that, the answer to the abandoned call sits on the queue and the *next* call on that endpoint takes it for its own, with no error anywhere. The mechanism already existed for the pager supervisor (`Endpoint::abort_call`); this is its second caller and the first one a program can reach. **Two orderings decide the semantics.** A deadline already passed refuses *before* the request is sent, so a caller out of time before it asked does not make a server work for an answer nobody will read, and does not spend the one unanswered call the endpoint allows. A reply already in hand beats an expired deadline, because a server can answer in the same instant the clock runs out and there is no reading of "give up waiting" under which an answer this thread is holding is thrown away. **A latent misattribution was fixed on the way**: the callee's causal id was restored only on the path that got a reply, so a server that timed out kept the departed caller's id and misattributed everything it did next — a deadline turns that path from pathological into ordinary. **`run` expires waits once more before it returns**, not only at the head of its loop: a deadline that passed while the threads it dispatched were running was otherwise noticed only when something re-entered `run`, which on a machine with a timer is a tick later and on a check without a pump is never. **Every in-kernel blob that calls now zeroes the deadline register** — seven of them, across x86-64, AArch64 and RISC-V 64 — and so does the SDK, which moves to `syscall3`. Left alone, the register carries whatever the compiler or the previous instruction put there, which is a stack address read as a deadline: no bound at all, or an immediate expiry, depending on the value. Checked rather than assumed: with the AArch64 blob's zero removed the boot still passes, so the register happened to be zero and the hazard is real but unobserved. **The ring-3 leg needs nothing broken to arrange it.** The client creates a channel, holds both ends, receives on neither, and calls on one: the peer is open, so this is not `PeerClosed`, and no thread anywhere will ever reply. Without a deadline the check does not fail, it hangs. **Four inversions, all run and all failing**: discarding the deadline register (`0x1bf00` — the client never reports), never recording the caller's deadline so the expiry pass cannot see it (the same), forgetting the call instead of abandoning it, and dropping the pre-send refusal. **A broken target on master was fixed too**, unrelated to this row: `api/isl-fuzz`'s seed builder still called `udp::write` with two bare addresses, which D278 replaced with a `Peers` value when the writer learned IPv6 — the fuzz target has not built since. The data-path ceiling moves 115 -> 118, which is the call and the two ends it closes and carries no datagram. | Deadlines on the remaining blocking calls — `ChannelRecv` and `PortWait`, neither of which a program can currently escape — and the retransmission timer, which now has every kernel primitive it needs and is TCP's work rather than the kernel's. |

It is therefore a bound with tick-granularity slack rather than an alarm. **The mechanism was invisible to every check, and the inversions are what found that.** Both passed: removing the expiry pass, and making the kernel discard the deadline register outright. Neither could fail, because in a healthy run nothing ever times out — the kcore tests only reached the pre-park check, and the boot check never waited for anything that did not come. It decodes domain and code now. The other was mine: the client's ninth report bit landed on the stack instance's first, and the sink XORs, so the two claims cancelled and the run reported neither — the report is a shared address space and running off the end of one program's byte is running into another's. **A deadline outliving its request** was a third: one left behind by a served request expired the next wait immediately and every wait after, a spin that serves nobody and reads as a hang. The data-path ceiling moves 112 -> 115. | Deadlines on the other blocking calls — `ChannelCall` above all, which is what a client needs to survive a service that stops — and the retransmission timer this finally makes possible, which is TCP's and not the kernel's. |

| D274 | **Two CI steps could not both run in one working tree, found by running them.** `tools/ci/docs.sh` writes the generated interface reference and rustdoc under `build-out/`; `tools/checks`'s walker skips `.git`, `target`, `.cache` and `.claude`, and did not skip `build-out`.
