Nothing had noticed because CI runs the two in separate checkouts and the combination only appears locally, which is the same shape as D268: a gate that is correct in the configuration anybody tests it in. `build-out` joins the list for exactly the reason `target` is on it. **The walker had no tests, which is why this was possible.** Every gate in that directory is a walk plus a predicate, so a directory the walk should not enter is a defect in all of them at once — and it surfaced as four thousand licence violations rather than as anything naming the walk. `tests/walk.rs` now covers both: nothing under any skipped directory is reached at any depth, and `build-out` specifically is on the list. **Inverted and measured**: removing `build-out` from the list fails the unit test naming it, and re-running `docs.sh` before the licence gate fails that gate — both were run, in that order, rather than reasoned about. | None — the list is complete for the outputs this tree writes today. A generated directory added later needs the same line, which the walker's tests will not catch on their own; the thing that would is a gate reading `.gitignore` rather than a constant, and that is a larger change than this defect justifies. |
| D273 | **The socket surface is a schema, and it says what it does not do (composition roadmap Phase 3, third landing).** `docs/roadmap/03` Phase 3: *"the socket surface is an ISL protocol like every other service boundary, and gets its reference page from Phase 0 at no cost"*. `flow_service.isl` is that protocol — `Bind`, `SendTo`, `RecvFrom`, `Close` over datagram flows, with `FlowAddress`, a closed eight-value error set, and payloads that travel as transferred memory objects in both directions. Both halves of the sentence are now true: the schema is conformance-gated like every other, its fuzz target is generated because it declares `@abi`, and `islc emit-docs` produced its reference page with no work at all — **34 pages from 33 schemas**, which is the return D248 promised for writing a backend instead of a document. **What it refuses to guess is the point.** `Listen`, `Accept` and `Connect` are *reserved* at ordinals 5-7 rather than written: a stream is a retransmission timer, a window and a reassembly queue, and none exists here — writing their signatures now would repeat the mistake `docs/api/01` records, twenty families described and none marked. The conformance test pins the reserved slots as well as the four in use, so a fifth method cannot quietly take the one TCP is holding. **Three deviations from `docs/network/01`, each named in the schema rather than discovered later.** There is no data ring: the normative design says a flow arrives as a handle carrying one, and a ring is a shared buffer by definition, so with `TransferMode::SHARE` still refused (D131) this contract costs **an object per datagram** — the per-packet number `docs/architecture/03` wants measured while the shape is cheap to change, and now the thing to measure it on. There is no port authority: *"listening is not ambient authority"* needs a namespace broker this tree does not have, so `port_authority` is a reserved field that must be zero, which is what stops a client compiled against a later schema appearing to hold authority nobody checked. And one flow per client, because a table that can fill needs an eviction story. **`RecvFrom` hands back a different object than it was given**, which looks wasteful and is not: a service cannot write into a buffer it holds `READ` on, and granting `WRITE` so it could would make a caller's memory writable by whoever it last spoke to. | A stack instance serving this contract — `userspace/net-stack` between the client and the driver, so a program with no device capability sends and receives a datagram by asking. The schema is unimplemented until then, which is the same state `network_driver.isl` was in before D150. |
| D272 | **A frame too large to be a message reaches the wire, and QEMU's DHCP server answers it (composition roadmap Phase 3, second landing; closes D271).** D271 measured the ceiling: `NetTransmitRequest.frame` is 64 bytes, `MAX_INLINE_BYTES` caps the whole message at 256 to hold budget B3, and 42 bytes of Ethernet, IPv4 and UDP headers leave **22 bytes of payload** — so no inline transmit can carry a datagram worth sending. The receive direction had solved this in D131 and the transmit direction never did, because ARP at 42 bytes fit. Now `NetworkDevice` gains **`TransmitBuffer` (ordinal 6, from a reserved slot)** taking a `NetTransmitBufferRequest`: the caller creates a memory object, fills it, and hands it over; the driver maps it read-only, copies into its DMA page, and closes the handle — which revokes the mapping and frees the pages in one syscall, because ownership moved to the driver when the message was delivered. A 290-byte DHCP DISCOVER built by `//api/net` (D271) goes out and **10.0.2.15 comes back from 10.0.2.2**, with the subnet mask and router options read out of the offer. **A method, not a field, and the first attempt is why.** Adding `buffer` to `NetTransmitRequest` broke every *inline* transmit: a `transfer handle` field names an index into the message's handle vector, so a request transferring nothing has no legal value to put there, and the decoder answered `HandleIndexOutOfRange` on index 0 of a zero-handle message — `a_frame_naming_a_buffer_that_did_not_arrive_is_refused` doing exactly its job against the ARP leg. The two forms also differ in **ownership**: inline is `CALLER_RETAINS`, out-of-line is `TRANSFERRED`, and one ordinal whose ownership depends on whether a field was populated is two contracts wearing one number. Splitting them left the inline wire format byte-identical, so `TRANSMIT_GOLDEN_PREFIX` is unchanged. **`TRANSFERRED` is forced, not chosen**: `SHARED_FOR_CALL` is what a transmit wants — lend the buffer, keep it — and `TransferMode::SHARE` is still decoded and refused (D131), so a caller pays an object per frame. That is the per-packet cost `docs/roadmap/03` says to measure before the shape sets, and it is now measurable. **Two defects found by running it, neither predicted.** The driver built its decoder with `Reader::in_message(&bytes, 0)` — a hardcoded handle count — so every out-of-line request decoded as `HandleIndexOutOfRange`; it is told what actually arrived now. And the client mapped each frame it inspected and released none, which exhausted the machine's memory objects and surfaced as the **driver's** next `MemoryCreate` failing, four frames away from the cause. **Two inversions, both run.** One flipped bit in the UDP checksum and the server never answers — the exchange stalls and the check fails, which is the statement that nothing in this tree judges those checksums. Forcing the same frame through the inline path reproduces D271's refusal exactly (`0x79`, status `u32::MAX`). | The socket surface as an ISL protocol, and the per-packet budget: an object created, transferred and freed per frame is the number `docs/architecture/03` wants taken while the shape is still cheap to change — a pool, or `SHARED_FOR_CALL` once an object table can refcount. |
| D271 | **The layers above the link exist, and building them found the ceiling that stops them reaching the wire (composition roadmap Phase 3, first landing).** `docs/roadmap/03` listed TCP and UDP as absent, noting that *"the one place a protocol above the link layer is parsed at all is `kernel/virtio/src/arp.rs`, which exists to prove a NIC round trip"*. `//api/net` is the first thing here that speaks a protocol above the link because something needs it carried: Ethernet II, IPv4 with its header checksum, UDP with the pseudo-header checksum that binds a datagram to the addresses it was sent between, and enough DHCP to build a DISCOVER and read an OFFER. `no_std`, `forbid(unsafe_code)`, no allocator, every length range-checked before it indexes — the `api/ext2` model, and on the fuzz gate's hand-written-parser list for the same reason, since a frame is the one input here nobody in this machine wrote. **The oracles are external where one exists**: RFC 1071's own worked example and a published IPv4 header with its published checksum, because a test that builds with this crate and parses with this crate proves only that the two halves are inverses and would pass just as happily if both had the byte order backwards. 16 host tests, and a fuzz target whose seed is a full four-layer frame — `run_blobs` refuses a run whose seed does not parse, so the harness passing is itself the statement that the seed is valid. **What it cannot do is reach the wire, and that is the finding.** The plan's first milestone was a DHCP round trip against QEMU's own server, and it does not fit: `network_driver.isl`'s `NetTransmitRequest.frame` is `array<uint8, 64>`, and a DHCP DISCOVER frame is **290 bytes** — the BOOTP fixed area alone is 240. Widening the field does not rescue it either, because `MAX_INLINE_BYTES` caps the whole message at **256 bytes** to hold budget B3, so no inline transmit can ever carry an IP datagram with a payload worth sending: 42 bytes of headers leave **22 bytes**. The receive direction already solved this — `NetFrameEvent` carries `buffer: transfer handle<Object, {READ, MAP}>` and the driver hands a frame over as a memory object it made and gave away (D131, D150) — and the transmit direction never did, because ARP at 42 bytes fit and nothing else had ever been sent. **Measured rather than predicted**: the leg was written, run, and failed with the client's own `0x79` code and status `u32::MAX`, which is `transmit`'s refusal of a frame larger than its buffer. `docs/roadmap/03` says of this phase that it is *"the item most likely to be underestimated by a multiple"*; the multiple is 4.5x on the first datagram anybody tried to send. | **CLOSED (D272):** out-of-line transmit landed as a *method* rather than a field — `TransmitBuffer` (ordinal 6) taking a `NetTransmitBufferRequest`, because a `transfer handle` field on the inline request has no legal value when nothing is transferred. The DHCP round trip this row could not finish now runs. What remains of this line is the socket surface and the per-packet budget `docs/roadmap/03` asks for. |
| D270 | **The coverage check compares source files, which is what makes it exact (closes D269's exit criterion).** D269 compared *packages*: every bare-metal package had to be named by some architecture. That is sound against a new port and blind against a second crate inside a package already named. Both sides are now `labels(srcs, set(…))` — the files a bare-metal target compiles, against the files the named targets compile — and the tree has **142 bare-metal source files, all linted**. **The first inversion I wrote did not discriminate, and running it is what showed that.** It added a second crate under `kernel/karch-x86_64/src/`, and the file-level check said "all linted" — correctly, because the existing target globs `src/**/*.rs` and was already compiling it. A coverage check can only be tested with something that genuinely escapes coverage; moved to `second/lib.rs`, outside that glob, the package-level check still reports "13 packages, all linted" and the file-level one names the file. **Four inversions, all measured to fire:** that one, a sixth port's crate, a query that cannot run, and — found while testing — an architecture deleted from `arch-lint-baseline.txt`, which now fails naming that port's files where before it silently stopped linting them. **What the check made visible and this row does not fix.** The query stops at `//kernel/...`. Outside it are **48 bare-metal targets and 33 source files — every ring-3 program's `src/main.rs` — that no target on any platform lints**, which is D183's hole one directory over and larger than the one just closed. Measured rather than estimated: including them reports **28 clippy findings and 10 unformatted files** under `--config=lint-aarch64`, and 6 and 3 under each of `riscv64` and `x86_64`. | Widening the query to `//...` is one word; what it needs first is a decision this script cannot make — which architecture's baseline owns a ring-3 program that is built for several — plus the 28 findings and 10 files above. |
| D269 | **A crate no gate had ever linted, found by closing D268's exit criterion.** That row left the hole it could see: `targets_for` in `tools/ci/arch-lint.sh` is a hand-written list and the loop walks `arch-lint-baseline.txt`, so a package neither names is not *failed* — it is never linted, which is the same silence D183 and D268 were each about. **Bazel knows the real answer.** A target constrained to `@platforms//os:none` is exactly one a host `bazel build //... --config=lint` skips, so `attr(target_compatible_with, "os:none", //kernel/...)` *is* the set this script has to cover, and the check compares it against what `targets_for` names. **It found one on the first run:** `kernel/width-conformance` — the 32-bit word-size gate, a real crate with its own `src/lib.rs`, never linted by anything. It is clean (0 findings, so `riscv32` stays at 9), which is luck rather than discipline: nothing had been checking, and the crate's own BUILD comment is about how a host build *silently skips* it. **Compared per package, not per target**, because a package can hold two binaries over one `srcs`: `kernel-aarch64_bin` and `kernel-aarch64_image_bin` are the ELF and the flat image of the same sources, and linting either lints the files. **A query that cannot run fails rather than skipping** — a check that goes quiet when its input is missing reads exactly like a check that passed, which is the failure mode of the two rows above and would have been this one's too. `LC_ALL=C` is pinned for the comparison: the locale's collation and `comm`'s disagree on `karch-arm-common` against `karch-arm32`, and an unsorted-input warning means the answer is unreliable rather than merely untidy. **Three inversions, all measured to fire:** a bare-metal package no architecture names, a sixth port's crate appearing with nobody adding it (the scenario D268 predicted, tested by creating one), and a query that cannot run. | **Closed by D270.** The comparison is per package, so a *second* crate in an already-covered package, with sources of its own, would still slip. Comparing source files rather than packages is what would close that, and it wants the srcs of every target rather than one query. |
| D268 | **The formatter ran on every port, failed, and said so into a pipe nobody read.** D183 found that the ports had never been linted *behind a comment saying they were*; this is the same shape one layer in. `tools/ci/arch-lint.sh`'s header said "the clippy and rustfmt aspects do not cross" the platform transition, and that is half right: `--config=lint-<arch>` sets both aspects and both **do** run. What did not cross was the *reading*. The script piped the build's output through `grep -E '^error: '` — the shape of a clippy finding — while rustfmt says `ERROR: … Rustfmt … failed` and `Diff in <file>`, neither of which matches, and the assignment took grep's status rather than the build's. So the check ran, exited 1, and was discarded. **Measured, both ways.** Against a tree with one deliberately unformatted line in `kernel-riscv32`, the old script **exits 0** and prints nothing; the new one exits 1 and names the file. The extent of the real drift was one file — `kernel-aarch64/src/el0.rs` — and it is small only because the D265–D267 work reformatted most of these files as a side effect of touching them; nothing had been holding them. **The two halves fail differently, so they are counted differently.** Clippy's findings stay held to `arch-lint-baseline.txt`, which may only fall. Rustfmt is not a count — a file either matches the formatter or it does not — so it is a hard failure with the diff printed. **And it has two failure modes, not one:** `Diff in` is a file the formatter would rewrite, while a failed Rustfmt action with *no* diff is a file it could not parse. Matching only the first would pass the second silently, which is the mistake this row exists to stop repeating; both are inversion-tested and both fire. **The contrast that locates the defect:** the host half was always sound. `bazel build //kernel/kcore --config=lint` exits 1 on an unformatted host crate and `presubmit.sh` runs under `set -e`, so nothing there was ever unchecked. One pipe, in one script, covering five ports. `docs/lifecycle/04` already says to verify with exit codes rather than by grepping for `error`; this is what the tree cost for not doing it in its own gate. | **Closed by D269.** The arch list in `targets_for` is hand-maintained and the loop walks `arch-lint-baseline.txt`, so a **sixth port would be invisible to this gate entirely** rather than failing it — the same hole `//:all_srcs` has, and it wants the same kind of check: that every `kernel/karch-*` and `kernel/kernel-*` package is named by some architecture. |
| D267 | **The entry functions became the phases they already ran in — the exit criterion D196, D265 and D266 each left open.** `kernel_main`/`_start` was the composition root's remaining problem after the file splits: RISC-V 64 **723 -> 24** lines with 12 phases, x86-64 **795 -> 327** with 6, AArch64 **3,127 -> 488** with 8. `kernel_main` on RISC-V 64 now reads as the boot's table of contents — console, memory map, translation, traps, store, then six checks and the alive marker — and what a phase needs and produces is in its signature instead of in eight hundred lines of shared scope. **What the splitting found was not length.** Two of the three were *staircases*: RISC-V 64's ring-3 ladder ran each check inside the previous one's `Ok` arm, fifteen levels deep by the last, and AArch64's device block was one 1,764-line brace at seventeen. The nesting was load-bearing for nothing — **every failure arm printed and ended the run**, which is one line of `or_die`, so what the indentation encoded was a control flow the code did not have. Flattened, the only `match` left in the ladder is what a *machine* does not have, which is a different question and reads like one; deepest indentation on RISC-V 64 goes 20 -> 9 levels. **Measured, including where it went the other way.** RISC-V 64's image is **2,592 bytes smaller** and AArch64's **3,272 smaller** — collapsing a staircase deletes code — while x86-64's grew **440 bytes**, because there was no staircase there to collapse and six calls is what was added. RISC-V 64's whole serial log is identical across all three of its boots but for four lines, and those four report the image's own size, which moved because the image did. x86-64's 36 claims and 23 `OK` verdicts are the same set. All 21 AArch64 boot checks pass, each asserting its own markers. The lint ratchet is unchanged on all five ports — but only after the split's own findings were **fixed rather than baselined**: a redundant field name on each of the three, an elidable lifetime, and a parameter that turned out to be dead because the count it carried was already reported by the phase that produced it. **A `// SAFETY:` comment was orphaned the way a doc comment is.** The conformance phase's boundary fell between the comment and the `unsafe` it justifies, and the unsafe-inventory gate caught it — the same class as D265's seven orphaned docs, from the same cause, and the gate saw this one because a missing `SAFETY` is what it looks for. **Not done, and on purpose:** RISC-V 32 and ARM 32 keep theirs. At 319 and 309 lines and five levels of indentation they are already readable, and splitting them would be churn rather than a phase boundary anyone needed. **Recorded, not fixed:** `claim smp.secondary-preempted` was withheld on one x86-64 run out of seven, in the run that shared the host with a build. The check withholds rather than fails when the handover does not happen, which is by design, and its call site is byte-identical and outside every moved span — but a timing-sensitive claim that a boot script asserts is a flake waiting for a slow CI worker. | The x86-64 `_start` that remains is 327 lines of linear bring-up, each step feeding the next; and `smp.secondary-preempted` given a bound that does not depend on host load. |
| D266 | **The RISC-V 64 port's composition root stopped being the whole port (D196/D265, the third and last port that needed it).** `kernel/kernel-riscv64/src/main.rs` was 5,971 lines and is 1,111 now, with 13 modules beside it: what the firmware says the machine is (`discovery`, `timer`), getting to U-mode and giving each program a space of its own (`umode`, `space`, `process`), what a program can be given (`exec`, `device`, `grant`, `irq`), and the driver framework (`blk`, `restart`, `relay`, `rebind`). Same idiom as the other two: `use crate::*` in each module, re-exported from the root, so the namespace stays the flat one it was and the modules buy a name and a header per area rather than a boundary. The unsafe inventory's single entry for this port was about 150 words in one sentence — and it still named the device-tree slices, the U-mode blobs, the DMA read and the PLIC masking after all four had moved. It is 13 entries now. **The inversion that passed, and what it cost to notice.** D265's proof was that the boot's claims and `OK` verdicts are the same set before and after; run here, dropping `timer_check` from `kernel_main` changed **neither** — and `smoke_boot_riscv64` passed too. This port's checks report in prose (`timer: 3 ticks at 100 Hz, Sstc delivering`) rather than through `kcore::verdict`, so only 11 of them are in that summary at all. The discriminator here is the **whole serial log**, timestamps stripped and the envelope normalised, which this port can afford because its boot is deterministic — no KASLR, no benchmark numbers, unlike x86-64's. Measured: 0 differing lines for the split across all three boots (`smoke`, `pcie`, `virtio_blk`), 2 for the inversion. **A check that is strong on one port is not automatically strong on the next, and the only way to know is to run the inversion on the port you are on.** **What changed in the output:** 6 lines per boot, across 2 modules — `umode` and `space`, the only checks that print from their own function rather than from `kernel_main`. **The image grew 3,544 bytes** (4,997,680 -> 5,001,224). The lint ratchet is unchanged at 94: no findings added, and unlike x86-64 this port had no `empty_line_after_doc_comments` to collect. One stale doc did go — a sentence about "the manager's and the probe's ELFs" glued to `system_store`, describing accessors that are `components::*` now. **What is NOT proven:** that `kernel_main` is any better off. It is 723 of the 1,111 remaining lines. With this the three ports that had accumulated a composition root are all split, and the same sentence is now true of every port in the tree. | `kernel_main` broken into the phases it already runs in, on all three ports; and the module globs replaced by named imports. |
| D265 | **The x86-64 port's composition root stopped being the whole port (D196, applied to the port that never got it).** `kernel/kernel/src/main.rs` was 11,035 lines — the Limine handoff, the machine's statics, `_start`, the trap and panic path, and, as the bulk of it, every check this machine runs, one function per area in a single file nothing could navigate. It is 1,637 lines now with 22 modules beside it, grouped the way `kernel-aarch64` groups its own: the primitives the boot assumes (`kstack`, `selfcheck`, `smp`), the substrate ring 3 runs on (`ipc`, `loader`, `sched`, `user`), the services and the framework that binds them (`channel`, `devmgr`, `fs`, `host`, `pci_bus`, `restart`), the faults the kernel answers rather than kills (`dpage`, `pagein`, `pressure`), what a program waits on (`jobs`, `ports`, `waitaddr`), and what the run says about itself (`correlation`, `observability`, `perf`). **The split is organisational and the code says so** — every module opens with `use crate::*` and the root re-exports each, so the namespace is the flat one it was when this was one file; what the modules buy is a name and a header per area, not a boundary. **The one real win beyond navigability is again the unsafe inventory.** One entry of about fifty words covered the entire port, naming boot entry and the PCI configuration ports in the same breath — and it still named the port pair, which no longer had anything to do with `main.rs`. That one entry is 21 now — `main.rs` and the twenty modules that carry `unsafe` — each naming what its own file does; the check modules that share one harness (the crate's static tables, the `global_asm!` blob and its linker symbols, the run's syscall handler, activating the space just built) share one wording, because the operations genuinely are the same. **The verdict renderer was tried as a module and is not one.** `report` moved out with everything else, and the boot then failed the **150-byte line budget every port's boot script enforces**: `kprintln!` takes its module from `module_path!()`, so `[kernel]` became `[kernel::verdicts]` on all 27 verdict lines, one went to 152 bytes and six sat within six bytes of the gate. Shortening the message would have cleared the gate and left the trap. `report` is not an area — it is the boot's own narration, and the message it renders already names the check — so it stays beside `_start` with the trap and panic path, the other output every area reaches through. Measured, not predicted: every other module's longest line has at least 28 bytes of headroom. **What changed in the output:** 39 lines across 7 modules, all of them the envelope keeping the submodule path — `[kernel::perf]`, `[kernel::user]`, `[kernel::dpage]` and four more. Everything else in the serial log that moved was measured to move between two runs of the same kernel (KASLR base, timer calibration, usable frame count, tick and switch counts, the benchmark numbers). **The image grew 8,128 bytes** (4,405,848 -> 4,413,976): `.rodata` is +1,920, which is the longer module-path strings showing up in the image; `.text` moved 16 bytes and `.data`/`.bss` not at all. The lint ratchet is unchanged at 160 — this split added no findings, where D196's added two. **Seven orphaned doc comments, found rather than made.** An item's doc stays behind when the item moves, glued to whatever follows; the tell is a `///` run ending at a blank line. There are seven, all describing hand-picked kernel-stack constants D53 deleted, and all seven are in the file as it stood before this change — the split only made them visible by putting them in files small enough to read. Six documented nothing and are gone; the seventh belongs to `job_kstack`, the function that replaced what it described, and is reattached with the claim D53 made false (a slot picked by hand) corrected. **Clippy had been naming this defect all along.** All seven were `empty_line_after_doc_comments` findings inside the port's baseline count, which is why `x86_64` falls 160 -> 153 — **the first fall in that file's history that came from fixing something rather than from deleting or moving code.** The lint was reporting a real documentation bug and the number absorbed it, which is what a budget does to a finding nobody reads. **The inversion, and what it says about the gate.** The proof a span-cut refactor needs is that no check quietly stopped running, so the check is that the boot's 36 claims and 23 `OK` verdicts are the same set before and after — they are. Inverted by dropping one call from `_start`: `//tools/qemu:smoke_boot_test` **passed**, because it does not assert that check, and the claim set was unchanged, because that check emits no claim. Only the verdict set saw it. The comparison discriminates and the boot gate alone does not, which is worth knowing before the next port is split. **What is NOT proven:** that `_start` is any better off. It is 795 of the 1,637 remaining lines — one function that runs every check in order — and splitting a function is a different job from splitting a file. | `_start` broken into the phases it already runs in; and the module globs replaced by named imports, which is what would turn this from a file split into a boundary. |
| D264 | **Composition roadmap Phase 1 is complete, and one of its bullets was mis-scoped.** All five machines run one root task from one source on one `kcore::loader`: it creates its own channels, walks a real ELF, grants capabilities its children hold, supervises a service to a clean start and gives up on one that never comes up. The kernel seeds a job — and, on the machines that have them, a bus and a device — and nothing else. **The phase's own inversion was run rather than assumed.** Its wording is that the inversion "is available for free, because a check that still passes with the root task removed is measuring the demos it was supposed to replace"; removing the root task from all five images fails all five boots, four on the missing `roottask.channel-created` claim and x86-64 on the verdict itself. **The correction.** The phase's second bullet names `device_manager_demo`, `driver_host_demo` and `component_manager_demo` as "the sequence, written three times in kernel code". That is true of the third, which went in D250, and of the composition half of the first. It is **not** true of the rest, and reading the survivors before deleting them is what showed it: the two on x86-64 are that port's *only* ring-3 device I/O and ring-3 interrupt delivery — a capability-gated port-I/O path with its refusal, a real IRQ delivered to a ring-3 driver, and a client whose `ChannelCall` that driver serves by driving hardware. AArch64's `bring_up_device_host` and `relay_pair` are not demos at all but **spawn helpers**, shared by four checks claiming a resident driver host over real virtio, a filesystem, a data-path budget and firmware loading. None of those claims is reachable through the composed path, so retiring them would delete coverage rather than stop maintaining two systems — the opposite of what the fourth bullet asks for. They go when the composed path reaches what they assert. **What the phase actually retired**: `component_manager_demo`, `cm_budget_selftest` and `cm_reclaim_stress` (D250), `driver_bind_check` (D256) and `compiled_program_check` (D262) — five, each replaced by a composed path making the same claim. **What it built**, in order: `ProcessGrant` and a compiled root task (D249, D251), a second and third and fourth and fifth port for the loader seam (D252, D257, D262, D263), an asynchronous `ProcessStart` with `ProcessWait` (D250), `ChannelCreate`, `PortCreate` and `PortBind` from ring 3 (D253, D254), `DeviceIrqBind` (D255), the driver framework composed in user space over a real PCI function (D256), a 32-bit ELF class and syscall ABI (D258, D259, D260), and the startup message (D261). | Phase 2 — a program comes from storage: the manager and the driver are in each image twice today, once in the kernel's component list and once inside the root task, and Phase 2 removes both copies by reading them from the verified store. |
| D263 | **Every port runs the same root task (composition roadmap Phase 1).** `arm32` gains a syscall sequence in `//userspace/uabi`, an executive substrate and a `LoaderSupport` impl, and with it the matrix closes: five machines, one program, one `kcore::loader`. They agree about almost nothing else — two are 32 bits wide and load a different ELF class, the RISC-V ports copy the kernel's root entries into every process and must be careful when, one identity-maps its kernel half and another reaches its through a `TTBR1` split. None of that reached `kcore`. **The finding, and it is the one this whole exercise was for.** `ContextOps::prepare_resume` on this port was the trait's **default no-op**, under a comment reading "there is no unprivileged level to transition from and every thread runs in the one kernel space" — true when written, and false since ring 3 (D109) and per-process `TTBR0` (D110) arrived. Nothing noticed for two milestones, because every check that ran user code activated its space **by hand** before entering User mode. A *scheduled* thread does not get that: the first one switched to took a prefetch abort on its own entry point, a page that was mapped, readable and executable in a space the CPU was not walking. This is D81's lesson in a second place — per-thread hardware state that the switch path does not install is state that silently belongs to whoever ran last — and the inversion is exact: restoring the no-op reproduces `fault 0x80000003 at 0x10000000`. **What this port makes simpler**, recorded because it is the only place a 32-bit machine here got an easier answer: the `TTBCR` split gives the kernel its own translation-base register, so a process's tables carry no copy of the kernel's and the 4 MiB root-entry hazard that shaped RISC-V 32's kernel stacks (D260, D262) has no counterpart. There is nothing to copy and nothing to guard. **Two smaller findings.** `.ARM.exidx` has a section *type* of its own and cannot be swept into `.text` — a linker script that tried failed the link outright — so the user script discards it as the kernel's own does; no program here unwinds. And `DIRECT_MAP_BASE + 0x4000_0000` is inside the direct map's range rather than past it, so the first kernel-stack window answered `AlreadyMapped`. **Size, per D231**: the ARM 32 image grows 405,720 -> 1,836,776 bytes, which is a kernel that carries a ring-3 program where it carried none. **What this does not do.** Neither 32-bit port carries the driver framework: `device-manager` and `blk-probe` are not built for those targets and `uabi::layout` names no 32-bit machine, so both root tasks compose what they can and claim nothing about a bus. | Phase 1's remaining scope is now the four kernel-side compositions the roadmap's fourth bullet retires — `device_manager_demo` and `driver_host_demo` on x86-64, `bring_up_device_host` and `relay_pair` on AArch64 — each of which still makes a claim no root task's run does. |
| D262 | **The root task runs on a 32-bit machine, and `kcore::loader` gets the caller that is not 64-bit (composition roadmap Phase 1).** `kernel-riscv32` gains a `LoaderSupport` impl and a root-task check; the program is the same source the other three compile, and it makes the same 45 launches. **Four is the number, and the fourth is the one that matters.** Every earlier caller was 64 bits wide, which is the single thing three callers could all have been wrong about together. This machine has half-width pointers, a different ELF class, page-table root entries spanning 4 MiB rather than a gibibyte, and a kernel half that **is** the identity map. None of that reached `kcore`: the seam is still the same six methods, and what the port writes is one impl, four loader arms and a check. **The root task's own parser learned the 32-bit class**, the same way `kcore::elf` did (D258) and for the same reason — a layout table rather than a second parser, because every check it makes (magic, type, machine, segment bounds, W^X) is the same check on both, and ELF32 reorders the program header rather than merely narrowing it. That is one of the two inversions: parsing child images at the ELF64 offsets makes the root task refuse its own probes with `exit=103`. **All five kernel stacks share one 4 MiB region, and they have to.** A process root copies the kernel half by value, so a mapping is visible to it only under a root entry that already existed (D260); a child's kernel stack is mapped when the child starts, which is *after* its root was copied. One region, its entry created by a guard page before anything else, is what makes every window reachable from the process running on it — moving the children's windows out of it faults the boot with no report. A gibibyte-grained port never had to think about this. **`has_framework` replaced a `target_arch`.** The root task's driver-framework images were unconditional after D256; this image carries no manager and no driver, and keying that on the architecture would have said it was a property of the machine. It is composition — the same source everywhere, a smaller image where it is left out — so it is keyed like the kernels' own program lists. **A demo dies**: `compiled_program_check`, D260's standalone ELF32-loads-and-runs check, is gone. The root task loads and runs compiled ELF32 programs and forty-five of them, so keeping the smaller check would be maintaining two systems — which is the discipline the roadmap's fourth bullet asks for. **Size, per D231**: the RISC-V 32 kernel grows 3,076,364 -> 3,217,872 bytes, the root task and its two probes replacing one probe. **What this does not do.** No driver framework here: `device-manager` and `blk-probe` are not built for this target, and `uabi::layout` still names no 32-bit machine, which is what they would need. And `arm32` remains the one port with no compiled ring-3 program at all. | `arm32`: the syscall sequence in `//userspace/uabi`, an executive substrate, and the same `LoaderSupport` impl — after which every port in the matrix runs the same root task. |
| D261 | **A child gets a startup message, and the 64-bit-only convention it replaces is gone (composition roadmap Phase 1).** `docs/api/01` has listed "install the initial handle set and startup message" since it was written; the handle half became `ProcessGrant` in D249 and the message half did not exist. A child learned where its capabilities landed through `ProcessStart`'s `arg` — one word — so a parent with two things to say packed them into its halves. That is a convention rather than a mechanism, and a **64-bit** one: on a 32-bit machine the argument register is 32 bits and the second handle has nowhere to go, which is what stands between `kernel-riscv32` and a root task (D260). **`ProcessStartArgs` v2** adds `message_ptr`/`message_len`/`message_va`: the kernel copies bytes out of the parent into a page it maps in the child at an address the parent names, and the child is told where by that address being its `arg`. A start carrying no message maps nothing, so every program that wants a plain scalar is untouched. **The kernel does not read the bytes.** Which schema a message carries is an agreement between a parent and the child it started — written in ISL like any other protocol rather than hand-packed — and `StartupHandles` is the first, with **named slots and no count**: a count and an array would be a hand-decoded vector, and the count is the only guard against a misparse in one (D101). **One page, refused rather than truncated**, and an unaligned destination refused too: a child that received part of its startup message would read a handle number out of whatever followed the cut. **The finding.** The first delivery used `write_user`, which validates against a process's mappings and then copies through the **active** space — and the active space here is the *parent's*, because the child has never run. It faulted on the child's address in the kernel. `copy_in` translates through the child's own tables and writes the frame it finds, which is how `address_space_map` populates a child's segments and works for the same reason. **Five inversions fire**: mapping the message page executable, mapping a page when there is no message, clamping an oversize message instead of refusing it, accepting a v1 struct (whose `arg` sits where v2's `message_ptr` begins), and — at boot — putting the wrong handle in the message, which makes `grant-probe`'s signal fail and the root task report `exit=110`. **What the host tests do not prove, stated rather than implied**: the bytes. `MockAddressSpace::write_bytes_to_frame` is a no-op because physical frames are not real memory in a host test, so the host tests assert the page's *shape* — present, user, writable, not executable. The boot proves the content and proves it harder than a memcpy would: `grant-probe` decodes the message and sends on the endpoint it names, so a message that arrived wrong makes `roottask.child-spoke` disappear. | The root task on `kernel-riscv32`: a `LoaderSupport` impl, and the root task's own ELF parser reading ELF32 — the two things left between a 32-bit machine and the composition the other three run. |
| D260 | **A compiled ring-3 program runs on a 32-bit machine (composition roadmap Phase 1).** `kernel-riscv32` reached ring 3 through a bespoke handler with two toy syscalls and a hand-assembled blob — enough to show U-mode can be entered and contained, and not a program: it could not be given an argument, could not be linked, and could not grow. It has the same executive substrate the three 64-bit ports use now — an `Executive`, a `ProcessTable`, and a trap hook into `kcore::dispatch` — and **none of that substrate is new code**, because `//kernel/width-conformance` has been compiling it at 32 bits on every build since long before there was anything here to run. `//userspace/restart-probe` loads as a real ELF32 (D258, D259), starts as a `Process` with a `Thread`, and exits with the argument it was given: a run that answers `0x5a` cannot have skipped the load, the entry, the argument register, the `ecall` or the exit. **The kernel's half of D259's width agreement.** uabi refuses an argument too wide for a register; this refuses a *result* too wide to come back in one, answering `ResultTooLarge`. Neither truncates, because a handle or an address that lost its high half is a different handle or address. **Three findings, and the first two are the 32-bit machine being a different machine.** `DIRECT_MAP_BASE` is **zero** here — the kernel is identity-mapped because RAM starts at the 2 GiB boundary (D106) — so `DIRECT_MAP_BASE + offset`, which is how every other port names a kernel stack, is a *user* address, and the first attempt put the kernel stack where the program's own text links. Second, a process root copies the kernel half **by value**, sharing the kernel's second-level tables: a mapping made afterwards inside an existing root entry is seen, one needing a **new** root entry is not — and Sv32 entries span 4 MiB against Sv39's gibibyte, so an arbitrary window almost always needs a new one. The thread faults on its own kernel stack in the trap vector's first store, with no report and no dying print. A **guard page** below the stack, mapped before the process root is taken, is what puts the entry in the kernel root first — and is a guard page on its own merits. **Third, and it is a real bug this found rather than a porting detail**: `karch::atomic`'s 32-bit `AtomicU64::swap` passed the caller's `Ordering` to *both* halves of the split, so `swap(.., Acquire)` handed `Acquire` to a store and the standard library's answer to that is a panic. It fired the first time this port ran a thread that drained a wakeup. The ordering is split per half now, the way `fetch_*` does it on a machine that has the instruction. Building those types at 32 bits said nothing about which ordering reached which half; only running them did. **Inversions**: removing the guard page reproduces the store fault with no report; telling the loader the image is 64-bit refuses it; and restoring the store's original ordering fails both new `karch` tests. Recorded honestly — those two tests assert that no ordering *panics* and that the value round-trips, **not** that the memory ordering is honoured: replacing the split with `Relaxed` on both halves passes them, measured rather than assumed. **Size, per D231**: the RISC-V 32 kernel grows 1,256,400 -> 3,076,328 bytes, which is the substrate and the embedded program together. **What this does not do.** The program's only syscall is `ProcessExit`, which is port-local on every port — so the dispatcher is wired and its first covered call from 32-bit ring 3 comes with the root task. `arm32` is untouched. And two things a 32-bit root task will need do not exist: `uabi::layout` names no 32-bit machine, and `grant-probe` packs two handles into one startup word, which is a 64-bit-only convention that exists because there is still no startup *message*. | A `LoaderSupport` impl for `kernel-riscv32` and the root task running on it — which needs the root task's own ELF parser to read ELF32, and the two-handles-in-one-word convention replaced by the startup message `docs/api/01` has always specified. |
| D259 | **The syscall ABI has a width, and a 32-bit machine now builds ring-3 programs (composition roadmap Phase 1).** `build/rules/arch.bzl` marked both 32-bit ports `user_flags = None`, whose stated meaning was that `//userspace/uabi` had no syscall sequence for them. Writing one is not a port: every uabi entry point takes `u64` because the ABI is written in 64-bit words, and an argument register here is half that — a `u64` would be passed in a **register pair**, shifting every argument index the kernel reads, which is the shape of the bug that already cost this port a boot handoff (D107). **Refused, not truncated, and not a 181-site sweep either.** The obvious fix — retyping every uabi entry point to `usize` — would have touched 181 call sites across 23 programs whose behaviour only a boot check can verify. Instead the surface stays `u64` and the narrowing happens **inside uabi**, in one place: `syscall_arg` narrows each argument and answers `None` for one that does not fit, and the call returns the kernel-domain `InvalidMapping` before it traps. A pointer with its high half silently removed is a pointer into somebody else's memory, and this tree's rule is that code which degrades says so — here saying so means not degrading at all. The **result** widens the other way and is lossless: a failure is `-((domain << 16) \| code)` over six domains and small codes, and a success on a 32-bit machine is a handle, a count or an address, all of which that register held already. `docs/api/01` says this now. **One `usize` in each program, and it is not cosmetic**: `_start(arg: u64)` reads its startup argument out of a register pair the kernel wrote one of, so entry points take `usize`. **A real producer for D258's `Machine::RiscV32`.** `//userspace/restart-probe` builds for `riscv32` — the first compiled ring-3 program for a 32-bit machine in this tree — and `//tools/checks:user_elf32_test` feeds the artifact a real linker produced to the real `kcore::elf` parser. That gate is the join between two packages that never reference each other, which is why it is a gate rather than a unit test: it asserts the entry is where `user-riscv32.ld` links, that the text segment is read+execute and not writable, and that the same bytes are **refused** for the 64-bit target — which only the class byte can do, since RISC-V gives both widths one machine number. **The inversion fires against the real artifact**: reading ELF32 program headers at the ELF64 offsets refuses the toolchain's own output with `BadSegment`. **What this does not do.** Nothing *runs* the program yet: `kernel-riscv32` reaches ring 3 through a bespoke two-syscall trap handler with no Executive, no process table and no `kcore::dispatch` — the substrate D100-D102 built for RISC-V 64 does not exist here. `arm32` keeps `user_flags = None`, because the same decision needs its own kernel side to be worth making. And `uabi::layout` is still per-port `cfg`s that name no 32-bit machine, so a program using it does not compile for one. | The U-mode substrate on `kernel-riscv32` — an Executive, a process table and a `kcore::dispatch` hook — which is what a compiled program needs before a `LoaderSupport` impl has anything to run on. |
| D258 | **The kernel's ELF loader accepts 32-bit images, which is the first thing the two 32-bit ports need to run a compiled program (composition roadmap Phase 1).** `kcore::elf` was ELF64-only; `riscv32` and `arm32` reach ring 3 with hand-assembled blobs, and every compiled ring-3 program in this tree is 64-bit. **A layout table, not a second parser.** The classes differ in the width of their address-sized fields and therefore in where the later ones start, but every check — magic, type, machine, segment bounds — is the same check on both; two parsers would mean two places for a bounds check to be missing from, and the one that gets less use is the one it would be missing from. **The program headers are reordered, not merely narrowed**: ELF32 puts `p_flags` *last*, after the sizes, where ELF64 puts it second. A parser assuming narrowing alone reads a segment's flags out of its file offset — zero, which is no permissions at all — and that is the inversion this ships with. **RISC-V forced `Machine` apart.** The ELF specification gives RISC-V one `e_machine` for both widths and distinguishes them by the class byte, so a variant carrying the machine number alone could not say which width a caller will run. `Machine::RiscV32` is therefore the first variant whose discriminant is **not** a machine number (`0x01f3`, unassigned), and `Machine::e_machine()` is what the parser compares against — no existing value moved. The class is checked against **the caller's target** rather than against a constant, because both are real classes and which one is right is a fact about who is loading. **Two inversions fire**: accepting either class refuses nothing in either direction, and reading ELF32 headers at the ELF64 offsets loses the segment's permissions. **What this does not do, and it is the honest part.** `Machine::RiscV32` has no producer yet. Nothing loads a 32-bit program because nothing builds one: `build/rules/arch.bzl` marks both 32-bit ports `user_flags = None`, whose stated meaning is that `//userspace/uabi` has no syscall sequence for them — and writing one is not mechanical. Every uabi entry point takes `u64` and answers `i64`; on a 32-bit machine a `u64` argument occupies a **register pair**, which would silently shift every argument index the kernel reads, and the result word `docs/api/01` specifies as one `i64` has only a 32-bit register to come back in. That is an ABI decision about width rather than a port, and it is the next gate rather than something to slip in behind this one. | The syscall ABI's width on a 32-bit machine — `uabi` in `usize`/`isize` rather than `u64`/`i64`, and what `docs/api/01`'s result word means where a register is 32 bits — and then a `LoaderSupport` impl for each of the two 32-bit ports. |
| D257 | **A third machine runs the same root task, and `kcore::loader` gets its third caller (composition roadmap Phase 1).** RISC-V 64 gains a `LoaderSupport` impl and a root-task check; the program is the same source, and the composition it produces is byte-identical to AArch64's — `launches=47 driver=0x3e80908000a0100`. **Three is the number that matters.** A seam with two callers has been generalized once, which is also how many times a wrong abstraction survives; this port builds its user space with `new_user` like x86-64 and unlike AArch64's `build_low_space`, takes its traps through `scause`/`sepc` rather than an exception vector, and routes interrupts through a PLIC. None of that reached `kcore` — the seam is still the same six methods, and what the port writes is one impl, four loader arms, and a check. **Two findings, and both were silent.** The kernel-stack windows were hand-picked at `0xffff_ffd0_...` — a perfectly valid Sv39 address that maps nothing, because on this port the kernel high half *is* the direct map. Worse than wrong: a process root here copies the kernel half **by value** at creation (D99), so it copies *root entries*, and a kernel mapping made afterwards is visible only if it lands under an entry that already existed (D100). Sv39 entries span 1 GiB, so even `DIRECT_MAP_BASE + 0xc000_0000` is a different entry from the `0xb?00_0000` family every other check uses — and a thread on its own root faults on its own kernel stack, in the **first store in `trap_vector`**, before any handler runs. No syscall trace, no dying print, and a fault address that is neither the stack nor anything the check names. The windows moved into the populated gibibyte. Second, `ProcessExit` did not call `kcore::loader::notify_exit`, so a child exited, its thread ended, and the parent stayed parked on a wait nothing would complete: the run ended with the root task still `Created`, no report, and a symptom naming neither the child nor the wait. **Two inversions fire**: dropping `notify_exit` reproduces exactly that state, and seeding the bus without `DERIVE` drops the launch count from 47 to 45 and leaves the report slot holding the root task's own pointer rather than a driver's. **Size, per D231**: the RISC-V 64 kernel grows 4,579,392 -> 4,979,200 bytes, because the manager and the driver are now in the image twice — once in the kernel's component list and once inside the root task. Phase 2 removes both copies. **What this does not do.** It retires none of this port's checks: `relay_check` and `driver_rebind_check` make claims the root task's run does not — a data-path budget refused at bind time, and a driver dying and its replacement binding the same physical device — so they are not this composition written twice. | The four kernel-side compositions still standing — `device_manager_demo` and `driver_host_demo` on x86-64, `bring_up_device_host` and `relay_pair` on AArch64 — and the two 32-bit ports still without a `LoaderSupport` impl. |
| D256 | **The root task composes the driver framework on x86-64 too, and the kernel's version of that composition is deleted (composition roadmap Phase 1).** `driver_bind_check` was 234 lines of boot glue that enumerated PCI, created a channel, spawned a device manager and a driver, and reached into both their handle tables. It is gone. What stays in the kernel is the part **no capability could replace**: a PCI function says what it is in configuration space, which is not per-device, so a capability to it would be authority over every function behind the bridge at once — the kernel reads it, normalizes what it found into the resource graph, and seeds the root task a **bus** carrying `DERIVE` with the real mass-storage function beneath it. The root task derives the device from that bus and hands it on, exactly as it already did on AArch64 — and over a *real* function rather than that port's synthetic graph. **The strong claim survives the move.** `driver_bind_check`'s best assertion was that the driver read a word from beyond the first page of the window it was granted and agreed with what the kernel reads at the same physical address, which a grant of the wrong region cannot fake and a one-page grant faults on instead of answering. That check is now `loader_demo`'s, against the report the root task's driver produced. **Three findings.** `blk-probe` **always exits zero** and reports by value, so the root task's `framework` field — the driver's exit code — proves nothing; the report word had to be captured, and this port's `DebugWrite` reads a *buffer*, so a driver passing a value in the pointer register with length zero was silently discarded. A `ROOT_REPORTS` sink keyed by order records the argument register first, the way AArch64's `EL0_REPORTS` already did. Second, `exec_restart` builds a fresh Executive and the device graph lives inside it, so registrations made earlier in `loader_demo` were simply gone by the time the root task looked — the handle was in its table and the object behind it named nothing. Third, `framework_exit` asked `HandleQueryRights` on handle 1 *late*, by which point handle 1 is something the program made: the same class of bug D255 found for the device, now fixed for both by one `seeds()` probe taken before anything is created, which checks the **right** (`DERIVE` for a bus, `BIND` for a device) rather than the slot being filled. **The last `cfg` came out of the composition.** Which report a machine's check expects is a fact about the machine, so it travels in the root task's own startup word — which the kernel always passed and the program had always ignored. AArch64 asks for the relay report its synthetic device can answer; x86-64 asks for the full probe. `compose_driver_framework` is now one function serving both ports with no conditional compilation at all. **Two inversions fire**: seeding the bus without `DERIVE` drops the launch count from 47 to 45, and granting the driver a window one page off its device leaves everything else passing with `bound=false` and a zero far-word — the discriminating one, because it is the only failure the composition itself cannot see. **Size, per D231**: the x86-64 root task grows 73,176 -> 261,952 bytes and the ISO 4,913,152 -> 5,095,424, because the manager and the driver are now in the image twice — once in the kernel's component list and once inside the root task, which has nowhere to read a program from. Phase 2 removes both copies. **What this does not do.** `device_manager_demo` and `driver_host_demo` reach their device by **port I/O** on a COM2 that predates `MapDevice`, so they are not this composition written twice and retiring them is a separate question. `bring_up_device_host` and `relay_pair` on AArch64 still need that port's root task to hold a real virtio device rather than a synthetic one. | The four kernel-side compositions still standing — `device_manager_demo` and `driver_host_demo` on x86-64, `bring_up_device_host` and `relay_pair` on AArch64 — and the three ports still without a `LoaderSupport` impl. |
| D255 | **A ring-3 program routes a real device's interrupt to a port it made (composition roadmap Phase 1).** `DeviceIrqBind` (52) is the call `docs/api/01` has listed as "bind interrupt object" since it was written and nothing implemented. Until now every interrupt route in this tree was installed by kernel boot glue calling `Executive::device_route_irq` on a driver's behalf, which meant **the last thing a driver host needed from the kernel was not a capability but a favour**: a program could map its device (23), allocate its DMA (24) and re-arm its line (26), and still could not say where the interrupts were to go. A root task holding a device and a port could compose everything about a driver host except the one edge that makes it a driver. **`Rights::BIND` on both capabilities, and neither implies the other.** On the device it is the authority to direct its line — deliberately not implied by `MAP`, for the reason `CONFIGURE` is not: a bus controller hands a function's registers to a driver it will not also let redirect the line, and it can only draw that distinction because the rights are separate. On the port it is the same right `PortBind` checks, because this *is* a port bind and a device route that skipped it would be a way around it. **The line is resolved against the graph, never taken as given** — `intid = 0` means the device's own, and a named one is checked against the lines the graph records for *this* device, so a holder may choose among its own vectors (what a multi-queue controller needs) and cannot name somebody else's. The route's holder is the **calling process**, which is what makes it end when that process does, in the same sweep that ends a departing driver's register windows and DMA leases. **Used, not merely implemented.** AArch64 boot now seeds the root task a third capability — the machine's own PL031 RTC, real, at its own physical address, on the line the device tree names — and the root task maps it, routes its line to a port it made, arms the alarm from ring 3, and parks. A real interrupt wakes it: `irq=34 deliveries=1`. The RTC for the reason D141 and D104 both chose it — it is owned by no driver, so proving the interrupt claim did not require becoming a virtio driver first. **Two witnesses, because the program's own word is not evidence.** The root task checks that the port event's source is the line the bind reported and its signal is the interrupt edge; the kernel's bridge counts deliveries independently. A check resting on the program alone passes on one that skipped the step, which is exactly what the inversion produces. **Two bugs the inversions found.** The skip guard asked `HandleQueryRights` on handle 2 at the *end* of the run, by which time handle 2 is something the program made — so on a port that seeds no device it would have handed a channel endpoint to `DeviceIrqBind`; it is asked before anything is created now. And a host test asserting the route's holder held with the holder replaced by the device, because the test harness gives its process the device's own object id — it uses a second process with an identity of its own. Six inversions fire: device `BIND` dropped (`exit=118`), port `BIND` dropped, the graph's line check removed, the holder changed, the device handle never seeded (`exit=0`, `deliveries=0` — the discriminating one), and a wait on a different port (the root task parks and boot's bounded pump reports it rather than hanging). **Size, per D231**: the root task grows 251,184 -> 257,376 bytes and the AArch64 kernel image 4,782,032 -> 4,794,320 — the ISL decoder, the dispatch arm, and a forty-line PL031 driver in the root task. **What this does not do.** It does not retire `bring_up_device_host` or `driver_host_demo`: those compose a *virtio* driver over a channel, and what the root task drives here is a clock. The mechanism they needed from the kernel now exists; what is left is moving the composition. | The five kernel-side compositions the roadmap's fourth bullet retires — `device_manager_demo`, `driver_host_demo`, `driver_bind_check` on x86-64 and `bring_up_device_host`, `relay_pair` on AArch64 — and the three ports still without a `LoaderSupport` impl. |

Owner for all: mjchandra@gmail.com.
